## 📚 Paper List

- **[Skill-JEPA: Learning Planner-Aligned Action Spaces for World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26825)**  `arXiv:2611.26825`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Tomás Couso Coddou, Christ Devia, Rex G. Liu, Rodrigo Toro Icarte_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models based on Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) learn from pixels to predict the outcome of actions in a latent space. To reach a goal, a planner samples many candidate action sequences, uses the model to predict where each one leads, and executes the best one. However, standard planners draw their candidates from a Gaussian, while the model has only seen the structured, state-dependent actions of its training data. Its predictions for such candidates are unreliable, and planning fails more often as goals move farther away. We propose Skill-JEPA, which learns an action space aligned with the planner. A variational autoencoder conditioned on the current state encodes short action sequences into latent skills with a standard Gaussian prior, and decodes them back into actions. The world model predicts the effect of skills rather than raw actions, and the planner searches over skills starting from that same Gaussian, so its candidates resemble the model's training data. Skill-JEPA adds a single module, trained end-to-end with the world model, and leaves the planner untouched, with no subgoals, hierarchy, or hand-designed skills. Across three JEPA world models and four benchmarks, average success on goals 80 steps away rises from 42% with raw actions to 62%; the gains grow with goal distance and hold across four planners. On AntMaze, a quadruped locomotion task, success on goals two maze cells away rises from 0.4% to 38%. This suggests that part of the difficulty of reaching distant goals lies in the action space, and can be removed without changing the planner.
  </details>

- **[Training Reasoning Models in Simulation to Natively Use Real Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18562)**  `arXiv:2611.18562`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiaming Tang, Zhuoyang Zhang, Junxian Guo, Fangzhou Zhao, Yilong Zhao, Zekai Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $\pi_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $\pi_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.
  </details>

- **[Tripwire: Robot Agents that Program Their Own Interrupts under Human Disturbance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27182)**  `arXiv:2611.27182`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sunli Chen, Yun Zong, Qinhong Zhou, Xiaowen Qiu, Jiaben Chen, Zeyuan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses are turn-based: the agent acts, waits for the result, then reasons. When the action invokes a pretrained robot policy, such as a vision-language-action (VLA) or world action model (WAM), the policy may react continuously to observations, but the agent cannot revisit its decision until the call returns. Meanwhile, a person may step into the robot's path or move the object it is about to grasp. Invoking the agent more often narrows this blind interval, but a reasoning model takes seconds per decision and most control steps need no new one. We propose TRIP, a training-free, event-driven harness in which the agent programs its own interrupts. With each call, the agent writes a tripwire: a lightweight, stateful Python program that runs alongside the policy, can query a compact VLM (for example, to locate a person and measure their distance), and decides whether the call continues or control returns to the agent. When it fires, the harness halts the policy and returns control with the reason; the agent then reassesses, adapts, and continues under a rewritten tripwire. We evaluate TRIP with three VLAs and one WAM on RoboHD, a benchmark of 168 RoboCasa365 scenes in which a simulated person disturbs the robot and any human-robot contact counts as failure. Compared with a turn-based harness using the same agent and tools, TRIP halves the rate of human contact with the strongest policy, raising safety from 61.3% to 81.3%, without sacrificing task success (38.7% vs. 31.0%). Real-robot experiments show that TRIP completes two manipulation tasks under human interference where the turn-based harness and the policy alone largely fail.
  </details>

- **[HumanoidPose: Monocular 3D Pose Estimation for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27181)**  `arXiv:2611.27181`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Jinlin Gan, Mingzhe Li, Junsheng Zhang, Zhibo Zhou, Jiahan Geng, Yuhua Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As humanoid robots increasingly enter real-world environments, recovering their 3D whole-body pose from monocular imagery is becoming increasingly important for external state perception, motion analysis, and robot learning. However, Humanoid Robot Pose Estimation (HRPE) lies at the intersection of conventional Robot Pose Estimation (RPE) and Human Pose Estimation (HPE), while not being fully addressed by either paradigm: RPE primarily focuses on non-humanoid robots, particularly fixed-base manipulators, whereas HPE is built upon human-specific skeletal topologies, body models, and motion priors. Despite recent progress in humanoid 2D keypoint detection and HPE adaptation, humanoid-native 3D pose recovery remains insufficiently studied, and a dedicated benchmark with suitable training data and evaluation protocols is still lacking. To address this gap, we introduce Humanoids, a benchmark for monocular 3D humanoid robot pose estimation, and propose HumanoidPose, a framework for this task. Humanoids provides synthetic 3D supervision across diverse humanoid embodiments, manually annotated real-world 2D data, and real-world RGB sequences paired with robot states for 3D evaluation on a physical Roban humanoid. HumanoidPose combines human-inspired visual perception with robot articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. Experiments on Humanoids and the DHRP-H1 benchmark demonstrate strong performance in articulation recovery and 3D pose estimation, including zero-shot transfer to real-world 2D and 3D evaluation. Ablation studies further show the contribution of robot-native articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. The dataset and code will be publicly released to support future research in the community.
  </details>

- **[RoboPRO: A Perturbation-Resilient and Obstacle-Aware Benchmark for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26971)**  `arXiv:2611.26971`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiyuan Li, Charles Eret, Dingsheng Liu, Xuan Zhao, Joshua Choi, Yangzheng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object manipulation in the real world requires robotic policies to be robust to contextual perturbations and capable of safe planning in cluttered scenes. However, the majority of existing robotics benchmarks contain clean tabletop environments with success rate as the sole metric, leaving two critical deployment questions unanswered: does the policy manipulate safely under real conditions and what does lead to its failure? To this end, we present RoboPRO, a Robotics benchmark for Perturbation-Resilient and Obstacle-aware planning, which unifies various axes of robustness in manipulation. RoboPRO comprises 80 diverse bimanual atomic and compositional tasks across four furnished indoor environments, with a total of 16,000 demonstrations and 5.6K diverse language instructions. The scenes are populated with different numbers of distractors (non-targets), ranging from clean, involving only target objects, to densely populated to impose both visual and operational clutter. Moreover, we propose two safety-aware event metrics to determine unsafe contact between the robot and the environment or distractors. Our evaluation protocol consists of a per-factor analysis of three key perturbations, namely visual, linguistic, and object-level, with different levels of distractor densities. We conduct extensive experiments on RoboPRO using state-of-the-art manipulation policies to analyze the impact of visual and operational clutter, perturbations, and instructional diversity. Our findings indicate clutter can drastically reduce success and increase non-target collision, different perturbations have different effects on the performance, and that training data composition affects overall performance. Our findings highlight the key challenges for manipulation in real-world conditions and motivate future research in this domain. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LoCo-WAM: Test-Time Training as Long Context for World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26853)**  `arXiv:2611.26853`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sheng Cheng, Deqian Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models guide robot actions by predicting what happens next, but typically condition on a short observation history. Extending this history increases computation at every decision and can require a separately trained model. We introduce LoCo-WAM, which learns from the outcomes of its actions through test-time training while keeping the same short history length. It uses newly observed frames to update a low-rank adapter in the video expert, whose adapted predictions guide subsequent actions. The updates accumulate within each episode and are reset between episodes, allowing past observations to influence decisions after they leave the input window. LoCo-WAM thus learns from a longer stream of experience without expert action labels, additional data or meta-training. Experiments on two world-action models show improved task success across four simulation benchmarks. On RoboCasa GR-1, a model with 2.4,s of history matches one trained with 9.6,s, while a model with 4.8,s exceeds it by 4.8 percentage points. Our analysis shows that the updates improve prediction of upcoming frames, affect the action mainly through adaptation of the imagined future, and have an increasing effect on actions as the episode unfolds.
  </details>

- **[Embodied-Harness: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27689)**  `arXiv:2611.27689`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhenxuan Fan, Zhuoya Wang, Yuqian Yuan, Wenqiao Zhang, Juncheng Li, Jun Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language model (VLM)-based agents offer a promising approach to robotic manipulation, yet remain limited by inadequate memory organization, uncertain failure attribution, and limited reuse of execution experience. We propose Embodied-Harness, a dual-agent framework that improves manipulation by refining the execution harness without updating model parameters. The Actor interacts with the environment through a robot-control interface, while the Reflector converts execution evidence into reusable operational guidance. Hierarchical Memory Architecture maintains short-term execution context and long-term experiential knowledge to support execution and reflection. Evidence-based Experience Reflection grounds failure diagnosis in observed interactions, while Recursive Harness Self-Improvement refines operational guidance across successive attempts. Experiments on RoboTwin 2.0, RoboSPA, and RMBench demonstrate the effectiveness of Embodied-Harness in generalization under environmental variations, spatial reasoning, long-horizon planning, and memory-dependent manipulation. Our method achieves success rates of 82.4%, 76.7%, and 94.4% on the three benchmarks, respectively. Real-world evaluation further yields an average success rate of 96.7% across three manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of Embodied-Harness to improve robotic manipulation through experience-driven harness self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[ROSWORLD: When Digital Task Competence Meets Physical Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27467)**  `arXiv:2611.27467`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Caoyuan Ma, Wendi Liu, Zhimin Zhang, Muyao Niu, Yujie Lu, Zengxi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-use agents now solve most desktop tasks through direct software input, but a robot using the same computer must turn GUI knowledge into physical contact with a keyboard and mouse. We introduce ROSWorld, a benchmark that couples a physically simulated robot workstation to a live OSWorld desktop, so that a robot and a desktop agent pursue the same goals under the same official evaluators. The evaluated model receives observations only from the workstation camera views and operates the peripherals through either a single-arm rigid probe or dual-arm dexterous hands. Across five frontier models and 40 tasks in ten applications, models that complete 31-39 tasks on the desktop complete at most 23 with the probe and 28 with the hands. Paired trajectory analysis shows that models adapt their interaction strategies toward keyboard input, yet physical execution frequently fails through missed presses, incorrect targets, and unintended contacts. ROSWorld makes the gap between knowing what to do on a computer and physically doing it measurable.
  </details>

- **[Mechanism Parity for Simulation-Based Validation of Autonomous-Driving Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27447)**  `arXiv:2611.27447`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Danial Safaei, Siddartha Khastgir, Matthew D. Higgins, Jeroen Ploeg, Mohsen Alirezaei, Son Tong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation is central to intelligent-vehicle validation, but visual realism or output agreement between a real scene and its synthetic counterpart does not prove that a perception system used the same evidence in both domains. This paper addresses that mechanism-parity gap for paired real-synthetic autonomous-driving perception. It unifies Decisive-Feature Fidelity, which compares decisive evidence maps in a frozen system under test (SUT), with Concept-Decisive Feature Fidelity, which projects decisive evidence into semantic scene concepts for diagnosis. The framework is a validation diagnostic rather than a standalone safety certificate: it asks whether output-consistent simulation also preserves the mechanism by which the perception model reaches that output. The empirical evidence comprises ten retrained diffusion ControlNet generators evaluated on a deterministic exact-geometry KITTI-Virtual KITTI 2 subset. Generated counterparts use a 1248-by-376-pixel padded inference canvas and are cropped back to the exact KITTI dimensions before evaluation. The final benchmark reports target-image, output-consistency, and mechanism metrics recomputed from a single audited output set, then adds metric-disagreement cases, bootstrap uncertainty, threshold sensitivity, and concept/cache integrity audits. Across the benchmark, output and appearance fidelity are at most weakly and inconsistently rank-correlated with decisive-mechanism distance for all three frozen SUTs, and a constructed positive control shows the estimator detects a known planted mechanism that output agreement misses, supporting decisive-evidence diagnostics for intelligent-vehicle simulation validation. All numerical claims are restricted to the declared exact-geometry benchmark, ten generators, and three frozen SUTs.
  </details>

- **[NavTTT: Adaptive Parametric Memory via Hierarchical Test-Time Training for Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27219)**  `arXiv:2611.27219`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyu Luo, Chengxing Lin, Jiaxin Huang, Jiale Liu, Jingyu Guo, Sirui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) demands that agents accumulate and reason over streaming visual observations throughout long navigation episodes. Existing approaches struggle to maintain long-term history, constrained by the finite context capacity of transformer architectures. To solve the problem, fast-weight test-time training (TTT) provides a mechanism for encoding streaming observations into an evolving parameter state. However, existing TTT does not distinguish informative observations from repetitive visual content, allowing redundant tokens to interfere with the retention of information needed for subsequent decisions. We propose Navigation-Conditioned Test-Time Training (NavTTT), which introduces adaptive memory formation and hierarchical memory integration for vision-and-language navigation. NavTTT leverages global navigation context to prioritize informative visual evidence and integrates language instructions with spatial information to construct spatially grounded memory representations. Episode-local nonlinear fast weights store these representations as Adaptive Parametric Memory (APM), preserving history beyond the visual context for later decisions. To reduce cross-layer coupling in memory formation, NavTTT maintains independent fast-weight memories across multiple decoder depths. This decoupled design prevents errors or biases from spreading across different memory levels. Meanwhile, it enables shared historical information to be independently represented at various levels of abstraction. Experiments demonstrate strong navigation performance on VLN-CE benchmarks and effective instruction following on a real robot.
  </details>

- **[FoRe: A Force-Guided Framework for Autonomous Grasp Recovery in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27011)**  `arXiv:2611.27011`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Peng, Yongkai Liao, Yuxin Cheng, Haoyuan Wang, Jian Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance in grasp-based manipulation, yet reliable recovery from local grasping failures remains challenging. Specifically, in pick-and-place tasks, policies may continue transport after a failed grasp instead of retrying, potentially reflecting overreliance on proprioceptive cues for stage progression and limited retry supervision. Existing recovery approaches typically rely on deployment-time guidance or demonstration augmentation. We introduce Force-guided Recovery (FoRe), a framework for autonomous recovery from local grasping failures. Using force feedback, FoRe partitions demonstrations into approach-grasp and transport-placement stages to train separate policies. To supplement limited retry supervision, FoRe then initializes a dedicated retry policy from the approach-grasp policy and refines it through local reinforcement learning (RL) from post-failure states. To avoid inappropriate stage progression, a controller combines force feedback with grasp-command status at inference to coordinate the stage policies, detect grasp failures, and invoke the retry policy. We also construct LIBERO-SUCTION, a force-augmented suction manipulation benchmark adapted from LIBERO, with complete demonstration trajectories for policy training. FoRe consistently improves task success rates for VLA-Adapter and $\pi_{0.5}$ on LIBERO-SUCTION, LIBERO, and real-world tasks. On LIBERO-SUCTION, average absolute gains are 18.4% and 6.5%, respectively. The corresponding real-world absolute gains are 25.0% and 21.7%. FoRe also remains effective with only one demonstration per task on both simulation benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Coherent Moment Propagation: Epistemic Uncertainty for Pre-trained Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26855)**  `arXiv:2611.26855`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Miguel Saavedra-Ruiz, Anqi Li, Claudia D'Arpino, Alperen Degirmenci, Tucker Hermans, Fabio Ramos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow matching policies underpin the state-of-the-art in robot learning, with impressive results ranging from long-horizon planning to dexterous manipulation. However, they provide no measure of whether their predicted commands can be trusted, particularly in novel situations outside their training set. Epistemic uncertainty reflects uncertainty about the model's weights and quantifies this ignorance, but calculating it at inference time from a flow matching policy is not trivial. Actions are produced by an ODE solver, requiring uncertainty to be propagated through the entire integration process. BayesDiff addresses this problem for diffusion models by propagating the mean and variance of the state through the denoising chain under a last-layer Laplace posterior approximation, but it severely underestimates the epistemic uncertainty. In this paper we: i) prove that, for flow matching and diffusion ODEs, BayesDiff's total variance decays at rate $O(1/T)$ as the number of Euler steps $T$ used by the solver increases; ii) introduce Coherent Moment Propagation (CoMP), two recursions inspired by the extended and unscented Kalman filters that fix this problem by carrying the cross-covariance between state and weights; and iii) widen the last-layer posterior with observation embeddings the policy already computes, improving uncertainty estimates without retraining for CoMP and several test-time estimators alike. We demonstrate these benefits on three manipulation benchmarks with applications in failure detection and demonstration selection for fine-tuning, where CoMP corrects BayesDiff's variance collapse while matching or outperforming several uncertainty estimators.
  </details>

- **[MomentMem: Memorizing Key Moments for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26745)**  `arXiv:2611.26745`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Guangyu Zhao, Yongchang Zhang, Ruizhang Jiang, Zhihong Yu, Yuhao Zhang, Tao Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation often requires remembering visual information that is no longer observable, such as the original location of an object. Existing approaches typically rely on full observation histories, which become redundant over long horizons, or text summaries, which may omit precise visual details. We study visual moment memory, the ability to retain such details from key moments for subsequent manipulation. To support this study, we introduce VMBench, a suite of long-horizon manipulation tasks requiring memory of spatial locations, visual appearances, and their associations. We further propose MomentMem, a hierarchical architecture in which a VLM-based memorizer selects keyframes and an executor predicts actions conditioned on the selected memories. This separation enables us to evaluate memory selection alongside closed-loop task success and to study memorizer generalization independently of the executor. On VMBench, MomentMem achieves 49.9% average success, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 31.7 percentage points. Moreover, we find that the memorizer transfers well to unseen appearances zero-shot. It can also adapt to an unseen task with only a few memory annotations, reaching 43.7% success, close to the 44.3% obtained with full memory supervision. Further experiments show competitive performance on RMBench and improvements in real-robot manipulation. These results highlight the value of retaining sparse visual evidence for memory-dependent manipulation. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Real-time neuromorphic audio and vision perception for autonomous robotic applications: Integration and experimental evaluation on a rover platform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27953)**  `arXiv:2611.27953`  `cs.RO`  
  _Guillem Fustier-Castillo, Ruben Ribes-Serrano, Javier Naranjo-Alcazar, Vicent Ortiz-Castello, Pedro Zuccarello_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neuromorphic sensing and computing offer a promising alternative to conventional deep learning pipelines for mobile robots, where perception must run continuously under tight latency and energy constraints. However, most neuromorphic solutions are still evaluated offline or on isolated benchmarks, and their integration into complete robotic systems remains largely unexplored. In this technical report, a four-wheel rover platform is presented that combines three real-time perception pipelines: a classical audio tagging pipeline based on Pretrained Audio Neural Networks (PANNs) running on an NVIDIA Jetson GPU, a neuromorphic audio pipeline in which a spiking neural network (SNN) is deployed on the SynSense Xylo Audio~2 chip, and a neuromorphic vision pipeline in which events from an event-based camera are processed by the SpikeYOLO spiking detector for traffic cone detection. On the Xylo chip, the whole processing chain---from the microphone signal to the classification of low- and high-pitched sounds---is performed on-chip, while the host only configures the device and reads the output neurons. All models are deployed as containerised services and integrated through ROS~2, and a configurable action dispatcher maps the detected classes to robot behaviours, closing the loop between perception and actuation. The platform is demonstrated in a proof-of-concept scenario in which the robot reacts to sound classes detected by both audio pipelines and detects traffic cones with the event camera, showing the feasibility of integrating neuromorphic hardware into a multimodal robotic platform operating in real time.
  </details>

- **[Instant-LIVO: Efficient LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry with Compact Geometry and a Bounded Single-Reference-Frame Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27633)**  `arXiv:2611.27633`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xinrui Wang, Ziwei Lan, Wenbin He, Mingjing Yang, Yihan Zhang, Yilin Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry (LIVO) is a fundamental technology for autonomous systems; however, deployment on resource-constrained platforms remains challenging due to physical limitations. This paper presents Instant-LIVO, a tightly coupled, high-speed, real-time LIVO system. The system introduces a compact geometric map based on spatial-hashed OctVox and accelerates local-neighborhood retrieval and plane fitting through cache-assisted heuristic staged K-nearest-neighbor searches. It also employs a bounded single-reference-frame visual map that adaptively updates the reference frame according to the tracking status, while using Schmidt-type uncertainty-aware photometric fusion to maintain the correlation between the reference pose and the current state. The system performs multisensor fusion through a sequential process consisting of IMU prediction, LiDAR geometric correction, and visual photometric correction. It was evaluated on 32 public benchmarks and custom datasets. While maintaining competitive accuracy on both X86 and ARM platforms, Instant-LIVO achieves average processing-speed improvements of approximately 2.3x and 2.1x, respectively, and reduces peak memory usage to approximately 1/20 and 1/13, respectively. The results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing LIO and LIVO baselines in both accuracy and computationalthis http URLimplementation is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GenPA: Image Generators as Efficient Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27446)**  `arXiv:2611.27446`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zian Deng, Tianyi Zhang, Zilong Zheng, Ziyu Liu, Tao Zhang, Yao Lyu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretrained visual generation models have shown great promise in robotic manipulation. However, a representative line of work still treats future visual prediction as the central link between visual generative pretraining and action learning, retaining it as a training objective even when future scenes are no longer generated at inference time. We argue that the instruction-following, spatial understanding, and visual imagination capabilities acquired through image generation pretraining can support control without future visual prediction: image generation models themselves can be directly adapted into powerful action learners. Building on this perspective, we propose GenPA, which fine-tunes an image generation backbone with a lightweight 19M-parameter action regression adapter and reuses its native generative objective for perception co-training. The native image-editing interface accommodates different visual targets without task-specific perception heads, enabling comparisons of current-scene perception, future prediction, and their combinations within a common policy architecture. At deployment, GenPA requires only a single backbone forward pass and action regression, without visual generation or iterative action denoising. Without additional large-scale robot-data pretraining, GenPA demonstrates strong control performance and robustness to perturbations across simulation benchmarks (99.10% success on LIBERO and 83.79% on LIBERO-Plus) and real-world manipulation tasks. Under compiled inference, GenPA takes 20.9 ms per action chunk on a single H100 GPU, achieving 1.7-3.4x speedups over strong VLA and WAM baselines. Our latency analysis further highlights architectural advantages of visual generation backbones for efficient robot control. Ablations across visual tasks and their combinations identify current-depth supervision as a simple, effective training recipe.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Future Views: Exploring Future-State World Models for Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27418)**  `arXiv:2611.27418`  `cs.RO`  
  _Kangyi Wu, Pengna Li, Yucheng Wang, Yance Fang, Xihan Ma, Zhizhong Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Navigation(VLN) requires an embodied agent to follow language instructions through unseen 3D environments, where the target spaces often lie beyond the current observation. Navigation world models address this challenge by predicting future views either as pixels or latent representations, providing valuable look-ahead information for action decision. Both paradigms define the future primarily through what the agent may observe, raising a fundamental question: is predicting future views the ultimate form to represent the future for VLN? Cognitive studies show that the human hippocampus prospectively represents both future goals and intervening locations along planned routes. Inspired by this predictive-map view, we organize the navigation-relevant future around goal-directed spatial states and route structure. We instantiate this perspective with two complementary abstractions: a short-horizon waypoint that specifies the next local state to reach, and a mid-horizon corridor that captures the spatial support of the upcoming route. Based on this design, we propose FS-Nav, a future-state world modeling framework that predicts waypoint-corridor states from spatial memory and language instructions, and communicates them to a VLM navigator through top-down and first-person interfaces. Experiments on standard VLN benchmarks demonstrate that FS-Nav achieves state-of-the-art performance. This highlights navigation-relevant future-state modeling as a promising direction for representing the future in VLN.
  </details>

- **[NormFlowSDE: Normalizing Flows for Amortized Bayesian State Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27362)**  `arXiv:2611.27362`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yi Zhang, Lars Mikelsons_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian state estimators, such as Kalman filters, estimate states from noisy observations based on dynamical models. However, obtaining accurate models can be challenging when the system involves effects that are difficult to model faithfully, such as friction, impacts, and deformation in contact-rich manipulation. Uncertainty in system parameters, inputs, and disturbances, together with simplified noise models, can further introduce simulation-to-reality mismatch and degrade estimation accuracy. These limitations motivate learning a distribution over state trajectories conditioned on observations and controls without explicitly specifying the underlying physical models. We introduce NormFlowSDE, an amortized Bayesian state estimator that learns a distribution over state trajectories by combining neural stochastic differential equations with conditional normalizing flows. Its key design choice is to utilize the capability of normalizing flows to learn stochastic behavior of variables, while leveraging the continuous-time dynamics of SDEs to model the evolution of states over time. Once trained, the model draws state paths for new measurement sequences without per-sequence optimization. In addition, we connect NormFlowSDE to stochastic continuous-time dynamics by proving weak convergence of a mean-centered, drift-corrected inference scheme to an Itô diffusion under stated assumptions. Finally, we benchmark NormFlowSDE against direct estimators and learned particle filters based on normalizing flows on partially observed dynamical systems, including simulated systems and real-world robot datasets. The results show that NormFlowSDE attains the lowest estimation error with calibrated uncertainty intervals on both robot datasets and filters four times faster than the fastest particle filter on every dataset.
  </details>

- **[ManipScope: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Instruction Following and Generalization in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27305)**  `arXiv:2611.27305`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiacheng Li, Yaping Li, Congsheng Xu, Qingchen Liu, Haonan Wang, Zhijie Deng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Completing a manipulation task does not establish that a robot follows the requested goal rather than a familiar routine. ManipScope is an evaluation-only diagnostic benchmark for robotic instruction following under changed task requirements. Built on RoboTwin 2.0's simulation resources, it specifies 48 held-out scenes across six task families with explicit source-task links; policies are trained on unmodified RoboTwin 2.0 demonstrations, and no demonstrations of the scenes are provided, so success requires reusing skills under a changed request rather than fitting supplied examples. Beyond task success, ManipScope provides a chain of diagnostics in which each step tests one explanation of a failure. For twelve policy checkpoints, 84.6% of failures in 13,200 episodes on eleven selection scenes displace a distractor, and gripper records show that failed episodes usually grasp a non-target. When the target is reassigned among the candidates on identical initial states, ten checkpoints follow every instruction on 3.9% of 1,000 states, below the 16% of an instruction-blind chooser, while a GPT-6 agent run as positive control reaches 88%. The failure recurs on unmodified training tasks and is not explained by missing manipulation skills, so success in these scenes largely reflects instruction-independent preferences; two checkpoints trained on the same data nevertheless follow color instructions. In a feasibility test, letting an agent select the target and the policy execute partly restores instruction following. Case studies with future-video and internal-intervention probes further show that forecasts and execution can agree on the wrong target and that action changes need not transfer the goal. Success, consistency, and sensitivity must each be checked against the requested goal.
  </details>

- **[PACE-WM: Parallel World Modeling with Action History for Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27173)**  `arXiv:2611.27173`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haishan Liu, Shijie Lian, Bin Yu, Yao Zhang, Changti Wu, Fanping Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  JEPA-style world models support visual planning by predicting future representations conditioned on future actions. However, similar observations can conceal different motion states, making temporal context essential for interpreting future action effects. We propose PACE-WM, a parallel world model that uses executed-action history to contextualize future prediction and planning. Specifically, PACE-WM maintains a recurrent memory of executed actions and uses this memory to condition a causal Transformer. PACE-WM predicts multiple future representations in parallel without recursive predicted-state feedback within each prediction window. We further introduce Reference-Anchored Minimum Progress (RAMP) for action selection during planning. RAMP scores candidate actions by their predicted goal improvement along direct and split paths relative to a shared reference. Across four visual planning tasks, PACE-WM with RAMP achieves an average success rate of $98\%$ and plans approximately $7$-$9\times$ faster than autoregressive LeWM under matched CEM settings. We also construct four controlled benchmarks to evaluate PACE-WM's capabilities in motion disambiguation, goal-relative motion, and goal adaptation. Project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BeyondEgo: Learning to Act Beyond Egocentric Perception via Humanoid Ego-Exo Foresight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27047)**  `arXiv:2611.27047`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhaoshan Yang, Chenglei Li, Haoyu Lei, Yutao Zhang, Yuyu Yang, Zhuo Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinating whole-body motion to perform manipulation tasks in complex environments. Yet egocentric vision-language-action (VLA) policies can miss critical body--scene relationships, and action imitation alone provides no explicit supervision for future scene evolution. We propose BeyondEgo to extend VLA perception in space and time. Spatially, we encode a synchronized exocentric view with frozen DINOv3 and concatenate the resulting tokens with egocentric VLM tokens to condition the action expert. Temporally, we use future observations from both views as privileged supervision for a residual adapter on task-conditioned VLM features. A foresight expert regresses DINOv3 representations of both views 0.6s ahead. At deployment, the foresight expert is discarded and the 4.2M-parameter adapter is retained. We also introduce HELM, to our knowledge, the first closed-loop humanoid loco-manipulation benchmark with synchronized egocentric and exocentric views across five whole-bodythis http URLHELM, BeyondEgo improves success from 48.0% to 92.0% in distribution and from 23.5% to 65.0% on 200 held-out scenes with layout, asset, material, and lighting shifts. Ablations show that dual-view DINOv3 foresight yields the best transfer results.
  </details>

- **[RoboColiseum: Co-evolution of Simulation, Evaluation, and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27042)**  `arXiv:2611.27042`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chenghao Yin, Da Huang, Di Yang, Jichao Wang, Junhui Wu, Nanshu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulation-based evaluation of embodied models requires calibrated interactions, diverse tasks, and explicit execution constraints. We present RoboColiseum, a platform combining system identification, composable physics and rendering, and multidimensional benchmarking. Recorded-command replay characterizes robot dynamics and object interactions, while a shared interface connects rigid and deformable simulation to rendering. Across 22 paired real--simulation robot--task configurations, task scores have a reported Pearson correlation of 0.9686. Eight evaluation tracks span instruction following, spatial reasoning, general manipulation, robustness, dexterous manipulation, precision manipulation, whole-body coordination, and execution across robot embodiments. Budget-aware scoring combines task progress, resource use, and completion rewards. We report 12 selected Season~I policies and all 11 systems on the public Season~II leaderboard, comprising eight Agent systems and three VLA policies. Agent2Act connects language models to robot-control execution; the VLA study uses mixed-task supervised fine-tuning. The results reveal substantial variation across tracks within each evaluation category. RoboColiseum connects simulation development with capability-oriented evaluation and provides a foundation for extending robot interactions and model assessment.
  </details>

- **[RoboDyna: A Benchmark for Evaluating Robotic Manipulation under Physically Grounded Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26969)**  `arXiv:2611.26969`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhiyuan Li, Rui Heng Yang, Xuan Zhao, Amir Rasouli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object manipulation in the dynamic real world requires understanding how the environment functions and evolves, and acting accordingly. Existing benchmarks, however, do not adequately address these needs. Physics-oriented benchmarks evaluate predictive fidelity but not closed-loop action. Dynamic robotic manipulation benchmarks often use simplified, physically implausible object motions, limiting evaluation of reasoning and action under realistic dynamics. Evaluation is also typically synchronized, freezing the environment during inference and making computation time irrelevant to task outcomes. To this end, we propose RoboDyna, a benchmark for evaluating the physical understanding and action capabilities of manipulation policies in dynamic scenes. RoboDyna comprises 20 conceptual dynamic tasks spanning five capability categories, with simple objects and minimal scene clutter, under a two-factor difficulty design, and 10 household tasks that instantiate the same capabilities in visually and semantically richer simulated real-world environments. RoboDyna provides 3,500 expert demonstrations, paired synchronous and asynchronous evaluation protocols, metrics for task success and partial completion, and interactive tools for human baseline evaluation. Evaluating vision-language-action models and a world-action model across 5.4K episodes, we find that all policies achieve success rates below 20%, revealing substantial limitations in dynamic manipulation. The world-action model captures simple physical dynamics but struggles with complex interactions, and its prediction fidelity does not consistently align with task success. Sync-async comparisons show that inference latency flips 5-12% of episode outcomes while changing average success rates by at most 3%. Human baselines substantially outperform all policies under matched conditions. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Mechanistic Analysis of Unified Proximity-Tactile Observation by a Capacitive Fingertip Array Constrained by a Metal Phalanx](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26950)**  `arXiv:2611.26950`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shun Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A capacitive fingertip array on a metal phalanx under thin silicone skin senses centimetre-scale conductive targets by fringing fields and contact force by dielectric compression. Electrode-to-body parasitics exceed the useful proximity increment by one to three orders of magnitude, and crosstalk and time-division-multiplexing (TDM) settling prevent identifying the capacitance-to-digital converter (CDC) reading with the electrode-target capacitance. The array is modelled as a multi-conductor system (sensing electrodes, driven-shield nodes, phalanx ground and an external target). The CDC measurand follows from the Maxwell capacitance matrix, with each inactive termination in the observation operator. Three mechanisms are examined: driven-shield hiding of shield-branch current and gain/phase-mismatch leakage; distinct far- and near-field operators; and TDM settling as a frame-rate limit set by shield load. A single layer used for both proximity and force reads Delta C = Delta C_force + Delta C_dielectric(epsilon_target), so its force map cannot be material-independent. A dual-array stack is analysed: the outer array is grounded as a Faraday screen during contact, and the inner array reads silicone-gap compression. FEM simulations give a monotonic, nonlinear contact response (4.6 fF at 0.05 mm to 370 fF at 0.5 mm on a 1.08 pF base), a nylon-versus-metal difference of at most 0.3 fF at exported-matrix resolution (not mesh-converged), mutual-term cancellation under driven-shield readout, and about 1 fF crosstalk on the coarse mesh. In proximity mode, electrode-shield coupling of 1.47 pF makes 10^{-3}-10^{-4} mismatch a quantitative gate: a 15.9 fF increment at 5 mm equals the leakage at |epsilon_A| ~ 10^{-2}. Force map, mechanical crosstalk and all bench quantities remain unvalidated; the equations and FEM evidence are a baseline for prototype calibration, not measured performance claims.
  </details>

- **[Phoenix: Learning Scene Structure Representations for Generalizable World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24938)**  `arXiv:2611.24938`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yupeng Zheng, Chaoyue Li, Linbo Wang, Xiang Li, Yuhang Zheng, Xiaotong Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World-action models jointly predict future observations and actions, yet reconstruction-oriented visual representations do not explicitly emphasize the spatial relationships and object-state changes needed for manipulation. We introduce Phoenix, which learns representations of scene structure for world-action modeling. Specifically, Phoenix Encoder enriches DINOv3's general visual semantics through dual-teacher distillation of geometric and spatiotemporal semantic knowledge. Next, CS-VAE causally aggregates and compresses Phoenix Encoder features into generative latents while preserving scene information and spatial layout. Finally, Phoenix jointly predicts these latents, complementary appearance latents, and robot actions, linking scene evolution with action generation. To study pretraining data efficiency, we curate approximately 39,000 hours of egocentric and robot manipulation data for representation learning and use the robot demonstration subset for WAM pretraining. Following benchmark-specific post-training, Phoenix achieves state-of-the-art performance across simulation and real-robot benchmarks spanning single-arm, bimanual, and dexterous manipulation. It demonstrates strong robustness to perturbations, achieving success rates of 94.8% on LIBERO-Plus and 72.4% on RoboTwin 2.0 C2R, alongside strong cross-embodiment transfer and real-world manipulation performance on Bench2Dex and RoboDojo-RealWorld, respectively. Pretraining-scale analysis shows larger performance gains from data scaling than a video-VAE-only baseline, indicating stronger scaling benefits and more effective data utilization. Moreover, Phoenix maintains high success rates when adapted with limited downstream demonstrations, demonstrating strong few-shot adaptation. Our code, pretraining data, and models are available on the project page to help the community reproduce our results.
  </details>

- **[LexiconVLA: Learning Reusable Atomic Action Codebooks for Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36774)**  `arXiv:2609.36774`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zeming Wei, Jianheng Ye, Xinshuai Song, Sirui Chen, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models struggle to reuse recurring atomic actions across manipulation tasks. Our diagnostic study reveals that reliable task completion does not imply consistent execution of constituent atomic actions across task contexts. We present LexiconVLA, a retrievable atomic-action lexicon for cross-task reuse. Global and detail codebooks capture shared interaction structure and fine-grained execution variation respectively, preserving both reusable patterns and execution details. Visual-Atomic Action Alignment couples trajectory reconstruction from visual state changes with visual outcome prediction from action codes, grounding the lexicon in motion and its effects. We learn these codebooks with trajectory reconstruction and visual alignment on our AtomAction Dataset of 57,803 segments from 69 tasks. A planner and scene-aware adapter translate new goals into code-conditioned subtasks for a shared policy, without skill-specific experts or deployment-time parameter updates. Across five policy backbones on 26 RLBench tasks, LexiconVLA largely maintains performance on 18 seen tasks while improving success on 8 tasks held out from policy training. With BridgeVLA, unseen-task success rises from 16.67% to 34.17% (+17.50 percentage points), and overall success reaches 71.08%, the highest among methods with reported results. Real-robot experiments demonstrate stepwise execution and failure recovery.
  </details>

- **[PRISM-SLAM: Probabilistic Ray-Grounded Inference for Scale-aware Metric SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19257)**  `arXiv:2605.19257`  `cs.RO`  
  _Eunsoo Im, Gyeonggwan Lee, Seunghwan Hong, Junghun Suh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular SLAM historically suffers from scale ambiguity and tracking failure in dynamic environments. While recent vision foundation models (VFMs) provide remarkable zero-shot depth priors, naively integrating these deterministic predictions ignores predictive uncertainty and frame-to-frame scale inconsistencies. We propose PRISM-SLAM, a real-time framework that rigorously integrates VFM priors into a structured Bayesian factor graph to achieve scale-aware, metric-consistent localization and mapping. Specifically, we introduce a Plücker Ray-Distance Factor to anchor monocular observations in absolute space within a globally consistent metric coordinate system, mathematically resolving scale drift by making the metric scale Fisher-identifiable. To handle environmental dynamics, we derive an epistemic uncertainty proxy from temporal depth consistency and formulate a Dynamic Scene Uncertainty Gating (DSUG) mechanism. This soft-gating approach probabilistically down-weights dynamic distractors without incurring the heavy computational overhead associated with traditional semantic segmentation masks. By employing a multi-process architecture that asynchronously processes VFM inference and geometric tracking, PRISM-SLAM provides verified metric output at 30 FPS using solely RGB input, bridging the gap between foundation models and real-world robotic applications. Evaluated on the TUM RGB-D and 7-Scenes benchmarks, PRISM-SLAM achieves a metric $SE(3)$ Absolute Trajectory Error (ATE) nearly identical to its oracle-aligned $Sim(3)$ error. This demonstrates that our system can produce deployment-ready metric trajectories by delivering robust metric SLAM solutions without any post-hoc scale correction. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Communication-constrained multiagent risk estimation: a simulation benchmark for adult sore throat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27468)**  `arXiv:2611.27468`  `cs.MA`  
  _Anas H. Alzahrani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distributed predictive agents may need to approach a shared estimate under limited, unreliable communication. We evaluated classical symmetric pairwise gossip among five agents initialized from frozen calibrated predictions in a synthetic adult sore-throat benchmark. Ninety runs across three fixed generator families supplied 270,000 underlying test encounters, each observed through four missingness views. Complete, ring and star graphs were compared at eight interaction budgets and transaction failure probabilities of 0, 0.2 and 0.5. The coordination extension was designed after inspection of the predictive benchmark and is exploratory. Outcomes were disagreement around the conserved arithmetic mean and its exact decomposition of mean-agent Brier error. In the base view at eight attempted pairs and 0.2 failure, disagreement variance was 0.000109 for the complete graph, 0.000134 for the ring and 0.000175 for the star. The paired complete-minus-ring difference was -0.000024 (95% Monte Carlo interval -0.000030 to -0.000019). The centralized mean had base Brier 0.069781, compared with 0.069368 for Kumo alone. Agreement therefore does not establish superiority to the strongest individual predictor. Code, fixed probability inputs and results support replay without model refitting. The study quantifies finite-budget coordination under specified simulation assumptions; it proposes no new gossip algorithm and establishes no clinical accuracy or utility.
  </details>

- **[Co-Design Gym: A Unified Benchmark for Embodiment-Policy Co-optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02366)**  `arXiv:2610.02366`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Aviraj Newatia, Yordan Tsvetkov, Leonard Pleiss, Andrew Spielberg, Rika Antonova_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Finding an optimal behaviour policy within a given environment is a widely studied problem in domains as diverse as games, robotics, energy infrastructure, communication networks, and multi-agent systems. Numerous benchmarks have been developed to support such research, but the vast majority assume that the agent's embodiment (design) is fixed, focusing instead on policy learning alone. Lifting this assumption gives rise to a broader class of problems in which optimizing embodiment and policy separately is highly suboptimal. An agent's embodiment strongly shapes which control policies can be discovered, while the optimal embodiment is in turn defined by the policies it admits. To help the research community study this class of problems explicitly and systematically, we introduce Co-Design Gym - a suite of benchmark environments for jointly optimizing embodiment and policy. Our environments span domains such as robotic manipulation and locomotion, multi-robot cooperation, deformable and soft dynamics, video games, electricity grids, wireless networks, F1 racing, multi-agent warehouses, and optimal control, offering 20 environment families (domains), with over 85 distinct co-design presets in total. We further contribute a systematic evaluation of representative co-design algorithms, characterizing the current state of the art. Together, these contributions lay the groundwork for cumulative, comparable progress in co-design.
  </details>

- **[SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26296)**  `arXiv:2611.26296`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Maximilian Seeth, Chengzhi Martin Hu, Marie Kempkes, Paul Röttger, Leo Schwinn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducing Skill$.$png: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. We show that Skill$.$png significantly outperforms state-of-the-art textual skills optimized using frontier LLMs in 9 of 16 settings (across 4 VLMs and 4 benchmarks) while requiring only 64 to 256 image tokens instead of roughly 900 to 2000 text tokens at inference time. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. Skill$.$png thus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[A Broader Look at Model Merging: Rethinking Implicit Regularization Induced by Task Arithmetic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07990)**  `arXiv:2610.07990`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Sin-Han Yang, Shih-Cheng Huang, Chieh-Yen Lin, Yun-Nung Chen, Shao-Hua Sun, Hung-yi Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model merging aims to build a multi-task model cheaply by combining the weights of individual task-specific models. To perform well across multiple tasks, most existing merging methods use an additional dataset to find the coefficients for the best linear combination of task-specific weight updates. However, we identify an implicit regularization in this standard practice: searching over coefficients restricts the candidate models to a subspace spanned by task-specific weight updates. In this work, we investigate whether this regularization is actually useful. Surprisingly, empirical results show that in existing benchmarks, optimizing merged-model weights without this regularization significantly boosts the performance of common merging methods across multiple architectures and domains, even in an extremely data-limited scenario where only one instance is available per class. We further investigate strategies for using the additional dataset in different settings and discuss their practical use. We show that generalization is a key factor when choosing the regularization strength. Overall, this work calls for revisiting the existing model-merging pipeline, motivating a broader exploration of the weight space and a reconsideration of the implicit regularization induced by task arithmetic.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Math, Code, and Logic: Do Reasoning Models Transfer to Scientific Argumentation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27722)**  `arXiv:2611.27722`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Buse Sibel Korkmaz, Antonio del Rio Chanona_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discourse depends on argumentative reasoning: identifying claims, evaluating evidence, and constructing coherent responses. While recent advances in reasoning-capable language models have demonstrated strong performance on mathematical, coding, and logical benchmarks, their ability to engage in scientific argumentation remains unclear. We present the first systematic evaluation of language models across seven tasks spanning argument mining, rebuttal generation, and discourse-level reasoning using research papers, peer reviews, and grant proposals. Our study reveals that even frontier models with strong general reasoning skills struggle with domain-specific argumentative tasks, highlighting a fundamental capability gap. To address this, we introduce a training framework that explicitly scaffolds the argumentative reasoning process in language models, substantially improving their competence in scientific discourse. The resulting compact models approach or exceed the performance of much larger proprietary systems and generalize to unseen conversational settings, demonstrating reasoning transfer beyond task-specific supervision. These findings underscore that effective scientific argumentation is not an emergent property of scale, but requires explicit reasoning-aware training, and they point toward practical pathways for building AI systems that can contribute meaningfully to scientific discourse.
  </details>

- **[Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation of RL Experts: Toward Continual Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27376)**  `arXiv:2611.27376`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhen Zhang, Changyi Yang, Xuehai He, Chengzhi Liu, Yutong Yin, Ziyue Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes a continual process. Weight merging and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into a soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. A single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teacher's gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In a multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40x that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward continual post-training on a fixed backbone.
  </details>

- **[The Vulnerability Window: Decision Instability in Quantized Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27155)**  `arXiv:2611.27155`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Omkar Shewale, Deepak Kumar, Divakar Kumar Yadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models and LLM judges are routinely quantized to 4 bits so that scoring many candidates stays affordable, and the usual check is that benchmark accuracy barely moves. That check is close to blind. Across eight reward models from two families (0.6B-8B), three generative judges and four 4-bit quantizers, quantization rewrites 1.1-12.3% of pairwise preference decisions on RewardBench while aggregate accuracy moves by as little as 0.03 points (p = 1.00), because the errors it fixes and introduces cancel. Flips concentrate where the full-precision margin is small relative to the quantization noise scale sigma: a Gaussian approximation Phi(-|m|/sigma) describes the aggregate rate, over-predicting it in all 30 scalar settings and failing for generative judges, and occupancy of the resulting vulnerability window falls from 25% to 7% with model size, which is what makes small judges fragile. The churn stays within noise under benign best-of-16 and becomes clear under adversarial pressure for some quantizers (146:54 harmful:helpful flips on one family, reversing sign on another). Downstream it changes what ships: across six (model, quantizer) pairs from two families, 14.6-65.7% of problems get a different best-of-64 selection while only 2.6-9.6% get a different final answer, with selections changing 5-8x more often than answers in every pair, and the pair the benchmark calls identical (0.03 points, p = 1.00) still changes 34.5% of them. Benchmark churn ranks that downstream change (rho = 0.89); benchmark accuracy does not order it reliably. Held-out audits then locate the useful diagnostic in the margin rather than the Gaussian: every margin-based predictor orders flips identically (AUC 0.927), and on the level a directly measured churn rate wins in-distribution. As a routing signal it is matched by plain top-k. The contribution is the characterization, not the model.
  </details>

- **[AhaBench: Do Agents Turn Experience into Reusable Insights? A Long-Horizon Benchmark for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05435)**  `arXiv:2609.05435`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zerui Cheng, Jiawei Xu, Huacan Chai, Jiayang Sun, Pramod Viswanath, Maxm Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Solving a task, even from a worked example, does not make an agent any better at the next one. As agents move from single tasks to long collaborations with people, turning experience into reusable insight becomes the capability that separates a tool from a collaborator that improves over time. AhaBench is a benchmark for this kind of continual learning. It simulates three patterns of long human-AI collaboration and holds a later test fixed while varying the experience before it: in Aha-Puzzle, the agent questions a partner who knows the answer to a new mystery after solving earlier ones; in Aha-Euler, a person teaches a computation and the agent applies it to new inputs; in Aha-Vending, the agent runs a delegated business for a simulated year. The learning mechanism is left open, so memories, skill libraries, and weight updates can be compared under common rules against a baseline of fixed-weight agents that see their experience in context. Our results show that today's frontier agents are not yet continual learners. Earlier Aha-Puzzle solutions help some models, most clearly Qwen 3.6 Plus, which gains +12.57 points over attempts on the same stories without earlier episodes, yet every model scores far below what it reaches with hints. On single-step Aha-Euler tasks, the same model solves 94.0% of requested held-out inputs when taught with an explanation and code but 6.0% when given only the question and answer, counting unanswered attempts as incorrect. In incident-free Aha-Vending operation, Doubao Seed 2.0 Pro ends the year at +495 USD, yet its profit averaged over the year is -10 USD because it recovers late. Exploring without help, reconstructing a method, and sustaining productive operation remain open problems, and AhaBench provides the paired tests a learning method must pass to solve them.
  </details>

- **[Towards Understanding On-Policy Distillation through the Lens of Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11829)**  `arXiv:2608.11829`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinmu Ge, Zizhuo Zhang, Yu Huang, Jianing Zhu, Lin Yuan, Wanli Gu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as a promising post-training technique for enhancing LLM reasoning. Under the reverse KL objective, the idealized optimum of OPD aligns the student distribution with that of the teacher. When the teacher consistently outperforms the student, this naturally suggests that OPD should yield broad improvements over the pre-OPD student. However, do such improvements extend across the entire range of test-time sampling budgets? In this work, we revisit this expectation through the lens of test-time scaling by varying the sampling budget $K$ and evaluating performance with pass@$K$. Across multiple settings, we observe two distinct patterns: OPD can improve pass@$K$ at both small and large sampling budgets, but it can also improve small-budget performance while reducing large-budget pass@$K$. We show one condition that guarantees such a reversal and an idealized reverse KL counterexample where it occurs even when the teacher has higher accuracy on every problem. To choose between two candidate teachers at a target sampling budget, we propose the \textit{Teacher Advantage Score at $K$} (TAS@$K$), which can be computed before OPD training to predict which teacher will lead to a larger improvement in pass@$K$. Across three domains and thirteen benchmarks, the ordering predicted by TAS@$K$ agrees with the observed pass@$K$ improvements of the resulting OPD models in 83.6\% of experiments, providing a useful signal for teacher selection at the target pass@$K$.
  </details>

- **[Self-Distilled Reasoner: On-Policy Self-Distillation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18734)**  `arXiv:2601.18734`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Siyan Zhao, Zhihui Xie, Mengchen Liu, Jing Huang, Guan Pang, Feiyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by compressing the knowledge of a teacher LLM to train smaller LLMs. On-policy distillation advances this approach by having the student sample its own trajectories while a teacher LLM provides dense token-level supervision, addressing the distribution mismatch between training and inference in off-policy distillation methods. However, on-policy distillation typically requires a separate, often larger, teacher LLM and does not explicitly leverage ground-truth solutions available in reasoning datasets. Inspired by the intuition that a sufficiently capable LLM can rationalize external privileged reasoning traces and teach its weaker self, we introduce On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), a learning algorithm where a single LLM acts as both teacher and student with different contexts. The teacher policy conditions on privileged information (e.g., verified reasoning traces) while the student policy sees only the question; training minimizes the per-token divergence between these distributions over the student's own rollouts. We demonstrate the efficacy of our method on multiple mathematical reasoning benchmarks, achieving superior token efficiency compared to reinforcement learning methods and better performance over off-policy distillation methods. Code repo:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Trust Region Q Adjoint Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27079)**  `arXiv:2605.27079`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Yonghoon Dong, Kyungmin Lee, Changyeon Kim, Jaehyuk Kim, Jinwoo Shin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy reinforcement learning of pretrained flow policies remains challenging due to the instability of optimization arising from the multi-step sampling process. Recently, Q-learning with Adjoint Matching (QAM) addressed this by recasting policy improvement as a stochastic optimal control (SOC) problem guided by a learned critic. However, QAM inherits a fundamental fragility of critic-guided improvement, since small critic errors can be exponentially amplified and often lead to performance collapse. This paper introduces Trust Region Q Adjoint Matching (TRQAM), a stable off-policy fine-tuning algorithm that adaptively controls the path-space KL between the fine-tuned and pretrained policies through projected dual descent. Specifically, we adapt a trust-region parameter $\lambda$ inside the sampling process of the flow policy and prove that $\lambda$ exactly weights the path-space KL in the SOC objective. As a result, our method can tightly control the deviation from the pretrained policy within a prescribed KL budget, achieving stable off-policy RL. Through experiments on 50 OGBench tasks, TRQAM consistently outperforms prior methods in both offline RL and offline-to-online RL. In particular, TRQAM achieves an overall success rate of 68% in offline RL, substantially improving over the strongest baseline at 46%.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Assimilate: Amortized Control for Autoregressive Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06637)**  `arXiv:2510.06637`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Prakhar Srivastava, Farrin Marouf Sofian, Francesco Immorlano, Kushagra Pandey, Stephan Mandt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive diffusion models (ARDMs) offer expressive priors for forecasting chaotic dynamics. However, maintaining consistency with sparse observations over long rollouts can require substantial test-time computation. We introduce Control-Augmented Data Assimilation (CADA), which trains a lightweight controller on top of a frozen ARDM to steer predictions toward future observations. Using a KL-regularized stochastic optimal control objective, the controller learns from short autoregressive rollouts to anticipate observations while staying close to the pretrained dynamics. At inference, it injects corrections at each denoising step without per-forecast optimization. Across two chaotic PDE benchmarks, CADA improves long-horizon accuracy, stability, and physical fidelity under sparse observations. On compact ERA5, it outperforms diffusion-based baselines in most regimes with over $10\times$ faster inference than conditional diffusion baselines. Applied to frozen GenCast, CADA also reduces temperature errors across 12--48-hour forecasts with spatially sparse observations. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SUAF: Spectral Universal Advantage Features for Zero-Shot Policy Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27919)**  `arXiv:2611.27919`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Huaze Tang, Runqing Miao, Tianren Peng, Lam Nguyen, Chao Wang, Jairong Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward-independent representations enable reinforcement learning agents to reuse knowledge across tasks, but limited representation capacity raises three fundamental questions: what statistical dependence should these representations capture, which components of that dependence should be retained for policy improvement, and how does discarding the remaining components affect decision-making? We address these questions through canonical dependence analysis. For a reference policy, we construct an action-centered, positive-lag canonical dependence matrix (CDM) that isolates the additional predictive contribution of the current action beyond the current state. Its singular value decomposition yields Spectral Universal Advantage Features (SUAF), paired features that recover delayed advantage through linear reward projections. We show that the spectrum quantifies mutual information between current action and future state-action visitation conditioned on current state and determines reward specific approximation errors. Retaining the leading modes gives optimal rank-constrained approximations under both isotropic average-case and reward-uniform minimax criteria. Separating the immediate reward contrast also avoids the spectral floor arising from direct compression of the full advantage operator. To learn SUAF from reward-free data, we develop an action-centered Soft-HGR objective with bidirectional temporal-difference estimation. We further connect spectral approximation errors to sufficient conditions for policy improvement. Finite-state experiments validate the spectral predictions and feature-learning method, while zero-shot evaluations on discrete tasks and continuous-control benchmarks demonstrate the utility of the learned representations across rewards.
  </details>

- **[On the Limits of Observability for Evaluating Coverage in Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27543)**  `arXiv:2611.27543`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziluowen Luo, Jun Yin, Songyang Liu, Ji Qi, Chuang Hu, Kyrie You Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to remove the influence of any requested subset of training data while preserving utility on the remaining data. Empirical evaluations measure predictive accuracy and infer training-set membership (MIAs) from model outputs or intermediate activations. However, by focusing on selected forget sets, these evaluations can overlook Coverage, the range of requests a method can fulfill. To assess this coverage, we first conduct a stress test of observability with 502 retrained models, finding that hidden embeddings reveal known deletions missed in predictions. To characterize capacity limits on coverage, we show that distinguishing all $n$ singleton deletions with linear scores can require a shared representation of rank $\Omega(\log n)$. We further extend the bound to arbitrary subsets and show when partial deletion can appear complete. Building on this analysis, we propose CEO, a framework for evaluating unlearning coverage under limited observability. Its compositional color-shape benchmark makes forgetting and retained utility measurable from the full output distribution and provides a leave-one-out retraining oracle for each target. From a fixed number of oracle observables, we estimate empirical target coverage, the fraction of targets reliably flagged when deleted and rarely flagged under any other deletions. To recover missed deletions, CEO further expands each observable representation into high-dimensional activation space with the frozen encoder of a SAE trained on the original model. Extensive experiments of 20 baselines shows that even on this simple synthetic task, only retraining from scratch covers all deletion targets.
  </details>

- **[Learning When to Ask: Advisor-Aware Post-Training for SLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27443)**  `arXiv:2611.27443`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for cheap, high-throughput agentic deployment, but they remain weak at multi-step reasoning and tool use that long-horizon tasks demand. We study how to post-train an SLM to seek advice from a stronger model and act on that advice itself, following an advisor-executor pattern. Out of the box, this pattern fails across open- and closed-source small-model families: wiring an SLM to an advisor is a harness-design matter that is not enough on its own. A post-training recipe is needed to teach the model when to ask, in a cost-aware fashion, and how to follow advice across steps. Our method, ADAPT (ADvisor-Aware Post-Training), is a multi-stage recipe that instills this behavior, learning entirely from the policy's own rollouts supervised by the environment's outcome, with no learned reward model or human annotation. Across three domains, compared to an untrained harness baseline, ADAPT improves success rate in embodied text reasoning (TextWorld) from 24.5% to 77.3%, code generation (LiveCodeBench) from 21.8% to 56.0%, and deep web search (GAIA) from 17.5% to 49.3%, while querying the advisor sparingly (~1.0-2.1 calls per task). On the two long-horizon domains it closes 70-85% of the gap to an oracle that perfectly times when to ask for advice, and on code generation it exceeds that oracle. The gains persist when the advisor is replaced by one unseen in training, showing that the learned help-seeking policy is robust to a changing advisor rather than tied to the one it was trained with.
  </details>

- **[THRIFTY: Cost-Constrained Reasoning-Data Synthesis for Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27436)**  `arXiv:2611.27436`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aasheesh Singh, Vishal Vaddina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small language models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for efficient, specialized deployment, and are typically trained on reasoning trajectories distilled from a larger teacher LLM. Generating this synthetic data is a major cost of the pipeline, especially when the teacher is a frontier model. Existing collection methods, however, are largely cost-unaware, or at best spread a fixed sampling budget uniformly across queries. Such allocation ignores the wide variation in problem difficulty and the strategic over- or under-sampling it invites. Thus, we propose THRIFTY, a Thompson-sampling bandit controller that spends a fixed generation-token budget to collect diverse correct reasoning trajectories, casting the task as a bandits-with-knapsacks problem with a novel diversity-aware objective. It tracks each problem's success probability with an online Beta posterior and spends each token where it expects the most new distinct correct solutions.
