Because repeated solutions add progressively less value, THRIFTY adaptively stops resampling saturated problems, concentrates budget on under-covered ones, and retires the unsolvable at the budget's opportunity cost. On math (AIME, MATH-500) and code (LiveCodeBench) benchmarks, THRIFTY gathers up to 22% more diverse correct trajectories per generation token than both the standard uniform-sampling recipe and the strongest adaptive baseline, with the largest gains in the scarce-budget and hard-problem regimes.
  </details>

- **[Hypergraph Structural Entropy-Enhanced Hierarchy-Aware Semi-Supervised Graph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27043)**  `arXiv:2611.27043`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Guangjie Zeng, Hao Peng, Angsheng Li, Li Sun, Chunyang Liu, Yicheng Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semi-supervised graph classification aims to learn graph-level representations by leveraging a large number of unlabeled graphs alongside a small set of labeled ones, effectively mitigating the issue of label scarcity. Despite significant progress by various semi-supervised learning strategies, complex relationships among graph vertices beyond pairwise edges, especially hierarchical structures, remain underexplored by existing methods. In this work, we propose a Hypergraph Structural Entropy-Enhanced hierarchy-aware framework, namely HypSEE, for semi-supervised graph classification. Specifically, we first construct walk-induced and embedding-derived hypergraphs to capture hierarchy-aware local and long-range higher-order relationships within graphs. Then, we devise a hierarchical hypergraph pooling approach guided by hypergraph structural entropy (HSE) to facilitate hierarchical representation learning in hypergraphs. Lastly, we employ an anchor graph-based consistency strategy to mutually align the representations from these two hypergraph views, enhancing the learning efficacy on unlabeled data. Extensive experiments on 8 benchmark datasets demonstrate the state-of-the-art performance of our approach.
  </details>

- **[Ruby: State-of-the-Art Potency Prediction across Discovery Regimes Using Learned Structural Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27015)**  `arXiv:2611.27015`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Michael Atkin, Dominique Beaini, Jules Berman, Rebecca Boiarsky, Jamin Chen, Alejandro Dobles, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small-molecule drug discovery proceeds through repeated design-make-test-analyze cycles. The goal is often to maximize potency while keeping other properties within the ranges required for a viable therapeutic candidate. Compound prioritization places different demands on computational methods as drug discovery progresses from hit identification through hit-to-lead and lead optimization, with changing computational budgets and increasing amounts of on-target experimental data. We introduce Ruby, a Robust Universal Binding affinitY predictor that uses intermediate representations from the structural foundation model Pearl without requiring an explicit binding pose for every compound. Ruby is trained on proprietary data plus GenesisPotency-2026, an audited public-source potency training dataset released for academic and commercial use under a CC BY-SA 4.0 license with row-level provenance and audit annotations. Ruby achieves the highest aggregate early enrichment across seven public hit-identification targets among evaluated methods, with adjustable inference effort trading off enrichment and computational cost over more than two orders of magnitude. Ruby achieves the highest average correlations across four public benchmarks: OpenFE, CASP16, FEP+ 4, and OpenBind. Ruby significantly outperforms other methods in zero-shot prediction across eight biochemical and cellular assays from five ongoing Genesis-Incyte programs, widening its performance advantage relative to the public benchmarks. Ruby continues to improve more than other methods when all are given the opportunity to recalibrate using earlier assay measurements. Together, these contributions provide an annotated training resource for the field and a state-of-the-art potency predictor for broad screening, prioritization within related chemical series, and assay-specific prediction as experimental measurements accumulate.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarks Are Surprisingly Compaction-Friendly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31778)**  `arXiv:2610.31778`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jeanne Salle, Hardik Bhatnagar, Matthias Bethge, Sahar Abdelnabi, Maksym Andriushchenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long horizon agentic tasks are now pushing LLM agents past their context window. To continue the task, the agent scaffold has to free up the context window, a process known as context compaction. Compaction is generally lossy by design: agents can silently lose task constraints, instructions, or their reasoning behind earlier decisions. Although compaction is a standard component of coding agent scaffolds, it has not been studied systematically with frontier models on realistic, long horizon agentic tasks. Previous work evaluate smaller open weight models on toy or synthetic trajectories. We fix the agent scaffold (OpenCode), implement five compaction strategies inside it, and evaluate three frontier models on three long horizon benchmarks (PaperBench, ProgramBench, PostTrainBench). For the two 1M context models, the compaction threshold does not measurably affect the task performance, whereas GPT-5.3-Codex, the model with the smallest context window, performs worse at lower thresholds on PaperBench and, less clearly, on PostTrainBench. No compaction method outperforms default self-summarization, and methods that discard more content cost some models up to 20 points while leaving other models the same. Compaction can, however, silently remove task constraints: on ProgramBench, the first compaction summary already drops about one third of the task's anticheating rules on average, and this rises monotonically with the number of compactions. On real user-agent sessions from SWE-chat, instructions about how the agent should work and what it should not do are dropped around 6 times more than specifications of what to build. We argue that current benchmarks are compaction-friendly: i.e., the objective is fixed and the agent's progress stays on disk, so most of what compaction discards can be recovered, except constraints that don't exist anywhere else.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Grouping Conformal Prediction for Effective Subgroup Reliability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11957)**  `arXiv:2610.11957`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Meihui Zhong, Wenxin Tai, Ting Zhong, Fan Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conformal prediction offers a distribution-free coverage guarantee, making it especially attractive for clinical applications. Standard conformal prediction, however, provides such guarantees only at the population level, and its prediction sets can exhibit coverage disparities across clinically important subgroups. A natural remedy is to calibrate within predefined groups. However, this can require access to sensitive subgroup attributes and is prone to a worst-group bottleneck: protecting the most difficult subgroup can inflate prediction sets for all, increasing cognitive burden on decision makers. To this end, we propose Stochastic Grouping Conformal Prediction (SGCP), a conformal framework for subgroup-reliable uncertainty quantification. It learns a stochastic grouping map that allows each sample to draw calibration information from others with similar calibration behavior, yielding a local score law that boosts reliability across subpopulations. We prove that SGCP retains the standard coverage guarantee. Experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show that it consistently reduces subgroup coverage gaps while achieving smaller or comparable prediction set sizes relative to existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[ThunderSync: Lossless Acceleration of Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05935)**  `arXiv:2610.05935`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Seil Kang, Hangoo Kang, Tarun Suresh, Youngeun Kim, Shreyas Pimpalgaonkar, Seong Jae Hwang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are moving beyond generating answers to pursuing long-horizon goals in interactive environments. Post-training these agents requires long, heterogeneous trajectories, and synchronous systems leave learner engines idle until rollout and verification finish. To squeeze out these pipeline bubbles, asynchronous training overlaps rollout and learning across updates, but comes at the cost of policy staleness. We introduce ThunderSync, which starts gradient computation as soon as all required inputs are fixed, without policy staleness. For group relative policy optimization (GRPO), ThunderSync computes each trajectory's score gradient as soon as the reward for that trajectory arrives, without waiting for the group. For on-policy distillation (OPD), it computes gradients for each completed agentic turn's teacher-scored actions while tool calls run in the sandbox. We prove that gradient streaming produces the same GRPO and OPD updates as batch-synchronous training, without changing either objective. On SWE-bench Verified and Terminal Bench 4.0, we train models to the same performance up to $1.9 \times$ faster than synchronous training. With zero policy staleness, ThunderSync also outperforms asynchronous training at a fixed budget by up to $2.47$ percentage points.
  </details>

- **[OmniHarness: Harnessing Generalizable Visual Generation via Symbolic Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16057)**  `arXiv:2609.16057`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xu Xu, Jinxiu Liu, Zhangbo Qiao, Cheng Song, Jiaxing Lu, Xiangyu Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unified multimodal large language models and multi-agent systems have advanced visual generation. However, several limitations remain. (1) Experience acquisition is typically reactive, occurring only in response to downstream tasks. (2) Accumulated knowledge varies in relevance and reliability, making efficient and effective reuse challenging. To address these limitations, we introduce OmniHarness, a framework for generalizable visual generation via symbolic policy learning. Before downstream objectives are specified, self-directed inquiry autonomously generates and executes practice tasks, with selection guided by capability novelty and the current competence frontier. Verified workflows are abstracted into symbolic policies that encode experience for task families. Symbolic retrieval searches workflow metadata in stages rather than full parameterized workflows, using reliability tiers to prioritize reliable knowledge. Execution feedback updates these policies after each task, guiding subsequent retrieval and reuse while model parameters remain fixed. Experiments across six benchmarks demonstrate strong performance and generalizable visual generation capabilities. On ComfyBench's Creative tasks, OmniHarness achieves a 95.0% resolve rate, exceeding the strongest baseline by 27.5 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[Chain of Operators: An Inference-Time Harness for In-Context Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12318)**  `arXiv:2606.12318`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Minghui Yang, Chenghan Wu, Ling Guo, Liu Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While scientific foundation models show immense promise in accelerating physical simulations and numerical forecasting, they remain notoriously brittle when encountering out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. Adapting these generalist models to complex OOD tasks typically requires expensive parameter fine-tuning. In linguistic AI, this bottleneck is bypassed using "harnesses", which serve as external scaffolding such as reasoning chains and tool use to adapt frozen weights to complex tasks, yet designing an equivalent harness for physical domains remains an open frontier. To bridge this gap, we introduce Chain of Operators (CHOP), a framework that guides a frozen foundation model through complex OOD tasks without updating a single weight. By exploiting the in-context learning capability of In-Context Operator Networks (ICON), CHOP systematically decomposes unfamiliar problems into a sequence of explicit, closed-form mathematical operations and multiple model calls, translating OOD queries back into the model's learned operating regime. Across diverse benchmarks, including canonical PDE problems and real-world air-quality forecasting, CHOP consistently and substantially reduces inference errors compared to direct model evaluation. Crucially, these modular operator chains remain fully interpretable and can generalize across entirely distinct families of physical equations. Ultimately, this work demonstrates how frozen scientific models can adapt through programmable inference, establishing a modular, potential paradigm for agentic scientific computing.
  </details>

- **[The Projector Forgets Too: Joint Merging for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14016)**  `arXiv:2604.14016`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zijian Gao, Wangwang Jia, Xingxing Zhang, Pengfei Qian, Tao Sun, Bo Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) is essential for sequential task adaptation of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) but is severely restricted by catastrophic forgetting. While existing literature focuses on the reasoning language backbone, in this work, we find that forgetting can arise from changes in both the cross-modal projector and the language model's LoRA adapters. In component restoration experiments, restoring an earlier task's LoRA adapter while retaining the final-task projector can leave an accuracy gap relative to restoring both components. To address these two sources of forgetting, we present \textbf{JoMe} (\textbf{Jo}int \textbf{Me}rging), which combines \textbf{C}ross-modal \textbf{P}rojection \textbf{M}erging (\textbf{CPM}) and \textbf{L}ow-rank \textbf{P}arameter \textbf{M}erging (\textbf{LPM}). Specifically, CPM recovers perceptual alignment by adaptively merging cross-modal visual representations via visual-prototype guidance during inference. Simultaneously, LPM recursively merges task-specific LoRA updates to mitigate interference across tasks. By leveraging recursive least squares, LPM yields a closed-form solution to the layer-wise least-squares objective on fixed inputs. Notably, JoMe operates as a training-free paradigm that achieves knowledge merging via efficient CPU-based algebraic operations, eliminating additional gradient-based optimization beyond initial tuning. Our extensive evaluations confirm the superior performance and robustness of JoMe across multiple MLLMs and benchmarks. Specifically, on the UCIT benchmark, JoMe achieves significant leads of up to 5.37\% and 1.72\% in final average accuracy over state-of-the-art methods across two different MLLMs, respectively. \footnote{Our code and data are included in the supplementary material for examination.}
  </details>

- **[EMA Policy Gradient: Taming Reinforcement Learning for LLMs with EMA Anchor and Top-k KL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04417)**  `arXiv:2602.04417`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lunjun Zhang, Jimmy Ba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) has enabled Large Language Models (LLMs) to acquire increasingly complex reasoning and agentic behaviors. In this work, we propose two simple techniques to improve policy gradient algorithms for LLMs. First, we replace the fixed anchor policy during RL with an Exponential Moving Average (EMA), similar to a target network in deep Q-learning. Second, we introduce Top-$k$ KL estimator, which allows for flexible interpolation between exact KL and sampled KL. We derive the stability conditions for using EMA anchor; moreover, we show that our Top-$k$ KL estimator yields both unbiased KL values and unbiased gradients at any $k$, while bringing the benefits of exact KL. When combined with GRPO, the two techniques (EMA-PG) lead to a significant performance boost. On math reasoning, it allows R1-distilled Qwen-1.5B to reach 53.9% on OlympiadBench compared to 50.8% by GRPO. On agentic RL domains, with Qwen-3B base, EMA-PG improves GRPO by an average of 35.6% across 7 datasets of Q&A with search engines, including 29.7% $\rightarrow$ 45.8% on HotpotQA, 27.4% $\rightarrow$ 40.5% on 2WikiMultiHopQA. Overall, we show that EMA-PG is a simple, principled, and powerful approach to scaling RL for LLMs. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TabularMath: Evaluating Computational Extrapolation in Tabular Learning via Program-Verified Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02523)**  `arXiv:2602.02523`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zerui Cheng, Jiashuo Liu, Jianzhu Yao, Pramod Viswanath, Ge Zhang, Wenhao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard tabular benchmarks reward interpolation: predicting values inside the data manifold, where local statistical smoothing works. However, much high-value tabular data, from pricing and financial rules to physical simulation, is produced by deterministic computations rather than noisy relationships. Can tabular models move from statistical interpolation to computational extrapolation, returning exact answers even beyond the range seen in training? We introduce TabularMath, a benchmark of 114 deterministic computations (233,472 rows) compiled from GSM8K and AIME problems into verified programs. We evaluate nine tabular methods, closed-form and symbolic regression baselines, and in-context learning with GPT-OSS-120B. On regression metrics, TabPFN v2.5 looks excellent: with 128 rows its median $R^2$ is 0.999 in-distribution, and it is the only tabular method whose median $R^2$ stays positive (0.76) when nearly every answer exceeds the training range. Exact answers tell a different story. Out of distribution (OOD), its accuracy falls from 39.5% to 7.8%, it answers no query correctly in 70 of 100 GSM8K tasks, and sixteen times more rows raise its OOD accuracy by only 0.4 points. Yet the answers are in the table: by construction, every label is a program's output on the row's inputs, and the training rows typically suffice to recover that program. Symbolic regression on the numbers alone recovers every OOD answer in 72 of 100 GSM8K tasks, and in-context learning with GPT-OSS-120B answers 39.2% of OOD queries. Tabular foundation models fit the trend but miss the computation, an inductive-bias gap that more data does not close. The competition-level AIME tasks remain open for every method.
  </details>

- **[SAME: Stabilized Mixture-of-Experts for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01990)**  `arXiv:2602.01990`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhen-Hao Xie, Jun-Tao Tang, Yu-Cheng Shi, Han-Jia Ye, De-Chuan Zhan, Da-Wei Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, but real-world deployment requires them to continually expand their capabilities, making Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) essential. Recent methods leverage sparse expert routing to promote task specialization, but we find that the expert routing process suffers from drift as the data distribution evolves. For example, a grounding query that previously activated localization experts may instead be routed to irrelevant experts after learning OCR tasks. Meanwhile, the grounding-related experts can be overwritten by new tasks and lose their original functionality. Such failure reflects two problems: router drift, where expert selection becomes inconsistent over time, and expert drift, where shared experts are overwritten across tasks. Therefore, we propose StAbilized Mixture-of-Experts (SAME) for MCIT. To address router drift, SAME stabilizes expert selection by decomposing routing dynamics into orthogonal subspaces and updating only task-relevant directions. To mitigate expert drift, we regulate expert updates via curvature-aware scaling using historical input covariance in a rehearsal-free manner. SAME also introduces adaptive expert activation to freeze selected experts during training, reducing redundant computation and cross-task interference. We also introduce a new benchmark to evaluate MCIT with long task sequence, and extensive experiments demonstrate SAME's SOTA performance. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Object-Centric World Models for Causality-Aware Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.14262)**  `arXiv:2511.14262`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yosuke Nishimoto, Takashi Matsubara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models have been developed to support sample-efficient deep reinforcement learning agents. However, it remains challenging for world models to accurately replicate environments that are high-dimensional, non-stationary, and composed of multiple objects with rich interactions since most world models learn holistic representations of all environmental components. By contrast, humans perceive the environment by decomposing it into discrete objects, facilitating efficient decision-making. Motivated by this insight, we propose \emph{Slot Transformer Imagination with CAusality-aware reinforcement learning} (STICA), a unified framework in which object-centric Transformers serve as the world model and causality-aware policy and value networks. STICA represents each observation as a set of object-centric tokens, together with tokens for the agent action and the resulting reward, enabling the world model to predict token-level dynamics and interactions. The policy and value networks then estimate token-level cause--effect relations and use them in the attention layers, yielding causality-guided decision-making. Experiments on object-rich benchmarks demonstrate that STICA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art agents in both sample efficiency and final performance.
  </details>

- **[Causality can systematically address the monsters under the bench(marks)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05085)**  `arXiv:2502.05085`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Felix Leeb, Zhijing Jin, Bernhard Schölkopf_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective and reliable evaluation is essential for advancing empirical machine learning. However, the increasing accessibility of generalist models and the progress towards ever more complex, high-level tasks make systematic evaluation more challenging. Benchmarks are plagued by various biases, artifacts, or leakage, while models may behave unreliably due to poorly explored failure modes. Haphazard treatments and inconsistent formulations of such "monsters" leads to a duplication of efforts, a lack of trust in results, and unsupported inferences. In this position paper, we argue causality offers an ideal framework to systematically address these challenges. By making causal assumptions in an approach explicit, we can faithfully model phenomena, formulate testable hypotheses with explanatory power, and leverage principled tools for analysis. To make the design of conceptual causal models more accessible, we identify several useful Common Abstract Topologies (CATs) in causal graphs which help gain insight into the reasoning abilities in large language models. Through a series of case studies, we demonstrate how the precise yet pragmatic language of causality clarifies the strengths and limitations of a method and inspires new approaches for systematic progress.
  </details>

- **[A Methodologically Rigorous Benchmark of Linear Regression,RNN, and LSTM for S&P 500 Forecasting:Identifying Sources of Performance Inflation in Financial DeepLearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27978)**  `arXiv:2611.27978`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hari Sitaula, Joseph Opitz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study examines one-day-ahead forecasting of the S&P 500 closing level using a 2006-2025 dataset that includes fundamental price data, macroeconomic indicators, and engineered technical features. We benchmark three model families, namely linear regression, vanilla recurrent neural networks (RNN), and long short-term memory networks (LSTM), within a unified preprocessing and chronological evaluation pipeline. A central contribution of this work is a methodological audit of common practices in the deep-learning-for-stock-prediction literature. We identify three sources of result inflation that frequently appear in published methodologies: wavelet-denoised evaluation targets, the absence of a naive persistence baseline, and best-of-N replicate reporting. We then re-evaluate all model families under a corrected protocol that controls for each. Performance is reported using root mean squared error, mean absolute percentage error, and the Pearson correlation coefficient, averaged across N = 30 independent replicates per configuration. We compare models on accuracy, stability across replicates, and architectural depth sensitivity, using the naive persistence forecast (predicting today's close as tomorrow's close) as a primary baseline against which all candidate models must demonstrate improvement.
  </details>

- **[Ranking-Preserving Coreset Selection for Efficient Evolutionary Hyperparameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27959)**  `arXiv:2611.27959`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mamoona Tasadduq, Maryam Bashir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperparameter optimization (HPO) is essential for high predictive performance, but its computational cost becomes prohibitive when candidate configurations require repeated training on large datasets. This paper proposes a ranking-preserving coreset (RPC) selection framework for accelerating evolutionary HPO. The method constructs a compact coreset that preserves the relative ranking of hyperparameter configurations observed on the full training data. Coreset selection is formulated as an evolutionary optimization problem that maximizes Spearman rank correlation between full-data and subset-based configuration rankings while penalizing subset size. The selected coreset is then used for HPO with three evolutionary algorithms: Genetic Algorithm (GA), Cross-generational elitist Selection, Heterogeneous Recombination and Cataclysmic Mutation (CHC), and Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO). Experiments on seven tabular, two text, and two image benchmark datasets show that RPC substantially reduces HPO time while maintaining test accuracy comparable to full-dataset HPO across decision tree, text classification, and image classification tasks. Across datasets and optimizers, HPO-stage speedups range from approximately 1.14x to 8.80x, while the one-time coreset construction cost is accounted for separately through end-to-end runtime and break-even analysis. On 20 held-out configurations excluded from coreset construction, RPC achieves higher ranking correlation than same-size random sampling on 8 of 11 datasets (dataset-level Wilcoxon signed-rank test, p = 0.014). These results show that ranking preservation extends beyond the configurations used during selection. The one-time construction cost can be amortized when the coreset is reused across HPO runs.
  </details>

- **[Topology Hub Optimal Transport: Eliminating Long Context Dilution at Flash Speed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27951)**  `arXiv:2611.27951`  `cs.LG`  
  _Dylan Forde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context models use sparse or structured attention to avoid quadratic cost, yet retrieving a small set of relevant facts embedded among hundreds of thousands of distractors degrades as normalization mass disperses with length. Hub-and-spoke optimal transport (OT) replaces the dense $L \times L$ matrix with a linear-size graph over local windows and $K$ shared hubs balanced by Sinkhorn scaling. Building on block-wise tail refinement and fixed-support transport topology (arXiv:2605.08123,arXiv:2609.07954), we introduce Topology Hub Optimal Transport (THOT): (1) bounded-support capacity gating via simplex sparsemax ($O(L\log L)$) or sorting-free token-local thresholded ReLU ($O(L)$), driving excess distractor mass to zero; (2) a three-sweep $1+2$ annealed row-closed Sinkhorn schedule that damps bipartite residuals; (3) an exact rank-$(D+2R+1)$ One-Reference-Tile adjoint in batched split-factor Triton kernels (BF16 Tensor Cores, FP32 potentials); and (4) a chunked exclusive-prefix causal variant with verified zero future leakage. On NVIDIA L4, sparsemax THOT trained at $L=128$ reaches 97.40% zero-shot recall of fixed bindings at $L=262{,}144$ under pure task loss ($\alpha=0$) and 98.83% at $\alpha=0.05$ (matched by $O(L)$ thresholded ReLU at $\alpha=0.05$, vs. 80.60% at $\alpha=0$), while softmax-gated hub OT drops to 3.52%. In bidirectional operator microbenchmarks, our kernel runs forward+VJP in 100.1 ms at $H=8, D=64, L=262\mathrm{K}$ (2.2x faster with 4.0x smaller compiler temporaries than latent softmax) and 1.34 ms vs. 4.37 ms for cuDNN FlashAttention at $L=4{,}096$; multi-layer LM runs use the unfused path. Zero-shot WikiText-103 multi-needle retrieval hits 100.00% (128/128 queries at $L=65\mathrm{K}$) vs. 0.78% for short-trained RoPE FlashAttention, and compact 4-layer WikiText-4K runs show modest causal and masked LM gains.
  </details>

- **[DPMA: Learning to Compress Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27759)**  `arXiv:2611.27759`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jon Irureta, Jon Imaz, Philipp Wiesner, Gorka Azkune, Javier Fernandez-Marques, Alex Iacob, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures offer high computational efficiency, but storing all experts requires substantial memory and limits deployment on resource-constrained hardware. Most retraining-free MoE compression methods commit to either pruning or merging experts, often with a uniform compression ratio across layers. We introduce DPMA (Differentiable Prune Merge Absorb), which jointly learns compression operations and layer-wise expert allocation through gradient descent under a global expert budget. Separate learnable coefficients allow DPMA to prune experts, merge their parameters, and absorb their routing contributions by forwarding routing weights to retained experts. These coefficients and expert assignments are optimised to match the original model's outputs on calibration data while its weights remain frozen. Across four MoE models, eight multiple-choice benchmarks, and five compression ratios, DPMA achieves the highest accuracy averaged over benchmarks and compression ratios on each model among the evaluated methods. At $75\%$ expert reduction, it outperforms the strongest baseline on every model, with gains of up to $6.7$ percentage points.
  </details>

- **[SMSD: A Semantic Misinformation Spreader Detection Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27661)**  `arXiv:2611.27661`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zeeshan Bin Siddique, Michael Bewong, Md Geaur Rahman, Sajal Halder, Selasi Kwashie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Misinformation spreaders on social media significantly amplify false narratives, making early detection critical to mitigate their spread. Existing approaches often overlook evolving user behavior, introduce semantic noise by ignoring user stance, and fail to handle newly emerging users effectively. To address these limitations, we propose a \underline{S}emantic \underline{M}isinformation \underline{S}preader \underline{D}etection approach (SMSD) that models misinformation propagation as a temporal, user-centric, and behavior-driven learning problem. SMSD adopts a multimodal architecture that incorporates three categories of features: user-level, network-level, and conversation propagation. A stance-aware labeling strategy is also introduced to differentiate supportive, opposing, questioning, and neutral users towards misinformation claims. Additionally, a monthly quantile analysis is introduced to simulate realistic, evolving user participation. Initial experimental results on the FibVid COVID-19 benchmark dataset demonstrate that SMSD improves recall by 45.0\%, F1 by 49.5\%, and MCC by 74.6\% under stance-aware labeling and by 17.7\%, 7.4\%, and 11.4\%, respectively, under threshold-based labeling. It consistently outperforms all baselines in both settings.
  </details>

- **[WEIR: Watershed Exploration and Drift-Aware Prefix Shrinkage for Multi-Turn Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27570)**  `arXiv:2611.27570`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pixel Nomand, Elena Voss, Marcus Hale, Sofia Reyes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn language model agents often receive only a single binary reward at the end of a long interaction trajectory, making it difficult to separate decisive decisions from routine steps or estimate accurate intermediate values. Prefix-tree methods improve sample efficiency by merging trajectories that share action histories, yet two structural flaws remain: naive pooling mixes stale off-policy outcomes into critic targets with fixed weights, and uniform or entropy-driven branching wastes continuations on dead-end states whose outcome is already settled. We introduce WEIR (Watershed Exploration and Intrinsic-Drift Shrinkage), an actor-critic reinforcement learning framework grounded in a Doob turn-variance conservation identity. WEIR maintains a cross-iteration canonical action-prefix tree with exponentially discounted visit counts and trains a prefix-conditioned critic toward a minimax drift-aware shrinkage target that adapts from single-trajectory returns at rare nodes to pooled empirical means at dense nodes. Using the same pre-update critic, WEIR allocates rollout budgets across tasks via Neyman stratification $B_q \propto \sqrt{\hat{p}_q(1-\hat{p}_q)}$ and directs within-task branches to watershed forks where high survival-headroom uncertainty coincides with steep value cliffs. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, ALFWorld, and WebShop on Qwen2.5-3B/7B-Instruct, WEIR achieves $32.18\%$ and $37.85\%$ average QA exact match and $84.83\%$ and $87.81\%$ ALFWorld success rates, outperforming CAPO by $1.50$--$3.74$ points while generating $28$--$29\%$ fewer rollout tokens than chain sampling.
  </details>

- **[Gumbel Substitution: Removing Sampler Drift from RL Without Trusting the Sampler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27375)**  `arXiv:2611.27375`  `cs.LG`  
  _Romain Fanucci, Malik El Hassani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When RL rollouts come from untrusted samplers on arbitrary hardware, precision and checkpoint lag, the sampling distribution $q$ differs from the trainer's policy $p$, and training on the sampler's tokens adds a drift term equal to supervised fine-tuning toward $q$. Published corrections (TIS, MIS, IcePop, score centering, GSPO, CISPO) all need $q(y)$, which a permissionless sampler reports and can misreport. We introduce Gumbel substitution. The coordinator fixes public Gumbel noise for every token position, bit-identical across GPUs, and the sampler draws $y=\arg\max(\ell^q+G)$. The trainer recomputes $v^*=\arg\max(\ell^p+G)$, the token its own policy would have drawn with the same noise, and scores $v^*$ in the sampler's context. For a sampler that follows the protocol, $v^*$ is an exact sample from $p$ given the prefix, so the drift vanishes in expectation for any $q$, without sampler-reported probabilities. We measure the residual left by the deployed heads, bound the one added by admission gates, and state what an adaptive sampler can still do. The same comparison gives a per-token match rate that admits and pays samplers. In the lab, on a 9B model with the same head on both sides, the expected drift of uncorrected training is at least 170 to 4,800 times that of substitution. On a 2B model under strong mismatch, uncorrected training collapses while substitution stays stable, comparable to MIS with score centering given the true $q$. On a permissionless network, a 9B model was trained for 158 optimizer steps on 15.75B tokens from heterogeneous samplers, with no skipped step and 1.21% of tokens substituted; it improved on all eight held-out benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Improved Accuracy Scaling with Problem Complexity in LLM Arithmetic Reasoning Using Executable Symbolic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27203)**  `arXiv:2611.27203`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yu-Bin Liu, Shi-Ju Ran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High accuracy on mathematical benchmarks does not establish whether reasoning remains reliable as problem complexity increases. We examine how executable symbolic representations change accuracy scaling in large language models compared with natural-language reasoning. To address this limitation, we introduce the GSM Arithmetic Benchmark (GSM-AB), a controlled benchmark that independently varies operand digit length and operation count to probe numerical scale and compositional complexity. We find that executable representations can improve accuracy and slow its decline as complexity increases. Lean reduces average sensitivity to operand digit length at every tested model size. By contrast, slower degradation with increasing operation count emerges at the larger model sizes. A Python program-of-thought (Python-PoT) control shows a similar operation-axis pattern, suggesting that this finding is not specific to Lean. These findings show that reasoning representation shapes arithmetic accuracy scaling and support executable symbolic representations for more reliable reasoning on complex arithmetic tasks.
  </details>

- **[Lift Out, Kick In: Decoupling Event Effects for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27191)**  `arXiv:2611.27191`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunying Zhu, Zichun Cao, Siwei Sun, Xiaocen Guo, Shengsheng Lin, Jing Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world time series are often shaped by exogenous events whose effects are superimposed on the system's underlying temporal dynamics. Historical observations therefore entangle persistent dynamics with event-induced deviations, making it difficult to determine which patterns should be extrapolated and which should be attributed to specific event contexts. Existing event-aware forecasting methods can incorporate event information or model event-related variation, but typically treat historical event attribution and future event generation separately. We propose Loki (Lift Out, Kick In), a general event-aware forecasting framework built on a shared Event Response Function (ERF). The ERF models event effects according to event type and relative temporal position and is shared across the historical and forecast windows. Loki lifts out estimated event-induced deviations from historical observations before forecasting, then uses the same ERF to kick in responses to known future events. This formulation separates event-response modeling from temporal forecasting while coupling historical attribution and future generation through a shared mechanism. Across five forecasting benchmarks, Loki achieves the best standardized MAE on all five datasets, reducing error by approximately 2.2%-11.7% over the strongest non-Loki baseline. Ablations and controlled analyses further support the roles of Lift Out, Kick In, shared response modeling, and compatibility across forecasting backbones.
  </details>

- **[BusemannFormer: Horospherical Self-Attention for Hyperbolic Graph Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27167)**  `arXiv:2611.27167`  `cs.LG`  
  _Youheng Yao, Ziyao Zeng, Wenbo Liao, Tianqi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperbolic neural networks excel on hierarchical graph data, yet existing hyperbolic Transformers largely adapt conventional attention without explicitly exploiting the ideal boundary, a geometric structure closely tied to tree hierarchy. We propose Horospherical Self-Attention (HSA): each query selects an ideal-boundary direction and scores keys by their Busemann depth along that direction, yielding a token-to-token hyperbolic attention kernel. We establish a flat-limit connection to dot-product attention, characterize score level sets as horospheres, and bound key-side gradients. HSA is integrated into BusemannFormer, a fully hyperbolic graph Transformer, with a log-depth variant that uses query depth to modulate attention concentration. We further introduce a finite-boundary-dictionary extension supporting linear-complexity global attention. Experiments on hierarchical and citation graphs show that BusemannFormer-LD outperforms the evaluated baselines on several node-classification benchmarks and achieves competitive performance across diverse graph structures.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Memory-enhanced Pattern Relevance Prediction for Long-tailed Graph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27141)**  `arXiv:2611.27141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiawei Chen, Wenjun Wang, Wei Yu, Qiyao Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-tailed graph classification (LTGC) is common in real-world graph datasets, where a few large head graphs provide rich structural information while many smaller tail graphs offer fewer structural cues, making it harder to learn discriminative representations for tail graphs. To address this size-based imbalance, we propose Dynamic Memory-enhanced Pattern Relevance Prediction (DMPRP), which transfers structural patterns from head graphs to tail graphs. Given a graph representation as a query, a dynamic expert memory module inspired by Knowledge-diverse Experts (KDEX) generates a residual vector that augments the original representation. We train the module using co-occurrence triplets sampled from head graphs at the node, rooted subgraph, and random-walk neighborhood levels. Experiments on five graph classification benchmarks show that DMPRP improves tail performance, and ablation studies show that both the expert module and random-walk supervision contribute to these gains. A controlled experiment on PTC further examines pattern transfer when smaller graphs are denser than larger ones. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing and Adapting kNNGuard for Long-Context Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27084)**  `arXiv:2611.27084`  `cs.LG`  
  _Niccolò Ronchetti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free activation guardrails such as kNNGuard score prompts by comparing last-token LLM hidden states (the Layer Ensemble branch, kNNGuard LE) and sentence embeddings (all-MiniLM-L6-v2, Embedding-kNN) against a small reference bank (50 examples per class), combining the two branches into a Fused Ensemble (kNNGuard FE). While effective on short inputs (up to 256 tokens), standard kNNGuard degrades substantially on long inputs (over 1000 tokens): averaged across Qwen3.5-4B and Gemma-4-E4B-it, kNNGuard LE AUROC drops from 0.742 (0.777 and 0.706) to 0.554 (0.587 and 0.521) on Content Harm Moderation (Aegis-2.0), and from 0.994 (0.995 and 0.992) to 0.556 (0.598 and 0.513) on RAG Hallucination Detection (HaluBench). We trace this degradation to three causes: (1) hidden-state drift as sequence length increases, (2) hard 256-token truncation in MiniLM, and (3) signal dilution when a long document is compressed into a single final-token vector. We then evaluate three training-free adaptations: a Length-Routed Dual Bank, a long-context embedding model (Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B), and Strided Window Pooling across intermediate token positions. On Content Harm Moderation, dual-bank routing and strided window pooling raise long-context kNNGuard LE AUROC from 0.554 to 0.828 (+0.274 AUROC) and fused kNNGuard FE AUROC from 0.721 to 0.886 (+0.164 AUROC) with no latency overhead from bank routing. On RAG Hallucination Detection, where the guardrail must verify logical consistency between a generated answer and a retrieved passage, sentence-embedding kNN remains near random chance across all lengths, while length-matched LLM hidden states (kNNGuard LE) recover medium-length detection (AUROC = 0.853) but remain limited on over-1000-token passages (AUROC = 0.607) due to final-token dilution over long contexts.
  </details>

- **[Action-Conditioned Spatio-Temporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27044)**  `arXiv:2611.27044`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yutong Xia, Yangyou Liu, Yuxuan Liang, Yuankai Wu, See-Kiong Ng, Roger Zimmermann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study spatio-temporal forecasting in networked systems under specified actions. The challenge is to capture the action's effects without predicting spurious changes in unaffected regions. However, direct supervision and evaluation of these effects require paired futures with and without the action, whereas observational trajectories reveal only the realized future. We introduce PairST, a benchmark comprising six datasets across traffic, power transmission and water distribution, with paired reference and intervened futures simulated from the same system state. These pairs enable separate evaluation of affected-region forecast accuracy and quiet-region response error. We further propose ActionST, which forecasts the intervened future by writing an explicit response onto an intervention-independent reference forecast. Specifically, node-time addressing constructs a query for each response position, a state-conditioned response writer computes candidate responses, and a graph-aware locality gate controls their strength. Experiments on six datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of ActionST at new action targets, with the lowest mean absolute error on five datasets and the best average rank. Our source code and the PairST benchmark are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Improving the Optimisation Landscape for Particle Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26931)**  `arXiv:2611.26931`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yan Lin, David T. Frazier, Paula Cordero Encinar, Chris J. Oates_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Several methods in statistics and machine learning represent a predictive distribution as an equally weighted mixture of models, or particles, trained via the mixture predictive likelihood. However, we prove that the mixture predictive likelihood is poorly suited to gradient-based optimisation, because it does not identify how the mixture is decomposed across particles, so that a poorly matched particle can be starved of training signal. As a remedy, we study a convex combination between the individual risk, i.e. the average log loss of the individual particles, and the mixture risk. At the particle level, this regularised risk imposes a reverse-KL barrier on the responsibilities of the particles, so that no particle can be starved of training signal. These phenomena are illustrated in two case studies: In the first, a predictively-oriented posterior is computed using mean-field Langevin dynamics, and the numerics are well-behaved only when our regularisation is employed. In the second, ensembles of low-rank adapters are jointly fine-tuned on large language models, across four backbones and eight multiple-choice reasoning benchmarks; direct minimisation of the mixture risk leads to responsibility starvation, whose severity decreases as the competence of the backbone increases, whereas our regularised estimator attains a lower held-out mixture risk on every backbone considered.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Tier Edge Triage: Optimizing Latency for Safe Clinical Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26914)**  `arXiv:2611.26914`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joshua Broyde, Sarita A. Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying generative AI agents in enterprise clinical workflows requires enforcing multi-dimensional safety and compliance policies--preventing HIPAA Protected Health Information (PHI) exposure, maintaining clinical scope-of-practice boundaries, ensuring patient-appropriate reading comprehension, and preserving brand integrity--under interactive latency constraints. Synchronous outbound calls to cloud Large Language Model (LLM) judges add substantial network and decoding overhead onto the critical response path. We present Multi-Tier Edge Triage, a safety screening architecture that co-locates a compact Tier-1 sequence classifier on the clinical agent's host server, resolving clear queries in-process without a network round-trip and escalating only ambiguous queries to a Tier-2 cloud LLM judge. To evaluate Tier-1 encoders without length or unigram shortcuts, we construct four length-matched contrastive twin benchmarks seeded from MedSafetyBench, ASQ-PHI, and a synthetic four-mandate clinical assistant policy (Generic HealthCorp). While frozen local encoders match cloud models on structured PHI detection (>= 98.3% accuracy in <= 23.5 ms), they degrade markedly on semantic safety and symmetric-blocklist prompts. However, end-to-end representation fine-tuning closes the multi-policy accuracy gap: a fine-tuned 335M-parameter BGE-Large classifier achieves 98.90% out-of-fold accuracy versus 99.20% for a zero-shot cloud LLM judge (exact McNemar p = 0.648, zero shared errors), 97.8%-98.2% recall, and a 0.0%-0.4% over-refusal rate in 39.0 ms (>14x faster than cloud LLM evaluation on our benchmark host). Per-policy error analysis reveals ceiling local recall on PHI (100.0%), brand (100.0%), and clinical scope (98.7%-99.3%) violations, isolating residual errors to reading-level checks (93.3%-95.3%) and motivating targeted multi-tier escalation.
  </details>

- **[A Sparsity Analysis of Traffic Forecasting Architectures: How Much Missing Data is Too Much?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26847)**  `arXiv:2611.26847`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohamed Hamad, Bilal Mutahir, Mohamed Mabrok, Nizar Zorba, Hamid Saeedi, Halim Yanikomeroglu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic forecasting models are typically evaluated under ideal conditions with complete data. This evaluation does not reflect the frequent sensor failures and communication outages common in real-world deployments. This paper presents a comprehensive analysis of multivariate traffic prediction models to evaluate their performance under varying levels of sensor data loss during inference. We benchmark several architectural paradigms, including graph neural networks, attention-based Transformers, and selective state-space models, across sparsity levels ranging from zero to complete sensor loss, using two widely used multi-channel traffic benchmarks. To ensure consistent comparison across traffic variables, we use normalized metrics and define a usability threshold to identify when predictions become unreliable. Our results show a clear trade-off between baseline accuracy and robustness: models with the highest accuracy under full-data conditions often degrade most sharply as data becomes sparse, while less accurate models maintain more stable performance. These findings show that model selection should be based on the expected reliability of the sensor network rather than peak accuracy alone. This work provides a practical framework for assessing traffic forecasting models and highlights the importance of robustness in real-world applications.
  </details>

- **[LEAN: Library-Based Adaptation for Asynchronous, Federated Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26805)**  `arXiv:2611.26805`  `cs.LG`  
  _Erdong Hu, Zhimin Ding, Yuxin Tang, Chris Jermaine_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated fine-tuning of foundation models with low-rank adapters (LoRA) is typically reconciled by averaging locally trained adapters. In cross-device settings, where participants hold heterogeneous data and join or leave asynchronously, averaging is poorly suited: locally trained adapters are not identifiable up to permutation, and averages of adapters trained on dissimilar data can be uninformative or unstable. We propose a simple idea called federated library-based adaptation (LEAN), which replaces averaging altogether. The server maintains a library of rank-1 basis pairs; participants check out random subsets, fine-tune them locally as a LoRA adapter, and check them back in, so that each pair is shuffled across sites and trained on many participants' data. Because no aggregation step is required, LEAN operates fully asynchronously and communicates only low-rank parameters. We prove that, in a synchronous setting, LEAN converges to a neighborhood of a stationary point, with the shuffle canceling data heterogeneity at first order. Across image and text fine-tuning benchmarks, LEAN outperforms averaging-based baselines in most settings with substantially less communication, and remains effective under heavy data heterogeneity, where averaging-based methods fail to reach nontrivial accuracy, and under heavy asynchrony, where they degrade substantially.
  </details>

- **[PSTO: A PDE-structured transport operator with shared implicit updates for multiscale CDR systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25607)**  `arXiv:2611.25607`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiwen Li, Dezhi Zhou, Kun Wu, Xingcai Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiscale convection-diffusion-reaction (CDR) systems couple spatial transport with local stiff reaction kinetics, making their numerical simulation computationally demanding. Conventional neural surrogates require costly coupled CDR training data, and their parameter counts scale with the number of transported variables, limiting scalability to high-dimensional CDR systems. Here we propose a PDE-Structured Transport Operator (PSTO) that learns convection--diffusion dynamics solely from reaction-free data. By embedding the PDE structure into the neural architecture, PSTO generalizes to full CDR systems at inference without retraining. A single transport operator block is shared across all transported variables and refined through relaxed fixed-point iterations, performing an implicit-like correction in latent space. This improves stability during recursive multi-step prediction without adding trainable parameters. We evaluate PSTO on three benchmarks: a canonical 2D CDR system, a multiscale Robertson counterflow flame, and a multiscale hydrogen planar jet flame. In every case, PSTO reduces the mean relative $L_2$ error after 100 coupled steps by at least 50% compared to the strongest neural baseline. Ablation studies on the fixed-point iterations and detailed error decomposition further confirm that PSTO achieves both high one-step accuracy and long-term stability in coupled rollouts. Time-step sensitivity analysis shows that PSTO maintains accuracy even with a tenfold larger time step, whereas baseline accuracies decay rapidly as the time step increases. This framework enables efficient, modular modeling of multiscale CDR dynamics by separating transport learning from the treatment of local kinetics.
  </details>

- **[Reuse or Relearn? A Spectral View of Earth Observation Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32756)**  `arXiv:2609.32756`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mehmet Ozgur Turkoglu, Valerio Marsocci, Dominik J. Mühlematter, Dominik Senti, Konrad Schindler, Helge Aasen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models are rarely used as generic, frozen feature extractors; instead, they are fine-tuned for the target downstream application. This practice is particularly prevalent in Earth observation (EO), and it raises a question that downstream accuracy alone cannot answer: does fine-tuning reuse the pretrained representation, or does it relearn a new one? We study this with spectral diagnostics that compare a model before and after adaptation, quantifying how well its dominant singular subspaces are preserved, how broadly the weight update is distributed, and how large it is. Using natural image models such as CLIP and DINO as a reference, we find that, under the evaluated fine-tuning settings, EO models undergo far larger, higher-rank updates and retain much less of their pretrained structure, so their downstream performance is often obtained with substantial changes to the pretrained weight structure. More broadly, foundation models, and EO foundation models in particular, should be assessed not only by benchmark accuracy, but also by how reusable their pretrained representation is.
  </details>

- **[SAVOR: Score Once, Learn Deeper in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24141)**  `arXiv:2609.24141`  `cs.LG`  
  _Keye Zheng, Hanyu Li, Zhan Cheng, Yuan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own samples with token-level scores from a stronger teacher, and it effectively transfers reasoning ability to small language models. Each training step pays for student rollouts and teacher scoring, yet common OPD configurations make a single pass over the scored batch and then discard it. We observe that reusing the batch alone is not enough, because no single fixed teacher target suits every batch. In our runs, a fixed $\alpha$ of 0.3 exceeds a KL envelope at every training step, while 0.2 leaves over a quarter of it unused. We propose SAVOR, which selects for each batch the strongest teacher target inside the KL envelope, freezes the scored batch, and reuses it for a fixed number of student updates without further teacher calls. On five math reasoning benchmarks with a 1.7B student and a 30B-A3B teacher, SAVOR raises macro accuracy over TOP-D from 15.41 to 17.78 with two passes and to 19.36 with three passes at about the same teacher budget. With three passes, SAVOR nearly matches the final accuracy of TOP-D after one third of training while using 67.2% fewer teacher-scored tokens. These results suggest budgeting reuse depth explicitly in OPD.
  </details>

- **[Crossing the Validation Crisis: Cross-Validation Reduces Benchmarking Variance Surprisingly Well](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12552)**  `arXiv:2606.12552`  `cs.LG`  
  _Célestin Eve, Gaël Varoquaux, Thomas Moreau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern machine learning progresses through empirical work, benchmarking new methods to evaluate relative performance. However, the statistical variability inherent in evaluation, exacerbated by the stochastic nature of many algorithms, often makes performance estimation unreliable with the limited test samples available. The result is a validation crisis, in which genuine advances are difficult to discern. Here, we show that doing more cross-validation markedly improves confidence when evaluating and comparing learning algorithm performances. We introduce the concept of sample gain, which quantifies the virtual data augmentation achieved by using multiple cross-validation splits to reduce benchmarking variance. Experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets (histopathologic scans and NLP fine-tuning) demonstrate that multiple splits can substantially improve the reliability and stability of performance estimates, with diminishing returns often setting in later than expected. We also introduce a procedure to dynamically early-stop cross-validation by estimating from the first few splits whether further splits will bring large sample gains. Our findings highlight the value of pushing cross-validation on available samples to achieve robust and reliable benchmarking, and lead to a simple recommendation: split repeatedly.
  </details>

- **[GIST: Gauge-Invariant Spectral Transformers for Scalable Graph Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16849)**  `arXiv:2603.16849`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mattia Rigotti, Nicholas Thumiger, Thomas Frick_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators on irregular meshes face a fundamental tension. Spectral positional encodings, a natural choice in graph learning for capturing geometry, require cubic-complexity eigendecomposition and inadvertently break gauge invariance through numerical solver artifacts; existing efficient approximations typically sacrifice gauge symmetry by design. Both failure modes break discretization invariance: models fail to transfer across mesh resolutions of the same domain, and similarly across different graphs of related structure in inductive settings. We propose GIST (Gauge-Invariant Spectral Transformer), a scalable neural operator that resolves this tension by restricting attention to pairwise inner products of efficient approximate spectral embeddings. We prove these inner products estimate an exactly gauge-invariant graph kernel at end-to-end $\mathcal{O}(N)$ complexity, and establish a formal connection between gauge invariance and discretization-invariant learning with bounded mismatch error. To our knowledge, GIST is the first scalable graph neural operator with a provable discretization-mismatch bound. Empirically, GIST sets state-of-the-art on the AirfRANS, ShapeNet-Car, DrivAerNet, and DrivAerNet++ mesh benchmarks (up to 750K nodes), and additionally matches strong baselines on standard graph benchmarks (e.g., 99.53% micro-F1 on PPI). Code available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mem-X: A Memory-Dependent Cross-Episode Benchmark for Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27166)**  `arXiv:2611.27166`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Zhikang Shi, Yuk Tung Samuel Fang, Hongyu Ding, Zixuan Chen, Yujin Wang, Junhui Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables embodied agents to reuse past observations across tasks. However, existing cross-episode vision-and-language navigation (VLN) benchmarks often leave it unclear whether successful navigation relies on historical memory or current exploration. We introduce Mem-X, a benchmark designed to evaluate memory-dependent cross-episode VLN. Mem-X organizes episodes into sessions in which subsequent instructions refer to objects or routes encountered in earlier episodes. Its three levels evaluate historical-object recall, in-memory localization and episodic indexing, and off-memory relocalization and historical-route reproduction. For the latter two levels, agents receive episode-local poses but no transformations between episode frames, requiring them to establish spatial correspondence with historical memory. Mem-X comprises 2,071 episodes across 710 sessions in 34 scenes, including 1,204 memory-dependent episodes. We also provide MemBridge, a modular baseline that combines semantic scene memory, cross-episode localization, and coarse-to-fine retrieval to support existing VLN agents. Experiments with state-of-the-art VLN methods demonstrate the benefits of MemBridge while revealing substantial room for improvement on memory-dependent navigation. The benchmark and baseline code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[UA-Depth: Uncertainty-Aware Depth Restoration for Material-Agnostic Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01645)**  `arXiv:2611.01645`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Rui Cao, Jia-Hui Pan, Yin Zhang, Qiwei Meng, Jiapeng Li, Gang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation requires accurate metric depth, yet commodity RGB-D cameras often fail on non-Lambertian objects such as transparent, reflective, and translucent materials, producing holes, structured noise, and biased depth. Most restoration methods produce only a single depth map, treating noisy and reliable regions uniformly for downstream tasks while ignoring regional uncertainty. We present UA-Depth, an uncertainty-aware depth restoration framework that iteratively co-refines depth and pixel-wise uncertainty, and uses uncertainty-gated features to suppress unreliable cues for stable updates. To strengthen zero-shot behavior, UA-Depth leverages a frozen depth foundation model as a prior to initialize missing or unreliable depth, and applies two-stage MAD-weighted log-depth scale normalization to reduce outlier bias in relative-to-metric alignment. We introduce an eight-dataset zero-shot benchmark across diverse scenes, materials, and sensors. Across these benchmarks, UA-Depth achieves the best mean rank among representative baselines, with particularly strong gains on datasets dominated by transparent and reflective objects. Beyond depth metrics, we demonstrate downstream improvements in category-level 6D pose estimation and 6D grasp synthesis by exploiting the predicted uncertainty. \emph{Code, models, and data for training and evaluation will be released upon publication.}
  </details>

- **[FIRE3D: Feed-forward Interactive 3D Scene Reconstruction Within A Minute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08848)**  `arXiv:2609.08848`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Hongchi Xia, Tianhang Cheng, Wei-Chiu Ma, Shenlong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present FIRE3D, a unified framework that transforms a single RGB image or casual video into interactable 3D scene assets for games and interactive applications in under a minute for up to twelve textured instances including preprocessing. At the core of FIRE3D is a feed-forward inference pipeline that predicts a compositional scene representation from posed RGB-D observations estimated from the RGB capture, including the 6-DoF pose, bounding box, mesh, and texture for detected objects. By modeling the scene as a collection of discrete entities, FIRE3D produces amodal object assets that can be independently edited and used in interactive applications. Our framework requires no test-time optimization, runs substantially faster than the compared reconstruction systems, and provides object-level completeness beyond existing feed-forward 3D approaches. We demonstrate leading detection accuracy, strong geometry reconstruction, and competitive rendering quality on the evaluated benchmarks, with substantial runtime gains.
  </details>

- **[EgoSurgHands: An Egocentric 3D Hand Pose Dataset & Benchmark for Open Surgery Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27101)**  `arXiv:2611.27101`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Ankit Pal, Guillaume Kugener, Sofia Volpi, Joshua Schwartz, Alex Idarraga, Pranav Rajpurkar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical skill assessment, AR guidance, and robot learning from demonstration all benefit from 3D tracking of the surgeon's hands, yet no surgical dataset offers real egocentric articulated 3D hand pose. We introduce EgoSurgHands, a benchmark for real-captured egocentric hand pose estimation in open surgery training on synthetic pads. Captured with multi-sensor glasses, it contains 41,909 training hand-instance rows across 55 procedure recordings and 9,476 human-validated evaluation rows across 13 held-out recordings, spanning 13 tasks, 3 glove colors, and 4 trainees from first-year medical student to third-year resident. Each frame carries a multi-sensor stack: intrinsics, hand landmarks with per-hand confidence, timestamps, and eye gaze, plus SLAM extrinsics where SLAM succeeded. Evaluation labels combine sensor-fused pseudo-ground truth, human cross-review of every frame, surgeon adjudication of flagged cases, and an independent multi-view triangulated reference. Alongside the within-corpus, procedure-disjoint split, a leave-one-wearer-out protocol tested unseen trainees. Across eight methods evaluated zero-shot, from MANO-based reconstructors to the two-hand IntagHand and the keypoint-only MediaPipe Hands, egocentric pretraining was associated with stronger transfer on every held-out wearer. WildHands and JointTransformer had 18\% and 40\% lower PA-MPJPE, respectively, than the non-egocentric HaMeR. JointTransformer was the best performing model with an error of 14.95 mm. A substantial surgical domain gap remains, and the widely deployed MediaPipe Hands detected a hand in only 24.1\% of evaluation rows. As a baseline, a 58K-parameter residual corrector (JRN) on frozen backbones reached 10.96-14.85 mm PA-MPJPE on held-out wearers for the two ego-pretrained backbones. We release the dataset and benchmark code atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[SparseHPS: A Unified Model for Human Pose and Shape across Depth Cameras and LiDARs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26730)**  `arXiv:2611.26730`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Ilya Zaychuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recovering 3D human pose and shape from point clouds is central to motion capture, robotics and self-driving vehicles, because depth cameras and LiDARs sense a person directly in metric 3D and work under any lighting. However, current approaches are each tailored to one sensor type: point density differs by more than ten times between devices, public datasets are labeled with incompatible body models, and LiDAR approaches need long input sequences to offset sparse scans. In this work, we present SparseHPS, a novel sensor-agnostic network that recovers SMPL-X pose, shape and global translation from a human point cloud with one set of weights, for single frames and streams alike. Specifically, SparseHPS encodes the cloud with a LitePT backbone over sparse voxels, represents every joint by a learnable query decoded with one common head, localizes the body by voting over voxels, and draws temporal context from a causal memory of tokens from the seven preceding frames. A single model is trained on ten datasets that mix rendered clouds with real depth-camera and LiDAR captures, all converted to one body model. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art results on the SLOPER4D and FreeMotion LiDAR benchmarks, where SparseHPS lowers the root-relative joint error by 22% and 11%, respectively, compared with the strongest prior method, which needs 32 frames. On real Azure Kinect captures of held-out HuMMan subjects, SparseHPS attains a joint error of 34.7 mm. Moreover, the current frame alone already yields the bulk of this accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Cross-Sensor Domain Adaptation for Bridge Point Cloud Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27060)**  `arXiv:2611.27060`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Linghui Huang, Zipeng Wang, Shunjie Chen, Peng Guan, Jiandong Wang, Siqing Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Segmentation models for bridge point clouds lose accuracy when trained on terrestrial laser scanning (TLS) data and applied to mobile laser scanning (MLS) data. This study re-examines the SemanticBridge TLS-to-MLS protocol to determine how sampling density, surface roughness, color, and acquisition viewpoint are expressed in the 8-cm sparse-voxel representation, and how adaptation gains depend on source-model convergence. Using one sparse-convolution U-Net, we combine raw-point statistics with controlled input perturbations and compare adaptive batch normalization, entropy minimization, Mean Teacher, and confidence-filtered self-training under 100- and 300-epoch schedules. TLS and MLS have similar nearest-neighbor spacing after preprocessing (12.08 and 12.02 mm), and a 4-mm perturbation of TLS points changes voxel occupancy by only 4.4%. Color distributions differ systematically, but zeroing color on identical voxels changes target mean intersection over union (mIoU) by only +0.02, and by +0.06 after the color augmentation is scale-corrected. The sensors also observe different portions of the bridge, yielding different class proportions. A 300-epoch source-only model obtains 57.80 mIoU on the paired-bridge protocol. Confidence-filtered self-training improves the 100-epoch baseline, but from a converged model it changes target mIoU by -0.97 (source-selected checkpoints) and -2.44 (final-50-epoch means). Repeated evaluation (standard deviation 0.27-0.64) and seed variation (1.88 across three seeds) are comparable to several method differences. Adaptation results on this benchmark therefore depend on representation scale, source-model convergence, and evaluation variability.
  </details>

- **[RefDecoder: Enhancing Visual Generation with Conditional Video Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15196)**  `arXiv:2605.15196`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiang Fan, Yuheng Wang, Bohan Fang, Zhongzheng Ren, Ranjay Krishna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation powers a vast array of downstream applications. However, while the de facto standard, i.e., latent diffusion models, typically employ heavily conditioned denoising networks, their decoders often remain unconditional. We observe that this architectural asymmetry leads to significant loss of detail and inconsistency relative to the input image. To address this, we argue that the decoder requires equal conditioning to preserve structural integrity. We introduce RefDecoder, a reference-conditioned video VAE decoder by injecting high-fidelity reference image signal directly into the decoding process via reference attention. Specifically, a lightweight image encoder maps the reference frame into the detail-rich high-dimensional tokens, which are co-processed with the denoised video latent tokens at each decoder up-sampling stage. We demonstrate consistent improvements across several distinct decoder backbones (e.g., Wan 2.1 and VideoVAE+), achieving up to +2.1 dB PSNR over the unconditional baselines on the Inter4K, WebVid, and Large Motion reconstruction benchmarks. Notably, RefDecoder can be directly swapped into existing video generation systems without additional fine-tuning, and we report across-the-board improvements in subject consistency, background consistency, and overall quality scores on the VBench I2V benchmark. Beyond I2V, RefDecoder generalizes well to a wide range of visual generation tasks such as style transfer and video editing refinement.
  </details>

- **[SAVIOR: Sample-efficient Adaptation of Vision-Language Models for OCR Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27319)**  `arXiv:2611.27319`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Akshata A Bhat, Sharath Naganna, Saiful Haq, Prashant Khatri, Krishna Chaitanya Reddy Tamataam, Neha Arun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optical Character Recognition (OCR) pipelines and vision-language models exhibit high accuracy on standard OCR and document-understanding benchmarks, yet they systematically underperform on document patterns that are operationally critical in financial workflows. These patterns include vertical or rotated text, logo-embedded vendor names, fine-print regulatory clauses, degraded scans, and complex multi-column layouts. While underrepresented in public datasets, they constitute a substantial portion of real-world failure cases. We introduce SAVIOR, a sample-efficient data curation methodology that targets such high-impact failure scenarios to adapt vision-language models for robust financial OCR. We further propose PaIRS, a structure-aware evaluation metric that measures layout fidelity by comparing pairwise spatial relationships between tokens. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct with SAVIOR-Train achieves a word recall of 0.93. When the resulting layout-aware OCR output is used as context for document question answering, it attains an F1 score of 0.89, outperforming both open- and closed-source models such as GPT-4o, Mistral-OCR, PaddleOCR-VL, and DeepSeek-OCR.
  </details>

- **[What Does a Zoom Actually Add? Decomposing Fine-Grained Perception Failures in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26904)**  `arXiv:2611.26904`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhixuan Ge, Anqi Li, Kexin Geng, Wei Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zooming into a region often turns a wrong answer from a vision-language model (VLM) into a right one, and a growing literature takes this as evidence that the model lacked the pixels. But a zoom changes several things at once. It removes the surrounding context, re-tokenizes, at a higher density, pixels the model has already seen, and, if the first pass was downsampled, exposes detail the model never received. We separate these factors for each failure. Holding the first pass fixed, we vary only the second look and assign every failure to the cheapest one that fixes it: reusing cached tokens, isolating the region, re-tokenizing the seen pixels, or acquiring new ones. The study covers 2,753 zoom-rescued failures of Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3-VL and InternVL3 on V*, ZoomBench and TextVQA, at first-pass budgets from 17x fewer pixels than the source to native resolution. First, most rescued failures did not lack the pixels: upsampling the pixels the model had already seen fixes 47-73% of them even at 17x downsampling, against 1-3% for a random region. Second, the first-pass budget decides how often new pixels are needed: depending on the benchmark, new pixels are required for 23-50% of the rescues at 17x and for 12-23% at 4x. The missing-pixel reading and the accounts that dispute it thus hold at different budgets. Third, the useful magnification is large and differs between objects and text: seen pixels fix more failures up to 6-12x their first-pass size per side on objects and saturate by 4x on text, and even native pixels gain from being rendered beyond native resolution. Allowing any magnification, 68-94% of the failures at 17x need no new pixels. These models more often need a better rendering of evidence they already have than new pixels, which motivates re-tokenizing a region before acquiring it.
  </details>

- **[StereoVLM: Binocular Vision-Language Models for Metric 3D Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27915)**  `arXiv:2611.27915`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Chi Yan, Guoyang Zhao, Wentao Yuan, Yixiao Ge, Yanning Zhou, Dan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit strong semantic understanding but remain limited in their ability to reason about metric 3D space in the physical world. Existing VLMs typically infer 3D scene structure from one or more images without known camera calibration or cross-view geometric constraints. This makes metric 3D inference inherently ambiguous and leaves it unclear whether their spatial predictions are grounded in visual evidence or driven primarily by semantic priors. In contrast, calibrated stereo provides explicit cross-view geometry that resolves this ambiguity and offers a more reliable foundation for metric 3D perception and geometric generalization. We investigate how VLMs can learn transferable 3D representations directly from calibrated binocular observations. To this end, we introduce StereoVLM, the first geometry-aware binocular VLM for metric 3D spatial reasoning. StereoVLM follows a hierarchical learning paradigm spanning from cross-view correspondence and disparity estimation to calibration-conditioned metric depth perception and semantic spatial reasoning. We further find that disparity supervision generalizes substantially better than direct binocular depth supervision on unseen environments. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce Stereo3D-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark across three levels, from point-level geometry to object-level spatial reasoning. StereoVLM-8B achieves 95.17% depth accuracy ($\delta_1$) and a 66.98 average object-level score on Stereo3D-Bench. Intervention results further indicate that the model's performance on Stereo3D-Bench benefits from valid cross-view geometry and cannot be explained solely by additional visual tokens or semantic priors. Hierarchical geometric supervision also yields average gains of 3.54 points across five spatial benchmarks and 4.3 percentage points in RoboCasa manipulation success.
  </details>

- **[WorldFlow: Joint Video and 3D Motion Prediction via Large-Scale Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39278)**  `arXiv:2610.39278`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Yuhao Lu, Jiongxu Chen, Hexiang Wei, Jiajin Wu, Juelin Li, Mingjian Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence.
