Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Redesigning Neural Networks to Not be Confidently Wrong even to Strong Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27811)**  `arXiv:2611.27811`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Michael W. Spratling, Heiko H. Schütt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early deep learning models attempted to simulate visual perception using a neural hierarchy that alternated between two types of layer: one modelled on cortical simple cells (that was designed to increase selectivity), and one modelled on cortical complex cells (that aimed to increase tolerance). We show that such a neural network (NN) architecture, when implemented with novel components that are better able to learn selective and tolerant representations, is capable of accurate and robust image classification and improved alignment between the behaviour of the model and the perceptual abilities of humans. Firstly, the proposed models are shallow, fewer than ten layer-pairs, which is more consistent with the rapid feedforward object categorisation performed by the biological visual system. Secondly, they are far less prone to producing classification predictions with high confidence for samples from unknown classes or for image consisting of pure noise, and hence, like humans are not easily fooled into perceiving objects that are not present in the image. Thirdly, for the proposed NNs adversarial samples cause predictions with low confidence, enabling them to be accurately identified. In contrast to humans, our networks still do not correctly classify adversarial samples. However, the behaviour of our networks is a significant step towards human-like behaviour compared to standard DNNs that produce highly confident, but incorrect, classification predictions for adversarial samples. The increased robustness to adversarial and unknown samples is achieved while maintaining competitive performance in terms of generalisation to novel samples from the training categories. This enables models of this type to be competitive with state-of-the-art DNNs across multiple different evaluation metrics and benchmark tasks.
  </details>

- **[A Multi-Center Dataset and Benchmark for SYNTAX Segmentation of Coronary Angiograms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22139)**  `arXiv:2607.22139`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dominik Bernard Lau, Jerzy Szyjut, Hubert Malinowski, Adam Brzeski, Tomasz Dziubich, Natalia Zielińska, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense pixel-level classification of coronary angiography is essential for automated cardiovascular diagnostics, yet the computer vision community lacks gold-standard protocols and robust datasets to tackle this topologically complex, heavily long-tailed domain. To bridge this gap, we introduce CARDIAG: a rigorous, multi-center benchmark dataset designed to evaluate deep learning architectures on fine-grained SYNTAX segment classification. Unlike prior efforts, CARDIAG systematically prevents patient- and center-level data leakage while providing an unprecedented suite of annotations: highly imbalanced multi-class labels, explicit catheter and stenosis masks, expert uncertainty maps, temporal sequence frames, and spatial metadata. Using CARDIAG, we conduct the most extensive architectural sweep in this domain, benchmarking 24 models spanning classic CNNs, Vision Transformers (ViTs), and State-Space Models (SSMs). Moving beyond naive overlap metrics, we evaluate topological preservation (clDice), vessel diameter error, and uncertainty error-detection. Our analysis reveals an insight: modern large-kernel CNNs (particularly ConvNeXt V2 with DeepLabV3+) still outperform recent ViTs and SSMs under a shared training recipe, establishing a Dice = 0.456. By ensembling complementary models, we push the baseline to Dice = 0.479. Through leave-one-center-out cross-validation and data efficiency analyses, we explore the current limits of out-of-distribution generalization. Ultimately, CARDIAG establishes a foundation for advancing medical computer vision for cardiology across many downstream tasks, including lesion detection, temporal frame interpolation, and 3D reconstruction.
  </details>

- **[Reconstruction Alignment Improves Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07295)**  `arXiv:2509.07295`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ji Xie, Trevor Darrell, Luke Zettlemoyer, XuDong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We discover a simple but effective way for visual understanding to help visual generation in a unified model. Unified multimodal models (UMMs) unify visual understanding and generation within a single architecture. However, conventional training relies on image-text pairs (or sequences) whose captions are typically sparse and miss fine-grained visual details, even when they use hundreds of words to describe a simple image. We introduce Reconstruction Alignment (RecA), a resource-efficient post-training method that leverages visual understanding encoder embeddings as dense "text prompts," providing rich supervision without captions. Concretely, RecA conditions a UMM on its own visual understanding embeddings and optimizes it to reconstruct the input image with a self-supervised reconstruction loss, thereby using visual understanding to improve image generation. Despite its simplicity, RecA is broadly applicable: across autoregressive, masked-autoregressive, and diffusion-based UMMs, it consistently improves generation and editing fidelity. With only 27 GPU hours, post-training with RecA substantially improves image generation performance on GenEval (0.73 $\rightarrow$ 0.90) and DPGBench (80.93 $\rightarrow$ 88.15), while also boosting editing benchmarks (ImgEdit 3.38 $\rightarrow$ 3.75, GEdit 6.94 $\rightarrow$ 7.27). Notably, RecA surpasses much larger open-source models and applies broadly across diverse UMM architectures, establishing it as an efficient and general post-training alignment strategy for UMMs.
  </details>

- **[PaMuk-Bench: A Parallel Multilingual Multitask Benchmark for Vision-Language Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27521)**  `arXiv:2611.27521`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Silviu-Florin Gheorghe, Radu-Stefan Popa, Adriana Valentina Costache, Adrian-Gabriel Chifu, Alessia Amelio, Paul Irofti, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While vision-language models (VLMs) currently obtain notable results for highly-studied languages, e.g. English, they exhibit significant performance drops in certain low-resource languages, especially due to the lack of annotated datasets. To this end, we propose PaMuk-Bench, a novel parallel multilingual multitask benchmark for vision-language tasks. Our benchmark includes a set of over 10K curated English prompts that are translated into six languages: Arabic, Danish, French, Hindi, Italian, Romanian. Parallel translations, which are obtained via a semi-automatic process, along with accompanying generated and retrieved images enable the comparative development and evaluation of VLMs across a wide range of tasks: text-to-image generation, image captioning, content-based image retrieval, image generation difficulty estimation, image retrieval difficulty estimation. We empirically show that zero-shot English and multilingual pre-trained VLMs compare consistently worse than their fine-tuned counterparts, confirming that PaMuk-Bench is a valuable resource that can drastically reduce the language gap across a variety of VLM architectures and tasks. PaMuk-Bench is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MemLife: Curating and Reasoning over Long-Term Egocentric Video Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40195)**  `arXiv:2609.40195`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Guangzhi Xiong, Xinyuan Zhang, Xiao Yang, Hyokun Yun, Kai Zhang, Shiun-Zu Kuo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term egocentric video enables personalized AI assistants to reason about daily life. However, as video histories grow to hundreds of hours spanning months or years, reprocessing raw clips for every query becomes computationally prohibitive. Memory systems offer a scalable alternative by compacting videos into text representations, but often fail on practical benchmarks: either the memory does not preserve key evidence, or the retriever fails to locate relevant entries due to retrieval competition in growing search spaces. To address these challenges, we introduce MemLife, a multimodal memory system that constructs entity-grounded, first-person text episodes and retrieves them via a time-indexed agentic reader. Without training or query-time video access, MemLife improves over the strongest training-free baseline by 4.6-12.0% across four long-horizon benchmarks. To further improve memory quality, we propose MemOpt, a reinforcement learning framework that optimizes the memory writer to produce faithful, informative, and retrievable memories. MemOpt consistently improves MemLife by 2.7-5.0% across different video and question distributions, with gains that generalize across writer and reader backbones and memory systems.
  </details>

- **[Verify Before You Generate: Sketch-Guided Efficient Motion-Planning for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17450)**  `arXiv:2511.17450`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yidong Huang, Zun Wang, Han Lin, Dong-Ki Kim, Shayegan Omidshafiei, Jaehong Yoon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent video generation approaches increasingly rely on planning intermediate control signals like object trajectories to improve temporal coherence and motion fidelity. However, these methods mostly employ single-shot plans that are typically limited to simple motion instructions, leading to suboptimal motion quality, or iterative refinement which requires multiple calls to the video generator and incurs high computational cost. To address this trade-off, we propose SketchVerify, a training-free framework whose key insight is to verify motion plans using lightweight video sketches before invoking the expensive video generator. This lightweight planning space enables efficient comparison of multiple candidate trajectories and bounded iterative refinement before a single final video-generation pass. Given a prompt and a reference image, SketchVerify samples multiple candidate trajectories and renders each as a lightweight video sketch by compositing the moving objects over a static background. A vision-language verifier then ranks these sketches according to instruction alignment and visual motion consistency, allowing unsuitable trajectories to be rejected before full video synthesis. The motion plan is iteratively refined until a satisfactory one is identified, then passed to a trajectory-conditioned generator for final synthesis. Experiments on WORLDMODELBENCH and PHYWORLDBENCH demonstrate consistent improvements in instruction following and temporally coherent, visually plausible motion. Using the same ATI-14B generator, SketchVerify improves the aggregate WorldModelBench score from 7.71 to 8.71. Compared with full-video iterative refinement, it achieves a 13.1x planning-stage speedup and a 3.6x end-to-end speedup.
  </details>

- **[Non-penetrating World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27963)**  `arXiv:2611.27963`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhang Dai, Yinwei Wu, Ziyue Lin, Han Ji, Xingyi Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study a new problem of preventing object penetration in interactive video world models. To understand how existing models handle this, we introduce NoPeBench, a benchmark that evaluates how world models respond to physical obstacles. Our evaluation reveals that none of the open-source models can reliably prevent such failures. Surprisingly, however, these models already encode the information needed to predict such violations. We find that the hidden states of diffusion transformers (DiTs) can predict whether a candidate action will penetrate an obstacle. Building on this, we introduce NoPeWorld, a lightweight framework that detects and prevents penetration. Specifically, we train a small prediction head on top of a frozen DiT's hidden states to read out if a candidate action is safe or not. Unsafe actions are then replaced with safe ones that best preserve the original intent. Despite its simplicity, NoPeWorld substantially reduces the physical violation rate, averaged over six video world models, from 74% to 16%. Remarkably, our method shows zero-shot generalization to open-domain imagery, third-person viewpoints, and moving obstacles. We publicly release our code, the NoPeBench benchmark, and model weights. Project Page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Search-RSI: Expanding Search Worlds Along Multimodal Agents' Capability Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27780)**  `arXiv:2611.27780`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengchao Lv, Yin Wang, Chunzheng Zhu, Wanli Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal search agents answer knowledge intensive visual questions by grounding them in retrieved evidence, and recent recipes train them on multi hop tasks that are synthesized from knowledge graphs and grown online to keep pace with the agent. These recipes, however, treat the searchable world as fixed: a new question can be well formed and verifiable while its evidence is absent from the retrieval backend, so the agent issues the right query, retrieves nothing, and is penalized as if its policy were at fault. Such unreachable negatives accumulate with every round of growth. We propose Search-RSI, a recursive self improvement framework that expands the search world along the agent's capability frontier. In each round, the current policy diagnoses its partially mastered regions of a task space spanned by hop depth, relation rarity, and distractor density; new tasks there are grown together with their evidence; and the same policy decides how they are used: candidates on which its probes disagree feed reinforcement learning, while those beyond its reach become hint guided demonstrations of its own. Keeping the policy inside this loop matters: an improver frozen at the start of training loses 3.2 points. On nine multimodal search benchmarks, Search-RSI keeps improving where static task growth saturates, raising the average accuracy of the recipe it builds on by 9.8 and 9.5 points at 9B and 27B, of which 7.0 points are out of reach for a world that is merely made consistent. The loop uses no external model and only privileged task structure that is removed before training, at 1.27x the compute of static growth.
  </details>

- **[Taking Apart and Putting Together: Tracking Objects Through Physical Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27398)**  `arXiv:2611.27398`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Davide Gardenal, Mingxuan Liu, Flavio Vella, Quentin Guimard, Elisa Ricci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Transformation Tracking and Understanding (TTU), extending video object tracking to environments where objects undergo physical assemblies and disassemblies. TTU requires continuously tracking objects through transformations, identifying new entities, maintaining a state graph of their relationships, and assigning semantic labels to transformations. While foundation models (e.g., SAM 2/3) maintain tracklets through appearance changes, they cannot update semantic identities as objects physically evolve. Prior work addresses only disassemblies, relying on expensive searches triggered only when tracking fails. We propose Tracking Instances via Proposals and Overlaps (TIPO), a training-free framework that continuously infers transformations via a generate-evaluate-judge pipeline. TIPO generates split and merge proposals from disjoint mask components, scores them against mask-decoder priors using inner and external overlap, and queries an MLLM on the top proposal for visual verification and semantic labeling. To benchmark TTU, we curate Video Instances, States, and Transformation Annotations (VISTA), a dataset including state graphs with 124 assembly and 132 disassembly events across 50 sequences and over 26,000 frames. TIPO achieves state-of-the-art tracking and state-graph accuracy on VISTA, while operating $6\times$ to $15\times$ faster than prior methods. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PanoUAV-Track: Full-Spherical UAV Visual Tracking in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27383)**  `arXiv:2611.27383`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Bingyu Li, Lichang Yang, Zhiyuan Zhao, Junyu Gao, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing UAV tracking benchmarks predominantly rely on perspective cameras with limited fields of view. Omnidirectional cameras enable full-spherical observation, yet tracking from moving UAV platforms remains largely unexplored. Unlike ground-level panoramic tracking, UAV-mounted omnidirectional cameras observe targets across both azimuth and elevation, introducing geometric challenges under equirectangular projection (ERP). To investigate this setting, we capture approximately \textbf{40 hours of real-world 8K panoramic UAV video} and construct \textbf{PanoUAV-Track} through temporal clip extraction, target annotation, and the integration of simulated sequences. The resulting benchmark comprises 1,895 sequences, 507,961 frames, and 2,872 annotated objects, covering real-world daytime and nighttime scenes alongside simulation, and supporting both visual and language-based target specification. Through extensive evaluations, we reveal a clear \textbf{perspective-to-spherical} generalization gap and identify tracking degradation associated with latitude-dependent distortion and ERP seam discontinuity. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{PanoUAV-Tracker (PUTrack)}, a geometry-aware tracking framework combining latitude-adaptive search and seam-aware periodic sampling. PUTrack improves zero-shot and in-domain tracking, particularly for seam-crossing targets. Code and benchmark resources will be available\footnote{\textcolor{tabpink}{this https URL}}.
  </details>

- **[VMSA: Video Memory Skill Agent for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27381)**  `arXiv:2611.27381`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Changin Choi, Jungwon Park, Jungmin Ko, Jimyeong Kim, Wonjong Rhee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video question answering requires retrieving and integrating relevant evidence from videos spanning hours or even days. Recent agentic approaches organize video information into external memories and iteratively retrieve evidence to answer questions. However, effective retrieval and reasoning remain challenging, as agents must determine what information to search for, which memory sources to access, and how to connect evidence across distant temporal intervals. To address these challenges, we propose Video Memory Skill Agent (VMSA), a framework that jointly designs structured video memories and memory skills for long-video understanding. VMSA constructs three complementary memory components. Segment Caption Memory and Entity Event Memory serve as searchable evidence sources, while Entity-Aware Episode Memory provides broad yet detailed video context to guide evidence retrieval and reasoning. To further support effective memory utilization, VMSA induces memory skills from successful agent trajectories, incrementally refining or extending a compact skill library to guide iterative evidence retrieval, reasoning, and verification. Across seven long-video question-answering benchmarks, VMSA achieves the highest accuracy on all three week-long egocentric benchmarks, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 9.4% on Ego-R1 Bench, and the best or competitive performance on four additional benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of all three memory components and memory skills, with memory skills also improving temporal evidence localization.
  </details>

- **[Unimodal Shortcuts and Joint Scene-Intent Dependence in Vision-Language Safety Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27339)**  `arXiv:2611.27339`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Jiajun Zha, Hanqing Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The safety of a request to a vision-language model often depends on the image: asking how to serve a dish is harmless for fresh food and harmful for spoiled food. High accuracy on situational safety benchmarks does not tell us whether a model's safety representation encodes both the scene and the user's intent, because a benchmark can often be solved from one modality alone. Studying frozen vision-language safety representations, we audit five public situational benchmarks and find that each admits a unimodal shortcut: images alone predict the labels, and on same-image prompt pairs a probe that never sees the photograph reaches 0.9645 AUROC. We therefore build a controlled evaluation that crosses safe and hazardous scenes with observation and action requests, so that no single-modality rule can exceed 0.500 group-ranking accuracy. On this control, linear probes on intermediate states of Qwen3-VL-8B and SmolVLM2-2.2B reach 0.958 and 0.930, showing that the scene-intent conjunction is linearly decodable; concatenating separately encoded image and text states already recovers most of this signal, so decodability is not evidence of a fusion mechanism. The same probes screen cheaply but generalize poorly to unseen images and other benchmarks. Reliable multimodal safety evaluation therefore needs controls that rule out unimodal shortcuts before accuracy is read as evidence of joint understanding.
  </details>

- **[Can Reasoning and Image Description Enhance Yes/No Visual Mathematical Understanding?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27302)**  `arXiv:2611.27302`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Guangcheng Song, Weichuan Wang, Linqi Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general visual tasks, yet their ability to understand mathematical images remains limited. This paper investigates whether explicit reasoning and textual image descriptions can enhance VLMs' performance on binary yes/no visual mathematical questions. To achieve this, we curated a paired evaluation set to benchmark prominent open-source VLMs under varying prompt and input conditions. In particular, we leverage Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting and use textual descriptions, either replacing or augmenting visual inputs, to isolate and evaluate mathematical comprehension. Our results show that explicit reasoning improves performance, especially in paired consistency accuracy, while textual image descriptions do not consistently help and may degrade performance. These findings suggest that reasoning is important for stable visual mathematical judgement, whereas description-based intermediates require more careful design.
  </details>

- **[EgoLAM: Learning Latent Actions from Hand and Head Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27290)**  `arXiv:2611.27290`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyi Ding, Yiwei Wang, Zhongxing Wei, Xianxin Lai, Yechi Liu, Xiaodi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent action models let a robot policy learn from video by turning the change between two frames into a training target. In egocentric video, a cup can move across the image because a hand pushes it or because the wearer turns. Both changes help predict the next frame, but only the first describes manipulation. During training, EgoLAM supervises the action code with measured wrist motion and supplies camera motion separately to the forward model. Even when the wearer turns while pushing a cup, the training target still describes how the hand pushed it. The action encoder pools object slots around each hand, and wrist translation and rotation can be decoded from the code in a common reference frame. Extracting a code then requires only two RGB frames. Alongside LARYBench classification and regression, we measure how much wrist motion a frozen code retains and how strongly it responds to a synthetic view or lighting change. EgoLAM has the highest hand-motion decoding score among eight evaluated models. Across three LIBERO suites, policies pretrained on its codes and fine-tuned on expert demonstrations achieve 79.38% mean success, 28.73 points above expert behavior cloning and 9.76 points above the strongest other latent action model in each suite on average.
  </details>

- **[EAGLE: Entity-Aware Grounding with Layered Evidence for High-Resolution Image Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27264)**  `arXiv:2611.27264`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhongjian Zou, Quan Qian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) still struggle with fine-grained understanding of high-resolution images: attention heads vary in their reliability for grounding target entities and their attributes, while localized details are often disconnected from the broader scene context. We propose EAGLE (Entity-Aware Grounding with Layered Evidence for High-Resolution Image Understanding), a plug-in framework that jointly selects grounding signals and organizes hierarchical visual evidence. EAGLE employs a fine-grained dataset to screen visually-grounded attention heads, followed by entity-aware grounding to extract target-specific regions. It then integrates hierarchical evidence construction and question-guided routing to select optimal view combinations, feeding them into the frozen MLLM to enhance fine-grained visual understanding. Experiments with Qwen2.5-VL-7B and InternVL3-8B demonstrate that EAGLE consistently improves performance across both backbones and achieves SOTA results on V*, HRBench-4K, and HRBench-8K compared with existing methods using the same backbone.
  </details>

- **[When Perception Collides with Priors: Diagnosing Modal Polarization in Video Understanding Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26972)**  `arXiv:2611.26972`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Tong, Jiaxin Qi, Yan Cui, Jinli Ou, Yuhua Zheng, Jianqiang Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong video comprehension, largely benefiting from extensive training on realistic video data. However, when video perception severely conflicts with internal priors or common sense, we identify a critical failure pattern termed Modal Polarization, where models fail to integrate modalities and instead bifurcate into two distinct extremes: Cognitive Inertia, where models rigidly adhere to priors despite contradictory visual evidence, and Perceptual Inertia, where they blindly trust video perception while ignoring logical inconsistencies. To investigate this, we introduce TheaterBench, a benchmark designed to expose Modal Polarization using counter-intuitive scenarios that violate physical laws or common sense - situations rarely encountered in standard training distributions. Extensive experiments reveal that current state-of-the-art (SOTA) models exhibit severe Modal Polarization, and many de-hallucination methods merely rebalance the trade-off between Perceptual and Cognitive Inertia rather than fundamentally resolving the conflict. This work serves as a foundational step for evaluating and enhancing the perceptual-cognitive equilibrium of future video understanding models.
  </details>

- **[Model the Annotation, Not the Noise: Semantic Annotation-Channel Prompt Learning against Noisy Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26702)**  `arXiv:2611.26702`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zibin Geng, Yitong Bao, Jia Li, Xuefeng Jiang, Sheng Sun, Yuwei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient prompt learning has become a cornerstone for adapting pre-trained vision-language models (VLMs), yet it remains highly vulnerable to label noise in few-shot Web and retrieval scenarios. Existing noise robust methods typically rely on hard sample selection or deterministic label correction, which discard valuable semantic ambiguity and overlook how observed annotations are actually generated. To bridge this gap, we reformulate noisy prompt learning through the perspective of data generation, proposing SACP, a Semantic-aware Annotation Channel-based Prompt learning framework. Instead of suppressing noise heuristically, SACP decouples latent clean-label prediction from instance-dependent annotation generation. By integrating frozen VLM semantic priors with lightweight visual residuals, SACP constructs a structured annotation channel optimized via generalized expectation-maximization with soft posteriors. Furthermore, a posterior-aware memory is introduced to preserve label uncertainty during test-time inference. Extensive experiments across different synthetic benchmarks and the real-world noisy Food101N dataset demonstrate that SACP achieves state-of-the-art performance against its counterparts in different label noise patterns, especially under severe asymmetric noise.
  </details>

- **[dKV Forcing: Separating Generation and Memory Representations through a Differentiable KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01648)**  `arXiv:2611.01648`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqi Li, Bo Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A causal video generator writes every segment it produces into a key--value (KV) cache, and later segments know the past only through it. Self-rollout training stops the gradient at this cache, and Self Gradient Forcing (SGF) restores it, but applies it to the same weights that generate the current segment. We find that writing and generating are different roles: in a pretrained generator the written KV already differs from the KV used for denoising, and on shared weights the gradient from future reads is negatively aligned with the gradient of generation. We propose dKV Forcing (dKV denotes differentiable KV), which gives the write its own parameters, a low-rank residual used only when a segment enters the cache, and trains them with the gradient of the later segments that read what they wrote. Opening this gradient through the cache in common use exhausts GPU memory. We trace this to checkpoint replay re-writing the cache, not to the gradient path, which removes the need for the second reconstruction pass of SGF. dKV Forcing keeps the cache as persistent differentiable pages and computes the exact gradient in one temporal traversal, with an $11\%$ lower memory peak and $28\%$ less resident memory. With pages that store hidden states, it trains 15\,s clips on which the two-pass reconstruction runs out of memory. Trained on 5\,s clips, dKV Forcing reaches the highest VBench-Long quality score at 60\,s and 240\,s and outperforms SGF by $3.6$ and $6.1$ points of aesthetic quality, with a higher dynamic degree.
  </details>

- **[When MLLMs miss what humans read: diagnosing and learning to read squint text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13465)**  `arXiv:2610.13465`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chen Peng, Shan Huang, Jianbo Huang, Linrui Qin, Qibo Sun, Mengwei Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal LLMs can fail to read text embedded in an image's coarse structure, text a human recovers by squinting, and report that no text is present. We name this class Squint Text and study it as a recognition deficiency, motivated by cases from a production moderation pipeline. We introduce SquintBench, comprising 1076 production-derived moderation images, and SquintCtrl, a controlled synthetic suite for mechanism diagnosis. Frozen-weight analysis shows that hidden text leaves a coarse signal while glyph identity is weakly decodable under global pooling. Restricting read-out to the text region improves glyph-identity decoding, identifying spatial selection as an important factor in glyph-identity read-out; an exact glyph mask further improves decoding. Suppressing high-frequency content also improves reading at a fixed visual-token budget, while visual token count may also contribute. These findings motivate Squint OPSD: a frozen teacher receives a downscaled view and the ground-truth transcription as privileged context, and its token-level distributions supervise a student along on-policy trajectories. At inference, the student reads the original image alone. Squint OPSD reaches 88.61 ANLS on SquintBench, versus 26.50 for the unadapted backbone, 24.44 for the best general-purpose model on the original view, and 68.60 at best for an off-the-shelf model under a downscaled view. These results substantially narrow the gap between available visual evidence and its generated read-out without auxiliary views at inference.
  </details>

- **[Towards Embodied Air-Ground Cooperative Object Search: Benchmark, Dataset and Agentic Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08402)**  `arXiv:2609.08402`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Boao Yu, Zimo Chen, Junreng Rao, Yue Hu, Zhengqiu Zhu, Yong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban object search, locating a specific object from a single reference image without route or direction instructions and confirming the instance up close, is a fundamental capability for embodied agents. Existing urban embodied benchmarks evaluate one segment of this chain: navigation ends at a coordinate, and detection and question answering stop at an answer. We present AGOS-Bench, the first benchmark for urban cooperative object search by an air-ground team, integrating aerial discovery and ground-level instance verification in one closed loop. Nine metrics assess search, cooperation, and verification, and they distinguish "found but unconfirmed" from "never found". AGOS-Dataset instantiates the task in 2,125 episodes across six city maps under seen and unseen test settings, contributing 27,720 Train Examples along with 1,100 Expert Trajectories. To solve the task, we propose AGOS-Agent, which combines algorithmic coverage and approach routing with event-driven MLLM judgments for target verification and cooperation. On the unseen test set, zero-shot AGOS-Agent (Qwen3.5-4B) achieves a success rate (SR) of 37.67%, 7.00 percentage points (pp) above a controlled baseline with identical information, actions, and prompts. LoRA fine-tuning raises SR to 57.00% (+19.33 pp). Adapted existing systems AGC-VLN-OS and AeroDuo-OS achieve SRs of 31.33% and 28.67%, respectively. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[R3D: Quantitative 3D Spatial Reasoning for Egocentric Wearables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02921)**  `arXiv:2607.02921`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Maxwell Horton, Wei Lu, Quan Tran, Yury Astashonok, Kirmani Ahmed, Babak Damavandi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantitative 3D spatial reasoning from egocentric RGB-D video is an important capability for next-generation wearable assistants. Yet existing benchmarks do not reflect the challenges of handling (1) natural egocentric video, (2) posed RGB-D video inputs, and (3) challenging quantitative 3D spatial reasoning Q&A. To fill this gap, we introduce R3D-Bench (Reasoning in 3D), a benchmark of 2,950 quantitative spatial reasoning questions across 15 types--spanning multiple-choice, distance-based, and volumetric reasoning questions--built on top of 57 egocentric video sequences from Aria Digital Twin. To set a strong baseline on this dataset, we introduce R3D, a model-agnostic spatial tool-calling framework. In contrast to existing approaches that directly embed 3D information into the model's input representation, R3D constructs a 3D scene from video using segmentation and depth-lifted object representations. It provides this information to an LLM through seven composable spatial tools. On R3D-Bench, R3D with Qwen3.6 35B-A3B outperforms all baselines $<$100B parameters, achieving 55.6% mean relative accuracy, a substantial increase over the second-best approach (CuTR+Tools, 47.7%). In the $\ge$100B parameter domain, our R3D Qwen3-VL 235B-A22B Thinking variant improves upon its base model by 7.7% absolute, achieving 57.0% accuracy and outperforming all open- and closed-source models except closed-source GPT-5.5 (59.1%), with which it remains competitive. We publicly release both R3D-Bench and our full implementation.
  </details>

- **[Moment-Video: Diagnosing Temporal Fidelity of Video MLLMs on Momentary Visual Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02522)**  `arXiv:2606.02522`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaolin Liu, Yilun Zhu, Xiangyu Zhao, Xuehui Wang, Yan Li, Xin Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in general and long-form video understanding, yet their ability to capture and use brief, answer-critical visual evidence remains underexplored. Momentary actions or state transitions may determine an answer while occupying only a small fraction of the video. Performance on broad video understanding tasks alone does not establish whether models can reliably identify and use this temporally localized evidence. We introduce Moment-Video, a benchmark for evaluating temporal fidelity through momentary visual event understanding. Questions are constructed around visually observable events or event sequences, requiring models to notice, count, describe, or reason about transient evidence. The benchmark contains 1,000 human-verified video-QA pairs across seven domains and 25 subcategories, covering four tasks: Temporal Occurrence, Temporal Counting, Action Description, and Temporal Reasoning. We evaluate 34 proprietary and open-source MLLMs. Under the main evaluation settings, the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves 39.6% overall accuracy, while most open-source models remain below 25%. Diagnostic analyses show that denser frame sampling improves some models, but substantial errors persist in an audited subset even when human review judges the sampled visual evidence sufficient to answer. Performance also declines on longer videos, suggesting additional temporal-localization challenges. By exposing these limitations, Moment-Video provides a diagnostic benchmark to guide the evaluation and development of video MLLMs that more reliably understand brief, answer-critical visual events.
  </details>

- **[Towards Clinically Interpretable Ophthalmic VQA via Spatially-Grounded Lesion Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22414)**  `arXiv:2605.22414`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyue Wang, Bo Liu, Meng Wang, Zhixuan Zhang, Chengcheng Zhu, Huazhu Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual Question Answering (VQA) holds great promise for clinical support, particularly in ophthalmology, where retinal fundus photography is essential for diagnosis. However, ophthalmic VQA benchmarks primarily emphasize answer accuracy, neglecting the explicit visual evidence necessary for clinical interpretability. In this work, we introduce FundusGround, a new benchmark for clinically interpretable ophthalmic VQA with spatially-grounded lesion evidence. Specifically, we propose a three-stage pipeline that collects 10,719 fundus images with 15,595 image-level meticulously annotated lesions. To ensure anatomical consistency and clinical validity, all lesions are spatially localized using the Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) grid, enabling standardized mapping to nine clinically meaningful retinal regions. Built upon this structured lesion evidence, 72,706 questions are then generated spanning four formats: open-ended, closed-ended, single-choice, and multiple-choice. We further benchmark multiple general- and medical- large vision-language models using dual metrics for answer accuracy and lesion-level reasoning. The experiments demonstrate that incorporating lesion-level visual evidence consistently improves model performance and transparency, highlighting the necessity of explicit spatial grounding for reliable and explainable ophthalmic VQA.
  </details>

- **[StreamPro: Reinforcement Learning with Multi-Grained Rewards for Proactive Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16381)**  `arXiv:2605.16381`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ao Li, Zihan Xiao, Zihao Yue, Boshen Xu, Linli Yao, Jiaze Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive streaming video understanding requires models to continuously monitor evolving video streams and decide whether, when, and what to respond. However, training models with such proactive capabilities remains challenging. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) suffers from severe imbalance between silence and response signals, while reinforcement learning (RL) faces sparse response-level rewards and difficulties in optimizing interdependent response sequences. To address these challenges, we propose StreamPro, a two-stage training framework. We first introduce CB-Stream Loss to alleviate supervision imbalance during SFT. We then employ Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with two complementary rewards: a refined Response-Matching (RM) reward that provides denser feedback for individual response decisions, and a rubric-based Sequence-Quality (SQ) reward that encourages globally coherent response sequences. To support the two training stages, we construct StreamPro-SFT-63K and StreamPro-RL-3K, respectively. To address the limited task coverage of existing proactive benchmarks, we further introduce StreamPro-Bench, encompassing three task categories: Perceptual Understanding, Temporal Reasoning, and Anticipatory Assistance, along with StreamPro-F1 to evaluate response correctness, timing, and frequency. Experiments demonstrate that StreamPro substantially improves proactive streaming video understanding, achieving an average StreamPro-F1 of 32.8 on StreamPro-Bench, compared with 9.2 for the strongest baseline, while maintaining strong performance on real-time streaming tasks, reaching 79.8 on StreamingBench-RTVU.
  </details>

- **[Learning and Comparing Local Traces for Weakly Supervised Image Manipulation Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06222)**  `arXiv:2601.06222`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinghao Wang, Changtao Miao, Dianmo Sheng, Tao Gong, Qi Chu, Nenghai Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image manipulation localization identifies edited regions and provides spatial evidence for assessing image reliability, but fully supervised methods require costly pixel-level annotations. Weakly supervised methods reduce this cost using image-level labels, but classification can rely on a few salient cues without ensuring accurate or complete localization. This difficulty is pronounced when manipulation traces are weak and unevenly distributed and authentic and manipulated regions share similar appearance. We propose a CLIP-based framework that integrates Trace-Oriented Vision--Language Adaptation (TOVA) and Relation-Aware Trace Discrimination (RATD). TOVA adapts the visual encoder and uses global visual context to aggregate edge-weighted patch features into image-conditioned class prompts. The resulting class representations support image-level alignment and guide patch selection for Hierarchical Trace Contrastive Learning, providing local contrastive supervision across visual layers. RATD combines local context, spatial structure, and original-image details, then compares learned descriptors between appearance-matched regions. Bidirectional comparison messages are integrated with local features for dense localization. The two components share a visual encoder and are jointly trained using only image-level authenticity labels. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks demonstrate that our framework outperforms the evaluated weakly supervised baselines in image manipulation localization.
  </details>

- **[LoopNav: Benchmarking Spatial Consistency in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.22976)**  `arXiv:2505.22976`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kewei Lian, Shaofei Cai, Yitao Liang, Anji Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ability to simulate the world in a spatially consistent manner is a crucial requirement for effective world models. Such a model enables high-quality visual generation, and also ensures the reliability of world models for downstream tasks such as simulation and planning. It must not only retain long-horizon observational information, but also enables the construction of explicit or implicit internal spatial representations. However, existing datasets do not explicitly enforce spatial consistency constraints, limiting both the ability to systematically evaluate this capability and to learn it through data-driven approaches. Furthermore, most existing benchmarks primarily emphasize visual coherence or generation quality, neglecting the requirement of long-range spatial consistency. To bridge this gap, we propose LoopNav, a dataset and corresponding benchmark centered on loop-based navigation for evaluating spatial consistency. The dataset comprises 200 hours of loop-based navigation videos with actions, collected from diverse locations in the open-world environment of Minecraft. We further introduce a Scene Graph Consistency Score(SGCS) to quantify spatial consistency while remaining invariant to pixel-level variations. Dataset, benchmark, and code are open-sourced to support future research.
  </details>

- **[QuantStream3D: Geometry-Aligned Low-Bit KV Cache Quantization for Streaming Visual Geometry Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27901)**  `arXiv:2611.27901`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zunhai Su, Keyu Fan, Ruishan Guo, Zhongzhu Zhou, Jingzehua Xu, Bohan Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming visual geometry transformers retain historical key-value (KV) states as cross-view geometric memory, but growing caches constrain long-sequence inference. We systematically characterize KV quantization in streaming geometry and uncover two linked challenges: Nonuniform Geometric Sensitivity and Cache-Geometry Mismatch. Quantization sensitivity varies across precision choices and cache locations, while perturbed retrieval changes geometry-head inputs and propagates through subsequent predictions and KV writes. Guided by these findings, we propose QuantStream3D, to our knowledge the first dedicated KV quantization framework for streaming visual geometry transformers. Geometry-Guided Mixed-Precision KV Quantization protects sensitive cross-view evidence, while Geometry-Aligned Quantization-Aware Distillation jointly adapts transformer blocks and geometry heads on student-generated quantized history. Across five benchmarks and two backbones, QuantStream3D achieves near-lossless geometric accuracy in most settings with predominantly 2-bit KV caches, and many configurations surpass unquantized inference. Compared with LLM-oriented KV quantizers, it reduces TUM absolute trajectory error and NeuralRGBD completeness error by up to 57.4% and 69.7%, respectively. Integration with existing native cache-pruning methods further pushes compression while improving reconstruction quality on NeuralRGBD. Our optimized StreamVGGT backend achieves $5.05\times$ physical KV compression and 23.1% lower latency at batch size one. Under a fixed memory budget on the same GPU, it supports $5.4\times$ the maximum batch size and up to $1.67\times$ the peak throughput of unquantized inference. Code and pretrained checkpoints will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[ENC-QA: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Vision-Language Models on Electronic Navigational Charts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27858)**  `arXiv:2611.27858`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiyoon Pyo, Jacob Arndt, Yao-Yi Chiang, Dalton Lunga_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Nautical charts encode safety-critical maritime information through standardized symbols, attributes, text, and heterogeneous geometries. Interpreting these charts and assessing the navigational significance of successive chart updates requires recognizing nautical features, determining their spatial relationships, and applying navigational knowledge. Vision-language models (VLMs) offer a promising approach to nautical-chart interpretation, yet systematic evaluation of their fine-grained nautical feature recognition, chart-based spatial reasoning, and application of navigational knowledge to assessing chart changes remains limited. We introduce ENC-QA, a diagnostic benchmark comprising 77,312 questions derived from 6,612 National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) Electronic Navigational Charts (ENCs). The benchmark evaluates four complementary capabilities: ENC Perception, Spatial Reasoning, Joint Perception and Spatial Reasoning, and Application. Of these, 8,227 questions form a progressive subset that decomposes selected chart-change cases into linked questions progressing from change detection to criticality assessment, enabling case-level analysis of how models use perceptual and spatial evidence to determine the navigational significance of chart changes. Evaluations of 10 open-weight VLMs reveal substantial limitations in fine-grained nautical feature recognition and feature-grounded spatial reasoning. Controlled evidence interventions show that relevant perceptual and spatial information improves change-criticality accuracy. Our findings establish ENC-QA as a diagnostic resource for nautical-chart understanding and safety-critical navigational decisions. We will release the benchmark and evaluation code upon publication.
  </details>

- **[When Does Looking Closer Pay Off? Simple Foveated ViTs for Fine-Grained Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27788)**  `arXiv:2611.27788`  `cs.CV`  
  _Boris Kriuk, Mohamed Sobhy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-grained recognition benefits from high resolution, but not every image region needs it. We study a foveated Vision Transformer (FoVT) that uses last-block class attention from a 224-pixel glance to select regions for refinement. Fine patches from the 448-pixel image replace the selected coarse cells, and the same backbone classifies the mixed-resolution input. We evaluate FoVT on five benchmarks with two seeds and 26 configurations per dataset. Attention beats random selection on all five benchmarks, by up to 2.45 percentage points overall and 4.18 points on the smallest objects. At equal transformer cost, Holm-corrected tests detect no FoVT deficit relative to a port of the official CF-ViT, but no dataset shows a corrected FoVT advantage in both seeds. FoVT requires no feature reuse, distillation loss, or selection schedule. Reusing coarse features shows no significant benefit and costs 1 to 3 points when the reuse module is trained at the head learning rate. Skipping refinement for the most confident 19 to 24 percent of images leaves accuracy unchanged to two decimal places. At the transformer cost of uniform 336-pixel inference, gated FoVT improves accuracy on four benchmarks, with the largest gains on NABirds and FGVC-Aircraft and no significant gain on Stanford Dogs. Foveation primarily saves computation: relative to a uniform 448-pixel model trained with the same auxiliary glance loss, ungated FoVT uses 44 percent fewer transformer operations, with no significant accuracy difference on four benchmarks and a 0.53-point deficit on FGVC-Aircraft. Wall-clock savings are smaller than the reduction in transformer operations.
  </details>

- **[SUPERVISE: A Unified Framework for Standardized and Reproducible Superpixel Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27753)**  `arXiv:2611.27753`  `cs.CV`  
  _Julien Walther, Rémi Giraud, Michaël Clément_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image segmentation into superpixels is a widely used technique in computer vision, with a large body of work developed over the years. Yet, its evaluation remains poorly standardized, as methods are compared under heterogeneous protocols, across inconsistent scale ranges, and with redundant or misaligned metric subsets. In this work, we introduce SUPERVISE (SUperpixel PERformance VISualization and Evaluation), a unified and reproducible benchmark that addresses these longstanding inconsistencies. Built on a scale-normalized protocol based on interpolation over the actual number of generated superpixels, SUPERVISE enables fair and consistent cross-method comparisons. We leverage this standardized framework to conduct the first large-scale statistical analysis of 20 evaluation metrics across 7 datasets and 31 methods, revealing strong inter-metric correlations and significant redundancy that challenge common evaluation practices. These findings provide empirical support for reporting a compact set of representative metrics. SUPERVISE is released as a lightweight, modular framework built on precomputed shared label maps, making large-scale reproducible evaluation accessible to the community. All code, label maps, and evaluation results are publicly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Sketch2TikZ: Multi-Reward Reinforcement Learning for Converting Hand-Drawn Sketches to Executable TikZ](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27620)**  `arXiv:2611.27620`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shubham Paliwal, Arushi Jain, Monika Sharma, Manasi Patwardhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatic digitization of hand-drawn sketches into editable formats is crucial for modern document workflows and LaTeX integration. Converting sketches into executable TikZ code provides a structured representation of diagrams, but remains challenging due to noisy and ambiguous inputs, which hinder reliable interpretation and limit the effectiveness of token-level supervision for ensuring global diagram validity. To address these challenges, we propose Sketch2TikZ, a reinforcement learning framework that optimizes sketch-to-code generation via a sequential multi-reward strategy, combining Code-Structure ($\mathcal{R}_{cs}$), OCR ($\mathcal{R}_{ocr}$), CleanDIFT ($\mathcal{R}_{cd}$), and Self-Similarity ($\mathcal{R}_{ss}$) rewards to enforce textual, visual and structural correctness. We evaluate Sketch2TikZ on the SkeTikZ and FlowChart-3B benchmarks using metrics including compilation success rate (CSR), image similarity, code similarity, and character similarity. Our method achieves near-perfect compilation success and improves visual and textual alignment over strong open-source baselines, while remaining competitive with proprietary GPT-based vision-language models. Extensive ablations demonstrate that curriculum-based reward scheduling is critical for reducing reward interference, stabilizing training and mitigating proxy over-optimization. Overall, our results highlight the effectiveness of reference-aware, multi-objective reinforcement learning for structured diagram generation from noisy sketches.
  </details>

- **[Leakage-Free Is Not Confounder-Free: What a Patient-Level Split Cannot Certify About a Medical Imaging Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27585)**  `arXiv:2611.27585`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ignazio Gallo, Mattia Gatti, Leonardo Sgroi, Darwin Castillo, Yuliana Jiménez-Gaona, Nicola Landro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patient-level splitting is the standard safeguard against subject leakage in medical imaging evaluation, but it cannot remove a confounder that every fold inherits: an acquisition variable aligned with the diagnosis across the whole cohort. We present a five-question audit of a candidate confounder: how well it predicts the diagnosis, whether it can be decoded from the images, whether diagnostic scores depend on it, whether the image adds predictive value beyond it and how much discrimination survives once it is controlled. We apply it to DMR-IR, the public dataset on which most machine-learning results in breast thermography rest. On 236 patients under a strict patient-disjoint protocol, the acquisition date alone predicts malignancy at AUROC 0.980, above the best of nine imaging arms (0.949), because benign and malignant patients were recruited in different years. The acquisition year is decodable from the pixels among patients who share a diagnosis, largely through a global thermal offset outside the breast, yet only one arm, the one pooling every frame of a visit, demonstrably carries it into its diagnostic score. No arm improves on the date on paired folds, and once the date is controlled, discrimination either collapses or survives only on comparisons the benchmark cannot attribute. The dataset contains only eight cross-label same-date pairs, from ten patients, which limits reliable evaluation within recorded acquisition-date groups. A second, independently acquired cohort, whose dominant channel is patient age, returns the same audit outcome from a different confounder. Mitigation may suppress such a confounder, but without cross-label acquisition support no evaluation on the benchmark can certify that what remains is pathology.
  </details>

- **[GameScrub: Learning HUD Removal from Real Gameplay with Recovered Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27547)**  `arXiv:2611.27547`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mohammad Areeb Qazi, Yiyan Hu, Yanan Wang, Xuezhi Liang, Xin Li, Fan Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gameplay videos are a rich source of experience for learning virtual-world dynamics, yet their frames contain heads-up displays (HUDs) that follow interface rules rather than scene dynamics. Removing HUDs would make this footage directly usable for learning world dynamics. However, training a removal model requires paired clips with and without HUDs, whereas real recordings contain only HUD-overlaid footage. Existing work synthesizes pairs by compositing HUD graphics onto HUD-free footage, requiring a separately curated clean corpus and producing HUDs that only approximate real ones. Models trained on such pairs leave 40 to 50% of real HUD elements untouched. We instead recover pseudo-clean targets directly from real recordings by inpainting HUD regions with a pretrained video diffusion model. Preventing HUD traces from leaking into recovered targets is challenging because latent video autoencoders mix information across mask boundaries. Our training-free recovery pipeline, GameScrub-Data, pre-fills HUD regions with flow-guided background estimates before encoding, excludes HUDs from scene conditioning, and confines restoration to masked regions through pixel-space compositing. The resulting pairs train GameScrub, a video diffusion removal model that handles diverse HUDs without inference-time masks or clean reference images. On the in-the-wild Game2World benchmark, GameScrub achieves an artifact-adjusted removal score of 71.95, compared with 57.86 for the strongest reference-free GameCleaner configuration, and removes 90.06% of annotated HUD elements versus 59.07%. These results demonstrate that real recordings with recovered targets can support effective HUD removal, advancing HUD-free gameplay data for world models and controllable video generation.
  </details>

- **[When Visually Rich Documents Meet Multi-Vector Retrieval for Region-level Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27542)**  `arXiv:2611.27542`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xu Yuan, Liangbo Ning, Yujuan Ding, Qing Li, Wenqi Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visually rich documents (VRDs) convey knowledge through textual, visual, and layout information that text-centric Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Text-RAG) struggles to fully exploit. Recent Vision-RAG approaches enable retrieval and generation directly from document images, retaining this multimodal information. However, earlier Vision-RAG methods commonly adopt coarse retrieval representations and full-page visual contexts, which can obscure local evidence and introduce substantial redundancy and computational overhead. To address these challenges, we propose MV-VisRAG, a region-level Vision-RAG framework that uses semantically complete regions for both document representation and visual augmentation. Specifically, MV-VisRAG first constructs these regions through a ContentAdaptive Regionalization module. It then encodes them into local region vectors and a page-level global vector using a VLM-based retriever trained through multimodal reconstruction pretraining and retrieval fine-tuning. With this compact multi-vector representation, MV-VisRAG performs late-interaction retrieval and estimates region contributions from the resulting matching scores. These contributions guide the selection of relevant regions, which are then rearranged into a compact visual context for generation. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate retrieval and generation gains over corresponding baselines, while region-level augmentation substantially reduces visual tokens and computational cost. Our code will be made publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AppCon-Bench: Perception and Misperception of Material Appearance in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27453)**  `arXiv:2611.27453`  `cs.CV`  
  _Santiago Jimenez-Navarro, Ana Serrano, Belen Masia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying the same material across changes in illumination or geometry is a critical ability for robust vision systems. Humans exhibit remarkable material appearance constancy, being able to recognize the intrinsic physical properties of an object's surface material across variations in extrinsic, confounding factors such as the lighting or the shape of such object. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) demonstrate strong performance across a wide range of semantic vision tasks, yet their ability to interpret subtle mid-level perceptual attributes remains less understood. We introduce AppCon-Bench, a benchmark comprising five tasks designed to assess material appearance constancy under controlled variations in geometry and illumination, and evaluate representative VLMs alongside human observers, investigating their ability to maintain consistent material judgments across viewing conditions and experimental contexts. After thorough analysis, we identify several key findings that reveal both similarities and systematic differences between human and VLM material appearance perception, highlighting the value of evaluating perceptual successes and failures beyond overall accuracy and informing areas of improvement for current VLMs.
  </details>

- **[Pose-based Co-speech Gesture Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27415)**  `arXiv:2611.27415`  `cs.CV`  
  _Frances Yung, Ninlawat Phuangschoke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Co-speech gesture detection identifies the spans in which a speaker gestures (e.g., beat or semantic gestures) in video. As a first step in gesture annotation, it is crucial for studying human communicative behaviour. Despite the availability of automatic tools and models, co-speech gesture detection remains underexplored, and its current state of the art is unclear: existing methods have been evaluated using different datasets, experimental setups, and metrics, making their performance difficult to compare. To address this gap, we benchmark existing detectors using the same cross-validation folds across three datasets (MULTISIMO, GESRes, and CABBS), with evaluation at the frame, gesture-span, and window levels. Our results show that previously reported performance does not consistently transfer to this common evaluation setting. Performance varies substantially across datasets, particularly with gesture frequency, indicating that the task remains far from solved. We further propose a CTR-GCN + BiLSTM model that outperforms the previous best method, MPSL, on CABBS. Our results provide a more consistent basis for comparing existing approaches and highlight key challenges for future research on co-speech gesture detection.
  </details>

- **[What Stays in the Picture? From Redundant Instructions to Dependency-Aware Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27388)**  `arXiv:2611.27388`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zi-Jun Wang, Yu Lin, Zhilin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce complex redundant-instruction image editing, where a single request specifies multiple objects, their attributes, and spatial relations while revising or canceling earlier editing requirements. Direct editing can fail to recover this intent, while splitting the request into successive steps can preserve obsolete edits and visual errors. Our key insight is to resolve the revision history into a final editing target that jointly guides planning and verification, so that obsolete requests do not become visual edits. Based on this insight, CRIE-Agent learns to consolidate revisions into object-level final intent and executes the retained objects in reference-first blocks, establishing referenced objects before their dependents. Verification then guides complete re-execution from the source image, with conditional layout hints, preventing visual errors from failed attempts from being carried into subsequent ones. We also construct CRIE-S, a benchmark containing 10,000 complex and redundant editing instructions with exact final intent annotations. Across five image-editing backends on CRIE-S, CRIE-Agent raises complete editing success from 0% under direct editing to 27.5--50.0%.
  </details>

- **[Borrow the Style, Follow the Prompt: Progressive Reference-Guided Stylization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27360)**  `arXiv:2611.27360`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Lin, Zi-Jun Wang, Zhilin Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-guided text-to-image stylization transfers visual appearance to content specified by text, but the reference subjects and backgrounds can interfere with what the generated image depicts. Suppressing this unwanted information can also discard transferable texture and material cues, creating a tension between content control and faithful preservation of the reference style. Accordingly, we propose ProStyle, which learns a content-suppressed style prior through cross-content supervision on same-style pairs with different subjects and backgrounds and injects it with weak conditioning at auxiliary layers to strengthen style expression. Building on this prior, a complementary pathway learns residual corrections from full reference patch features with the content-suppressed style pathway frozen, before both pathways undergo joint refinement under the same objective. For cross-content learning and evaluation, we introduce ReConStyle, which provides 134K training pairs, two test settings, and object-level measures of target-content fidelity and reference-object overlap alongside style similarity and text alignment. ProStyle outperforms all compared SD1.5-based baselines and achieves the best average rank of 1.86 across four automatic and three human-evaluation criteria on ReConStyle-Full. In the user study, it substantially outperforms the baselines in text alignment and combined suitability, with selection rates of 84.36% and 50.90%, respectively. On the external Style-Rank benchmark, it also achieves the highest CLIP-Text and ImageReward scores.
  </details>

- **[ReSA: Preserving Reference-Specified Foreground in In-Context Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27341)**  `arXiv:2611.27341`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dezhao Su, Bingxu Hou, Jingdi Lei, Lin Qian, Likai Wang, Yunyun Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context segmentation uses annotated references to specify the target and its extent in a query image. Accurately transferring this reference-specified foreground requires recovering target regions while suppressing irrelevant predictions. We propose ReSA, a reference-guided framework that unifies query adaptation and mask selection through a shared reference kernel. ReSA leverages foreground and background reference prototypes to constrain prediction changes during adaptation and guide foreground selection through reference-weighted reconstruction. This shared guidance promotes consistent use of reference information across prediction refinement and mask formation. ReSA further selects among adapted predictions and aligns boundary refinement with the selected foreground threshold. We establish a theoretical bound connecting reference-response changes during adaptation and soft reconstruction. With a frozen visual encoder and lightweight test-time adaptation, ReSA requires no offline task training. Ablation studies demonstrate improved foreground retention and effective suppression of false-positive regions, while qualitative results show more complete masks with fewer isolated artifacts. Across ten object, part, and cross-domain segmentation benchmarks, ReSA achieves the highest average mIoU among the evaluated methods, reaching 62.6% in 1-shot and 71.8% in 5-shot.
  </details>

- **[Zing-1.0: A Playable World System Built on Controllable Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27336)**  `arXiv:2611.27336`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenbo Zhang, Guoli Zhang, Xuewen Yu, Xueliang Yang, Jiangqiao Yan, Teng Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Zing-1.0, a Playable World System built on Controllable Rendering. An executable engine resolves player actions under the world's rules and produces proxy video from the updated state. A streaming video model synthesizes the player's view from this proxy, and the runtime delivers it to inform subsequent actions. (1) Principle and system. We formulate Controllable Rendering and define the interfaces among world construction, world execution, observation synthesis, and the interaction runtime. To our knowledge, this is the first implementation-independent specification of a Playable World System. (2) AI-native engine. WorldKit provides structured authoring tools and reusable character, camera, and control components for coding agents to construct executable proxy worlds. It supports extensible interactions and precise geometric motion control while separating executable behavior from final visual appearance. (3) Streaming renderer. We adapt MiniMax-H3 for block-causal autoregressive generation and incremental decoding. Proxy video constrains structure and motion, while text and separate scene and subject references specify appearance. We train the generator on diverse tasks and distill it to generate each block in a few denoising steps. The renderer leads the compared streaming editors on OpenVE (3.90) and LongV2VBench (3.44). On StreamEval, Zing-1.0 scores 57.86 on instruction editing and 54.06 on reference editing, exceeding the published baselines on both tracks. (4) Interaction infrastructure. We build an Interaction Runtime that routes player actions to WorldKit while supporting in-play prompt and reference updates. Cross-block joint execution and sparse attention with compressed K/V reduce inference overhead. With the DiT on a single H200 and encoding and decoding on one H100 each, the serving system achieves a steady-state throughput of 23.82 FPS at $832\times480$.
  </details>

- **[SIR-Agent: Skill-Augmented Image Restoration Agent with Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27285)**  `arXiv:2611.27285`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yijian Wang, Qingsen Yan, Weiyu Zhou, Mingde Zhang, Axi Niu, Haokui Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents for Image Restoration (IR) aim to leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to autonomously perceive image degradation and invoke IR tools. However, existing IR agents typically rely on fine-tuning MLLM parameters to adapt to new IR tasks, leading to catastrophic forgetting of IR knowledge. To address this limitation, we propose a skill-augmented IR agent, dubbed SIR-Agent, that maintains IR knowledge in a scalable skill library and uses Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) to facilitate efficient skill-library evolution. Specifically, we design a Chain Feedback-driven Skill Revision (CFSR) to evolve the skill library. The CFSR analyzes restoration chains to identify and revise weak operations in existing skills. The compatibility of a revised skill with known tasks decides if it replaces the original skill or is appended independently. Furthermore, to advance CFSR's skill-library evolution, we present an RSI-driven skill meta-learning mechanism, which identifies CFSR's inefficient operations and autonomously refines its revision strategy. Extensive experiments across 14 IR benchmarks, covering 6 single-degradation and 8 mixed-degradation scenarios, demonstrate SIR-Agent's superior IR performance. Further evaluation on 7 unseen tasks validates SIR-Agent's generalization to new IR tasks.
  </details>

- **[PP-CFG: Polarity-Preserving Guidance for Controllable Few-Step Image and Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27266)**  `arXiv:2611.27266`  `cs.CV`  
  _Junyi He, Xu Peng, Chengming Xu, Sixiao Zheng, Donghao Luo, Chao Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step diffusion distillation reduces sampling cost but often weakens adjustable classifier-free guidance (CFG) and runtime negative prompting. We introduce PP-CFG, an architecture that keeps positive and negative text conditions separate and combines their attention responses with opposite signs within a shared visual backbone. Its Scale-Time Polarity Mixer refines each condition using the guidance scale and denoising timestep, allowing prompt influence to vary throughout generation. The student retains a continuous scale input and selectable negative prompts with one visual backbone pass per step. We evaluate PP-CFG on Wan2.1 for video generation with distribution matching distillation (DMD), and on Qwen-Image with both latent consistency model (LCM) distillation and DMD. PP-CFG achieves 81.46 VBench in four steps and lower video teacher-student distances across guidance scales than step-distilled DMD. On NegElement-1K, our benchmark of 1,000 matched image and video prompt cases, it achieves higher target-removal rates than the corresponding teachers. These experiments support the method's generality across attention architectures, generation modalities, and distillation objectives while retaining competitive generation quality.
  </details>

- **[WINSO: A Large-Size Very-High-Resolution Remote Sensing Dataset for Wind Turbine Detection and Solar Panel Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27241)**  `arXiv:2611.27241`  `cs.CV`  
  _Di Wang, Ke Li, Zhaokun Li, Weiping Ni, Junzheng Wu, Yahu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wind and solar energy are central to the global transition toward net-zero emissions and sustainable development. Accurate spatial inventories of wind and solar facilities are essential for future energy planning. Satellite remote sensing provides a practical and efficient means to acquire such geospatial data at scale. However, reliable facility mapping from large-size satellite images remains constrained by the scarcity of carefully annotated datasets and the lack of systematic evaluation protocols. To address these limitations, we introduce \textbf{WINSO}, a large-scale wind and solar benchmark based on very-high-resolution (VHR) satellite imagery with ground sampling distances (GSDs) of 0.5--0.7\,m. WINSO comprises 10,962 large-size images sampled from diverse geographic locations across the Asia-Pacific region, covering at least 71,600\,$\text{km}^2$ and totaling 286.36 gigapixels, with individual image dimensions reaching 44,032$\times$62,208 pixels. Through manual annotation and expert verification, a total of 166,473 instances are labeled using horizontal bounding boxes (HBBs) for wind turbines and polygon masks for solar panels. To build baselines for clean-energy facility mapping, we evaluate state-of-the-art detection and segmentation models, specialized ultra-high-resolution (UHR) architectures, and vision-language models across different inference strategies. Furthermore, cross-dataset evaluations on two external public datasets illustrate the strong generalization capability of the WINSO. WINSO establishes a standardized benchmark to advance automated clean-energy mapping, paving the way for wide-area monitoring of the clean-energy transition.
  </details>

- **[Real-Anchored Complementary Evidence for Generalizable AI-Generated Image Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27161)**  `arXiv:2611.27161`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenpeng Mu, Tanfeng Sun, Zhenyu Shao, Qiang Xu, Xinghao Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evolving generative models challenge detector generalization, as methods trained on generated examples may rely on generator-specific cues. By contrast, authentic photographs share regularities rooted in physical image formation and acquisition, providing a common reference beyond individual generators. Building on these regularities, we propose Real-Anchored Complementary Evidence (RACE), which learns complementary global and local detection evidence using only authentic training images. First, the global pathway combines frozen and learned representations to assess proximity to authentic references. Next, it incorporates responses to controlled perturbations, complementing static compatibility with representation sensitivity. The local pathway then reconstructs complete trace descriptors, using intermediate features and signed perturbation responses as context while restricting detection evidence to static terminal-residual reconstruction errors. Finally, authentic-image statistics align the global and local scores for fusion, and an independent authentic set calibrates a decision threshold held fixed across test generators. Across six benchmarks, RACE achieves the highest average AUROC of 96.59% among the compared methods, reaching 97.18% on five out-of-domain benchmarks. Together with robustness to image perturbations and variation in authentic-image sources, these results support authentic-image regularities as a transferable basis for detection across diverse generators and image conditions.
  </details>

- **[TrajRealm: A Large-Scale Point Tracking Dataset with Diverse Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27136)**  `arXiv:2611.27136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ni Yang, Weiguang Zhao, Jiafei Cao, Yankai Jiang, Haoyu Guo, Tao Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose point tracking requires large-scale training data with diverse dynamics, yet existing datasets struggle to combine rich dynamic content, accurate scene geometry, and sufficient scale.
