We introduce TrajRealm, the first public synthetic point-tracking dataset to reach the ten-million-frame scale, constructed from games and embodied simulations. TrajRealm uses the rich environments and motion systems of modern games to capture diverse targets, activities, and interactions across diverse worlds, while embodied simulations add robot manipulation and indoor navigation data. The dataset provides RGB observations, depth, camera parameters, and persistent 2D and 3D point trajectories. These annotations provide precise, geometrically consistent supervision across foreground objects and static environmental surfaces. We also build TrajRealm-Bench with eight subcategories under Interaction and Locomotion to evaluate tracking across diverse scenes. Benchmark evaluations reveal the strengths and limitations of existing trackers in handling complex motion and maintaining long-term point correspondences. Training on TrajRealm improves the performance of existing tracking methods on synthetic and real-world benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[RemoteEvolver: Expanding Earth Observation Capabilities through Agent Harness Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27110)**  `arXiv:2611.27110`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shengxiang Xu, Liang Yao, Shuyi Chen, Yishu Wang, Shimin Di, Fan Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We are interested in developing a continually self-improving remote sensing agentic system. It should identify and address its capability gaps, retaining the resulting improvements to enhance performance on subsequent tasks. However, existing systems typically rely on fixed toolsets and confine their experience accumulation to re-orchestrating predefined capabilities, constraining both capability expansion and the transfer of improvements across tasks. As the infrastructure for agentic systems, the harness manages tools and workflow knowledge as editable, persistent resources outside the base model. These resources can therefore be updated and reused across tasks without additional post-training. Based on these observations, we propose RemoteEvolver, an external harness for remote sensing agents that drives tool and workflow evolution through execution feedback. With the base model parameters fixed, RemoteEvolver acquires missing specialized tools on demand and distills verified successful execution experience from individual tasks into agent skills that can be reused across tasks. The acquired tools and accumulated skills jointly support planning and execution on subsequent tasks. Experiments on Earth-Bench and OpenEarthAgent-Bench show that RemoteEvolver achieves average accuracy gains of 8.18 and 23.86 percentage points over its initial state when tools are fully available and partially missing. Under the same settings, it also outperforms the state-of-the-art methods by an average of 6.77 and 17.10 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Hindsight Forcing: Learning Better History from the Future in Autoregressive Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27074)**  `arXiv:2611.27074`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haonan An, Yihang Tao, Yu Guo, Renlong Chen, Jiasheng Zhang, Yuguang Fang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-step autoregressive video diffusion enables real-time streaming generation, with each generated chunk serving as historical context for subsequent generation. To avoid the memory explosion of backpropagation through time, previous self-rollout methods typically truncate cross-chunk gradients or detach historical latents. As a result, earlier denoising computations receive no downstream feedback through the generated history on how to synthesize frames that facilitate subsequent generation, leading to visual drift and structural collapse as rollouts extend. To address this limitation, we propose Hindsight Forcing, which allows historical chunks to learn directly from future outcomes by decoupling forward generation from backward credit assignment via a latent straight-through bridge. In the forward pass, each chunk completes a full multi-step rollout to update the KV cache. In the backward pass, the bridge routes gradients from future losses back to a single selected denoising step per historical chunk, training both the context writer and historical denoiser on shared parameters without retaining the full trajectory graph. Remarkably, Hindsight Forcing requires no additional model parameters, architecture modifications, or separate trajectory replay. Extensive experiments on VBench demonstrate that Hindsight Forcing outperforms existing baselines in overall visual quality and dynamic motion, while ablation studies validate the benefit of supervising historical denoising. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[$R^2$: Recognizing and Reducing Redundant Visual Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27070)**  `arXiv:2611.27070`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yilin Zhao, Jinyu Wang, Wenming Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies have shown that visual tools can improve multimodal reasoning. However, the effects of tools vary substantially across samples, and benchmark accuracy alone does not reveal whether tool interactions are beneficial or redundant. We present $R^2$, a framework that recognizes and reduces redundant visual tool use in vision-language models. We first introduce a sample-level Category Analysis of Tool Utility (CAT), which categorizes samples according to whether a model can benefit from tools. This analysis reveals that benchmark scores may obscure a model's tool-use ability. Across seven benchmarks, samples benefiting from tools account for at most 12.44% of each benchmark, while the majority gain little from tool interaction or are even harmed by it. To assess individual calls, we propose the Visual Feedback Dependency (VFD) metric, which compares the likelihood of the same subsequent reasoning under actual and alternative visual feedback without an external evaluator or trajectory regeneration. Redundancy-Aware GRPO (RA-GRPO) combines this dependency signal with a category-conditioned base call cost to assign relative efficiency advantages among correct trajectories, supplementing outcome-based supervision. Across the seven benchmarks, RA-GRPO reduces the mean number of tool calls by 12.39% relative to Vanilla GRPO at comparable benchmark accuracy. Evaluated by tool-utility category, RA-GRPO improves category macro-average accuracy over Vanilla GRPO by 3.35 percentage points.
  </details>

- **[ViewDreamer: A Robot World Model beyond Fixed Camera Setups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26981)**  `arXiv:2611.26981`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ye Mao, Jinhui Xiong, Egor Larionov, Shiyang Lu, Krystian Mikolajczyk, Wei Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models are increasingly important for robot learning, yet most generate each camera view separately or tile all views in a fixed layout. Robot demonstrations, however, are captured by synchronized cameras whose number, placement, and order vary across embodiments. We present ViewDreamer, a single world model that handles variable sets of cameras by disentangling per-view generation from cross-view interaction. Each view follows the generation path of a pretrained single-view model, and views interact only through shared slot tokens. We stabilize multi-view training with a curriculum that gradually increases the number of views. Cross-View Slot Agreement uses visual foundation models to group geometrically and semantically similar regions across views into shared slots. On RoboView-Bench, which covers one to four cameras, ViewDreamer achieves the best generation quality among the models evaluated and improves cross-view consistency over independent per-view generation. These gains carry over to our world-action model (WAM) and action-conditioned world model (AC-WM). Our WAM outperforms the FastWAM-Joint baseline on LIBERO-Plus, RoboTwin 2.0, and RoboDojo, and on a real Franka robot. Both variants improve out-of-domain generalization to various camera settings and scenes.
  </details>

- **[TouchBench: Can Vision-Language Models Feel Invisible Physical Properties?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26883)**  `arXiv:2611.26883`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziyao Zeng, Ziyi Kou, Chuan Qin, Mia Huang, Roger Boldu, Zhiwen Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used for embodied perception and reasoning, yet their physical understanding is largely evaluated on questions answerable from images. Many properties critical to manipulation, such as an opaque bottle's fill level or an object's compliance, may only be revealed through touch. We introduce TouchBench, an egocentric vision-language-tactile benchmark that distinguishes visually answerable questions (Tier A) from those involving properties hidden from the camera (Tier B). It pairs head-mounted video with synchronized 137-channel whole-hand tactile sensing, comprising 900 clips from five participants across 11 tasks, with 900 absolute and 4,102 relative questions. Four state-of-the-art VLMs average 66.4% accuracy on Tier A relative questions but only 49.8% on Tier B, near chance (50%). To bridge this gap, we propose Tactile-as-Prompt (TaP), which presents 34 interpretable tactile statistics as text, enabling VLMs to reason with touch without a learned tactile encoder. TaP raises zero-shot Tier B relative accuracy to 62.9% and reaches 73.1% after leave-one-user-out fine-tuning, outperforming video alone (67.4%) and a learned tactile-token encoder (67.3%). The gains extend to unseen objects and users. On a separate 20-hour in-the-wild corpus, tactile text improves fine-tuned contact-question accuracy from 67.1% to 75.5%. These results highlight the limits of vision-only physical reasoning and establish language as an effective interface for tactile perception.
  </details>

- **[Effectiveness of Synthetic Defects for Anomaly Detection in Industrial Small Load Carriers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26868)**  `arXiv:2611.26868`  `cs.CV`  
  _Vincent Wahyudi, Kuniko Paxton, Amila Akagić, Martin Storath, Dhavalkumar Thakker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent industrial anomaly detection methods use procedural noise to generate synthetic training defects. Their effectiveness in real-world settings and the impact of different noise generation methods have not been sufficiently investigated. We evaluate the effectiveness of training using synthetic defects for both supervised and unsupervised methods, the impact of different noise generation methods, and the differences in detection performance between benchmark datasets and a real-world small load carrier (SLC) dataset. Trained on synthetic defects only, a supervised classifier reaches 0.65 AUROC on real SLC defects, while three unsupervised methods using the same kind of noise (Perlin) reach 0.87--0.90. Except for SuperSimpleNet, which fails with the foreground-confined fractal Brownian motion (fBm) recipe, models trained with the custom recipe or with nine other noise types do not differ reliably from those trained with Perlin noise. Finally, results with synthetic defects on benchmark datasets do not carry over to the SLC dataset, possibly because the synthetic defects differ too much from the real ones (a larger angle in feature space). These results call for caution when training models on synthetic defect images.
  </details>

- **[Spatial Query Execution as Verifiable Rewards for Multi-step Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26787)**  `arXiv:2611.26787`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiyoon Pyo, Dongwon Jung, Zekun Li, Nayeon Kim, Muhao Chen, Yao-Yi Chiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) remain unreliable on spatial reasoning problems that require composing grounded relations across multiple objects. Yet, scaling reasoning supervision is challenging since human-annotated visual datasets often rely on fixed relational annotations and coarse object localization, making it difficult to obtain precise intermediate reasoning states across diverse spatial configurations and complexities. We introduce FARON (Fully Automated Reasoning Over spatial relatioNs), a framework that executes spatial queries to provide both execution-grounded reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards. Rather than relying on existing annotated data, FARON constructs synthetic 2D and 3D scenes with explicit geometries, enabling configurable object arrangements, spatial relations, and multi-step compositions. Deterministic query execution yields intermediate sets and final answers, providing precise supervision without manual relation annotation. Our pipeline converts intermediate states into natural-language reasoning traces for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while the execution-derived answers provide verifiable rewards for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Controlled experiments show that trace-based SFT improves multi-step spatial reasoning over final-answer supervision and that subsequent group relative policy optimization (GRPO) provides further gains. The resulting models also demonstrate transfer across external spatial reasoning benchmarks and multiple LVLM backbones without benchmark-specific fine-tuning. These results show the potential of using spatial query execution as a unified, flexible source of reasoning supervision and verifiable outcome rewards for multimodal spatial reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Tracktail: Multi-View 3D Point Tracking without Depth Input](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26779)**  `arXiv:2611.26779`  `cs.CV`  
  _Katie L Rupp, Jonathan Wong, Sharmishtaa Seshamani, Ana Mafalda Vicente, Lili Karashchuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Tracktail, a novel architecture designed to efficiently track query points in 3D from multiple calibrated views without any depth input. Our architecture separates the task as a per-camera video encoding of features and a per-query decoder. Notably, camera cross-attention is only performed across queries in the decoder, allowing for efficient inference (7 FPS and less than 4 GB memory for 10 views and 100 query points). We further introduce a per-camera scene scale computed from camera geometry, which allows us to normalize inputs and predictions appropriately across a vast variety of dataset scales. We train on a synthetic Multi-View Kubric dataset as well as a novel Multi-View Point Odyssey benchmark dataset, which consists of synthetic renders of humans and animals across 499 frames. Tracktail achieves state-of-the-art results on the Panoptic Studio, DexYCB, and Multi-View Kubric benchmarks, outperforming methods that rely on estimated depth. Tracktail also demonstrates a strong ability to maintain accurate tracking over longer ranges compared to existing methods. Our code, data, and weights are publicly available:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ConRef: Auditing Reference Reliability in 2D/3D Registration Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26758)**  `arXiv:2611.26758`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jianfeng Yue, Feng Li, Tianyu Song, Julius Watrinet, Ulrich Eck, Nassir Navab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: Accurate 2D/3D registration is crucial for image-guided orthopedic interventions, enabling the alignment of preoperative 3D anatomy with intraoperative X-ray observations for surgical navigation. Public benchmarks have facilitated the development and evaluation of increasingly accurate registration methods. However, as registration errors approach the uncertainty of benchmark reference poses, measured performance may be limited by reference inaccuracies rather than algorithmic errors alone. Methods: We propose Consensus Reference (ConRef), a multi-view framework for auditing reference poses in existing X-ray/computed tomography (CT) registration benchmarks. By jointly exploiting multiple X-ray observations, ConRef estimates an additional reference pose, quantifies its uncertainty, and identifies potential X-ray outliers. We validate the framework using independent anatomical evidence and a newly acquired X-ray/cone-beam CT (CBCT) phantom dataset with bead-based reference poses. Results: On our BeadPhant dataset, ConRef differed from independent bead-based references by 0.08-0.12 mm, while discrepancies with original references reached 2.06 mm on the Femur dataset. It also flagged 22 DeepFluoro X-rays as potential outliers. On Femur F5, reference choice changed the submillimeter success conclusions of the same registrations. Conclusion: Our findings demonstrate that reference discrepancies can influence the evaluation of high-accuracy 2D/3D registration methods. By providing an additional, independently validated reference and identifying potential inconsistencies, ConRef enables systematic auditing of existing benchmarks and supports more reliable evaluation of registration error.
  </details>

- **[SCATR-ITS: a scalable transformer for large-area individual tree segmentation from LiDAR point clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26715)**  `arXiv:2611.26715`  `cs.CV`  
  _Lassi Ruoppa, Verneri Seppänen, Antero Kukko, Juha Hyyppä_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Individual tree segmentation (ITS) from LiDAR point clouds is one of the most widely studied segmentation tasks in forestry. Although deep learning methods define the state of the art on the majority of ITS benchmarks, their high computational complexity and long runtime currently prevent their adoption in large-scale operational workflows. In this work, we present SCATR-ITS, a scalable transformer model specifically designed for efficient ITS over large geographical areas. Based on the ForestFormer3D framework, SCATR-ITS transitions to superpoint-level processing to accelerate inference, with the superpoints constructed from learned embeddings. The architecture incorporates size-aware superpoint-level postprocessing, a dynamic query count module to handle high variation in forest structure, and a maximal input stride designed to minimize prediction redundancy. SCATR-ITS achieves accuracy on par with the state of the art on the FGI-EMIT and FOR-InstanceV2 datasets with an up to 8.5$\times$ higher inference speed. In addition, SCATR-ITS has a significantly lower GPU VRAM footprint during both training and inference, and exhibits excellent zero-shot generalization across multiple datasets. To demonstrate large-area ITS capabilities, SCATR-ITS is applied to a 142 km$^2$ region of Finnish national laser scanning data where it achieves an average throughput of 13.8 min/km$^2$, $\sim$18.6$\times$ faster than ForestFormer3D and approaching the speed of the unsupervised watershed algorithm, which it vastly outperforms in accuracy and segment quality. Overall, SCATR-ITS delivers state-of-the-art accuracy at speeds where operational-scale adoption becomes viable, paving the way toward countrywide deployment. The source code for SCATR-ITS and pretrained weights are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Carry the Fine Print: Budget-Aware Visual Token Compression for Document OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25506)**  `arXiv:2611.25506`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yixuan Huang, ShengFan Zhang, Jiawei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token compression often treats salience or similarity as a proxy for transcription importance. Complete document OCR breaks this assumption: small symbols and repeated entries can be indispensable to the transcript despite being visually inconspicuous. We present Select and Compensate, a two-stage framework built on a simple idea: choosing where to retain a token need not determine all the evidence it carries. Selection combines adaptive encoding resolution with global importance and local contrast to choose which tokens to retain as anchors. Compensation enriches these anchors with nearby discarded content and its relative position, without adding tokens. The OCR backbone remains frozen, and native coordinates and structural tokens are preserved. We evaluate complete page transcription across five backbones on OmniDocBench and document reconstruction on MPDocBench. With half the original visual tokens, our input-adapted configuration reaches 95.42 OmniDocBench Overall on Ovis versus 96.09 for the uncompressed model, and paired evaluations show gains over simply reducing the input resolution on both benchmarks. With the original input grid, our method stays within 0.17 points of the uncompressed model at 75% retention and exceeds a learned global selector by 4.70 points at 25%. On a fixed 200-page Ovis workload at the same 50% visual-token budget, only 3 of its outputs hit the length limit, versus 30-34 for the evaluated RTPrune, DivPrune, and HiPrune adaptations, and it finishes the batch 35-40% sooner. Code will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Fold the Loop: Decoding-Time KV Memory for Looped Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25369)**  `arXiv:2611.25369`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kun Zhou, Wenmian Yang, Zhensheng Wang, Yanzeng Li, Weijia Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen visual reasoning by repeatedly applying weight-tied modules. However, their decoding memory grows with recurrent depth and limits the image size and batch size that fit in a fixed memory budget. Each recurrent step produces its own key-value (KV) states, so the cache holds several copies of the same image. We find that, after an exact prefill, earlier steps still need access to visual information but can reuse the final visual KV within the same module. We propose FoldCache, which compresses visual KV along the recurrent axis while preserving the full prefill computation. FoldCache partitions recurrent steps within each module into groups and retains the final visual KV of each group for its members to share during decoding. Sharing stays within module boundaries, while question and answer KV remain separate for each step. The grouping granularity controls the degree of compression, and folding can be combined with token eviction and quantization to further reduce memory use. On LoopVL, FoldCache approximately halves prefix KV memory while matching full-cache accuracy on five open-ended benchmarks. These memory savings support batch sizes up to 1.7 times those of a full cache under the same GPU memory budget, and up to 2.6 times with the more aggressive FoldCache-Lite.
  </details>

- **[SCRiD: Spatial and Contextual References for Generalizable AI-Generated Video Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24524)**  `arXiv:2611.24524`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Tan, Zekai Zuo, Boyang Chen, Jinghui Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizable AI-generated video detection remains challenging because natural motion and content variations can resemble synthesis artifacts, making isolated video discrepancies unreliable indicators of authenticity. To address this challenge, we propose Spatial and Contextual Reference-informed Detection (SCRiD), a framework that interprets video variations through complementary spatial and contextual references. Specifically, we introduce Matched-Coordinate Residual Response (MCR) to characterize temporal residual associations under motion and coordinate references with matched subpixel sampling conditions. We further develop Real-Conditioned Score Adaptation (RCSA), which learns a content-conditioned denoising reference from authentic videos and probes its responses across multiple noise scales and quality views to guide feature adaptation. To integrate these complementary sources of evidence, we design Reversible Evidence Exchange (REE) to enable information exchange through reversible symplectic updates. We train SCRiD using synthetic videos from only 4 generators and evaluate it across 88 generator or task subsets spanning 6 benchmarks. Our method achieves average ACC and AP of 72.72% and 74.23%, respectively, outperforming the strongest baseline by 3.24 and 0.81 percentage points. Ablation and robustness experiments further validate the effectiveness of the proposed components and the stability of SCRiD under common post-processing operations.
  </details>

- **[Do VLMs Need to Keep Looking at Long Videos? Write It Down, Then Look Away](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24259)**  `arXiv:2611.24259`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Li, Zhitao Gao, Lingling Zhang, Heng Wang, Chengqian Ma, WenHao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) reason about long videos over hundreds to thousands of tokens. Every decoding step re-reads the key-value (KV) cache of the entire video. Must the model keep looking while it reasons? In LookAway, the model first writes timestamped notes on what the question needs and then puts the video away. Once the notes close, the inference engine hides the video's KV cache for the rest of the response. Across both hybrid and pure-attention VLMs, dependence on the video is concentrated early in the response. On Qwen3.6-27B, writing notes preserves accuracy better than free reasoning at the same withdrawal step. Replacing them with another video's notes harms accuracy, whereas keeping the video visible alongside the model's own notes adds no significant benefit. Without additional training, LookAway nearly matches full-video thinking accuracy at 0.12-0.13 times its modeled decode cost. To shorten this initial look, we fine-tune the model on its own correct LookAway responses. Reinforcement learning then rewards correct answers while penalizing KV reads. The trained model puts the video away after a median of 66-68 tokens. Across three held-out benchmarks, it writes more tokens than an identically trained full-video model yet reads only 27-35% as many KV positions at matched accuracy.
  </details>

- **[RoughCut-Bench: A Benchmark for Automatic Video Rough-Cut with Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24098)**  `arXiv:2611.24098`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruisi Zhao, You Li, Sihan Chen, Aosong Cheng, Chixiang Ma, Sen Pei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video rough-cut is a core stage in media production workflows, organizing raw media materials into an initial video cut. Despite recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) for video-related tasks, automatic rough-cut remains underexplored, without a comprehensive benchmark for evaluation or mature workflows for producing executable cut plans. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce RoughCut-Bench, which covers two practical settings: (1) a visually driven setting over video cases, covering highlight detection and selection, source-video ordering, and adherence to user requirements when provided; and (2) a speech-driven setting over automatic speech recognition (ASR) transcripts, covering filler removal, expression deduplication, and low-value content trimming. Across these settings, the benchmark contains 101 visually driven cases, including 831 raw videos with 1,476 highlight annotations. It also includes 84 speech-driven cases, each consisting of an ASR transcript with annotated cleanup spans. The evaluation compares cut plans with these annotations in both settings and additionally uses a human-aligned agentic evaluator to assess rendered-cut quality in the visual setting. To apply LVLMs to automatic rough-cut, we further propose agentic workflows that guide them to produce structured cut plans from input materials. Experiments show that current LVLMs remain limited in producing expert-aligned cut plans. Our benchmark, code, and prompts are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Autoregressive View Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23417)**  `arXiv:2611.23417`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jensen, Zhou, Samuel Rota Bulo, Denis Rozumny, Duncan Zauss, Peter Hedman, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Autoregressive View Model (AVM), which generates a scene as a stream of views without constructing an explicit 3D representation. Through a unified in-context interface, AVM conditions on images or videos to generate short clips along a requested camera path. Visual context grounds reconstruction and interpolation, while a learned prior enables extrapolation into unseen regions. AVM retrieves relevant views from inputs and generated history to form a bounded active context. We fine-tune a pretrained bidirectional video diffusion transformer with flow matching in an N-in-1-out formulation. We then tune a causal model on its own rollouts to improve photorealism, reduce denoising steps, and limit long-horizon drift. We scale training to approximately 8M scenes and video clips. We evaluate generation capacity and scene persistence across benchmarks for camera control, text control, visual quality, dynamics, sparse and 4D reconstruction, and long-horizon consistency. The results demonstrate that AVM adapts to diverse input and output settings through one in-context interface.
  </details>

- **[PCRG: Training-Free Pruning and Caching of Reference Tokens for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22717)**  `arXiv:2611.22717`  `cs.CV`  
  _Siyuan Wang, Haoran Li, Yi Yue, Tak L. Fung, Quan Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-to-video diffusion transformers pack the variational autoencoder (VAE) latents of all references and the generated tokens into one full-attention sequence, in which reference tokens make up about half. Because attention cost grows quadratically with sequence length, these tokens take up an even larger share of the attention computation, yet most of them are static, smooth or irrelevant to the output. To accelerate reference-conditioned generation, we propose pruning and caching of reference tokens for video generation (PCRG), a training-free, plug-and-play method that uses two signals the model already computes to assign each reference token to one of three tiers, namely pruned, cached or kept. The first is a low-level redundancy r from VAE latents, since a token whose latent barely changes over time or across its neighbors adds little new content. The second is a high-level importance g from the vision-language-model (VLM) conditioner, where the attention that the prompt and any first or last frames pay to a token tells whether the output needs to read it. Tokens that are not redundant are kept, and redundant tokens are cached if important and pruned otherwise. Pruned tokens leave the sequence before the first step with all rotary position embedding positions unchanged. Cached tokens have their keys and values computed once and reused, and kept tokens are processed as usual. On MiniMax-H3, already distilled to 8 timesteps and quantized to int8, PCRG is 1.57x faster end to end on our V2V-Bench and 1.49x faster on the public VACE-Benchmark, while the quality of VBench drops by only 0.24% and 0.17%, respectively. PCRG shortens the sequence rather than changing how attention is computed or which steps are run, so it also stacks on step caching and sparse attention. On V2V-Bench it reaches 1.86x with TeaCache, 1.82x with Spark at nearly unchanged fidelity and 2.15x with Veda.
  </details>

- **[Pros4D: Modeling Evolving 4D Worlds in Latent Space for Dynamic Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19803)**  `arXiv:2611.19803`  `cs.CV`  
  _Rongxin Gao, Dongxuan Liu, Chu Li, Chengyang Ji, Jialing Huang, Runyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic spatial reasoning from monocular video enables vision-language models (VLMs) to understand how 3D environments evolve over time, supporting physical world understanding and embodied intelligence. Existing efforts primarily address observed-dynamics understanding, whereas agents in dynamic environments also require future-dynamics prediction beyond the observation boundary. Moreover, external geometric processing and textual descriptions can increase inference complexity or lose information when representing continuous dynamics. These limitations motivate a shared internal state that preserves observed dynamics and remains predictive beyond the input video. Accordingly, we present Pros4D, a framework for modeling evolving 4D worlds in latent space that unifies retrospective understanding of observed-dynamics with prospective reasoning for future-dynamics prediction. Specifically, Pros4D-Engine derives geometry-grounded supervision and constructs paired examples for both tasks, while Pros4D-Bench evaluates them across eight spatial task categories spanning dynamic cameras, objects, and scenes. At the model level, Pros4D compresses observed dynamics into latent observed states and autoregressively rolls them forward into latent future states. 4D Latent Alignment (4DLA) grounds these states through complementary visual-semantic and geometric supervision, while joint language supervision connects them to task reasoning. Experiments across benchmarks show consistent improvements over strong baselines in both capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Toward Cross-Rubric Surgical Skill Assessment via Joint Embedding Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15125)**  `arXiv:2611.15125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiajun Cheng, Haidar M. Abdul-Muhsin, Juha Choi, Shan Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert review of surgical videos is essential for surgical training but labor-intensive, motivating AI-assisted skill assessment to provide feedback at scale. Although surgical assessment rubrics may use different language to describe similar skills, existing automated methods commonly predict predefined skill levels or numerical scores tied to fixed rubrics, limiting their ability to transfer across semantically equivalent assessment criteria. We propose Predictive Rubric Alignment (PRA), a joint embedding prediction approach that scores surgical videos using natural-language descriptions of skill levels. Rather than matching level probabilities, PRA predicts a rater-weighted combination of skill-level description embeddings. Adjacent description features define a score-parameterized path, and the predicted embedding is projected onto this path to read the score. We introduce two variants: \textit{PRA-item}, which uses learned queries for predefined assessment items, and \textit{PRA-cond}, which generates queries from textual item names to enable assessment of unseen items without retraining. On AIxSuture, a dataset of expert-rated suturing videos, PRA outperforms published baselines in skill classification. We further evaluate zero-shot transfer to the smaller surgical skill assessment benchmark JIGSAWS. Trained on only two AIxSuture rubric items, PRA-cond assesses unseen JIGSAWS items without further training, yielding positive score correlations and greater Grad-CAM overlap with instruments and suture than a description-matching baseline. These results support predictive rubric alignment as a promising direction for flexible and explainable automated surgical skill assessment.
  </details>

- **[DailyWildfireCAN: A Dataset and Benchmark for Next-Day Wildfire Spread Prediction in Canada](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14207)**  `arXiv:2611.14207`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mahacine Ettahri, Hager Radi Abdelwahed, Toluwaleke Olutayo, Daniel Aloise, Gaétan Marceau Caron_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wildfires represent a critical ecological and socio-economic challenge in Canada, where vast boreal forests and a warming climate have amplified the frequency and severity of extreme fire seasons. Predicting next-day wildfire spread can support fire management, but requires capturing complex interactions among weather, fuels, and terrain. Deep learning has emerged as a promising approach to learning these relationships from heterogeneous environmental data, yet its development and evaluation depend on representative datasets. Existing deep learning research on wildfire spread relies predominantly on US-focused datasets, leaving Canadian fire environments underrepresented. To address this gap, we present DailyWildfireCAN, a multi-modal, multi-temporal dataset and benchmark for predicting next-day wildfire spread across Canada. The dataset tracks continuous fire progression over multi-day events at a high spatial and temporal resolution of 90-m and 24-hour, respectively. This resolution preserves fine-scale information relevant to fire-edge geometry, local fuel variations, and terrain effects, supporting research toward improved daily wildfire spread forecasting. DailyWildfireCAN covers 2,440 wildfire events across eight fire seasons 2017-2024 and integrates 33 features across diverse modalities, including topography, meteorology, land cover, vegetation and forest structure, human influence indices, and satellite imagery. We establish a comprehensive benchmark comparing mono-temporal and multi-temporal prediction models, accompanied by a multi-dimensional evaluation framework assessing robustness across wildfire characteristics, geographic regions, and temporal conditions, spatial prediction quality, and sensitivity to reference label variations.
  </details>

- **[MICRO: A Surgery-Phase Retinal Image Rigid Registration Benchmark and One-Step Frequency-Structure Similarity Registration Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13045)**  `arXiv:2611.13045`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mingdao Gong, Yan Hu, Mingzhen Yuan, Jialu Zhang, Junming Feng, Yiming Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Precise multimodal retinal image rigid registration (MRIRG) is essential for aligning cross-modal vessel structures, offering critical guidance for ophthalmic surgery. Despite its significance, the advancement of MRIRG is hindered by the lack of publicly available multimodal retinal image rigid registration datasets spanning various ophthalmic surgery phases (e.g., preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative). To address this issue, we introduce the MICRO dataset, a large-scale MRIRG benchmark, comprising 1,365 cross-modal retinal image pairs with standardized annotations across all ophthalmic surgery phases. Moreover, existing deep registration methods achieve competitive MRIRG results via a two-step segmentation-then-detection framework, but segmentation quality heavily constrains their performance. We argue that this dependency stems from an implicit need for keypoint topology constraints, which have not been exploited for MRIRG through a one-step registration framework. Inspired by the above analysis, we propose a novel One-step Frequency-Structure Similarity Registration (OFS2Reg) learning framework for MRIRG that leverages the potential of cross-modal vessel structure similarities to construct keypoint topology constraints under a shared detection network. To be specific, OFS2Reg applies a well-designed frequency-guided structure preservation descriptor to construct coarse keypoint topology constraints in descriptors by highlighting significant vessel structures. Additionally, we develop a new cross-modal vessel structure similarity learning method that refines cross-modal keypoint topology constraints to precisely align pixel-level cross-modal vessel structures by leveraging cross-modal vessel structure similarity priors.
  </details>

- **[WAM-Sim0.1: World Anchored Memory Meets Online RL for Consistent and Realistic Driving Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07939)**  `arXiv:2611.07939`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiahao Cui, Jiaye Li, Wenhao Zhang, Yaoyi Li, Shan Luan, Quanhui Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive driving world models systematically suffer from structural drift, identity drift, and quality decay over long rollout horizons due to transient conditioning signals that lack persistent state. We present WAM-Sim, a driving world model anchored by a persistent hierarchical memory and optimized via online flow-matching RL. WAM-Sim structures persistent context across three temporal and semantic scales: pose-aligned structural tokens encoding persistent road topology, read-only scene tokens supplying geolocated background appearance, and write-once instance tokens preserving individual traffic participant identities across occlusions and chunk boundaries. To restore visual sharpness and physical plausibility degraded by multi-scale conditioning, we introduce an online post-training framework that optimizes self-generated rollouts via flow-matching RL guided by hierarchy-aligned visual rewards and a vision-language model physical judge. The invariant memory empirically confines policy optimization within the anchored context, helping suppress semantic collapse and reward hacking, while online RL systematically enforces physical commonsense and fine-grained visual fidelity. On the nuScenes benchmark, WAM-Sim achieves a superior generation fidelity (FID=3.47, FVD=25.72) and strong downstream perception performance (24.16 mAP, 71.23 road mIoU). On minute-scale rollouts of self-collected data, WAM-Sim substantially mitigates structural and identity drift, preserving high scene similarity and subject identity coherence across extended horizons. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Any3R: Feedforward 3D Reconstruction from Any Camera Type](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36150)**  `arXiv:2610.36150`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jingong Chen, Qingwen Zhang, Youming Deng, Chulwoo Pack, Olaf Wysocki, Boris Jutzi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Geometry foundation models recover camera poses and dense scene geometry from multiple images using feed-forward networks, but are typically designed for pinhole cameras. This assumption is embedded in the backbone itself: positional encoding locates each patch by its pixel index and the training loss weights every pixel equally, so both treat image coordinates as if they fixed the viewing direction in 3D space, which holds only for pinhole cameras. We condition both on the dense ray field, which maps each pixel to its 3D viewing direction and represents any central camera regardless of its projection model. $\textit{Angular coordinates}$ use the viewing direction of each patch for positional encoding, and $\textit{solid-angle weighting}$ scales the point loss by the angular area covered by each pixel. Together, they allow the same encoding and supervision to be used across different camera models. $\textbf{Any3R}$ is therefore a feed-forward model that reconstructs from any mix of central cameras without lens-specific retraining. On a benchmark of eight central cameras across three datasets, where every method reconstructs with its own predicted camera poses, it is the most consistently accurate method. $\textbf{Any3R}$ enables 3D reconstruction from crowdsourced and archival image collections containing diverse camera types. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WildAIGC-Bench: Can AI Image Detectors Handle the Wild?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28087)**  `arXiv:2610.28087`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zijian Yu, Hangwei Zhang, Shunxing Chen, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An AI-generated scene or a subtle edit to a photograph can make a false claim about a public event appear credible. Existing benchmarks test detectors across generators and platforms, but offer limited insight into how well they recognize fabrications that circulate as evidence for such claims. We introduce WildAIGC-Bench, an event-driven benchmark designed for broad coverage, timely updates, and human-verified labels. A recurring search across news outlets, fact-checking sites, and social posts identifies emerging cases, and reviewers verify the evidence for each image. The benchmark contains 600 AI-generated or edited images and 638 genuine photographs of related subjects. It preserves posts alongside crops of the depicted images and links documented edits to their original photographs where available. All ten visual detectors achieve lower balanced accuracy on WildAIGC-Bench than their respective averages across eight external benchmarks with both classes. On 37\% of the AI images, at least three quarters of the evaluated detectors predict real. Cropping improves recall for most visual detectors but reduces it for most general-purpose multimodal models. Comparisons with editing originals further reveal local alterations that most detectors miss even when they correctly accept the original photograph.
  </details>

- **[TailorGS: Made-to-Measure Density Control for 3D Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31248)**  `arXiv:2609.31248`  `cs.CV`  
  _Afif Boudaoud, Jiayi Liu, Alexandru Calotoiu, Torsten Hoefler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D Gaussian Splatting reconstructs a scene from a set of posed photographs called the capture; Gaussian count affects reconstruction quality, storage, and rendering cost. How to select this number automatically across capture scales remains unresolved: density control techniques effective on standard benchmarks can leave larger captures with too few Gaussians to reconstruct fine details. We observe that the surface to represent, given by capture extent and resolution, is known before training, whereas the capacity needed to reconstruct its content depends strongly on the 3D optimization trajectory and becomes apparent during reconstruction. We introduce TailorGS, which unifies coverage-scaled exploration and coverage-weighted self-distillation under a non-redundant 3D scene coverage measure. Before training, TailorGS groups observed surface points into coarse voxels and shares each voxel's weight among views observing it from the same side. Combined with image resolution, these weights set a generous exploration cap that limits budget inflation from repeated observations. During the first 80% of training, TailorGS grows toward this cap. The resulting model serves as teacher for a single closed-form self-distillation step, balancing fidelity to its renderings against Gaussian count under the same coverage measure. The retained primitives are refined over the remaining 20% of training. On 13 standard scenes, TailorGS matches the mean PSNR of the best-performing evaluated baseline, LeGS, with 48% fewer Gaussians. On eight large captures, the same configuration automatically scales to larger models when necessary, achieving the highest mean PSNR across 13 evaluated baselines: 0.63 dB above the runner-up by allocating 2.8 times as many Gaussians.
  </details>

- **[Reason in the Words You Speak: Idiolectal Paraphrasing Off-Policy Traces for Reasoning Distillation in VideoLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26684)**  `arXiv:2608.26684`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ji Soo Lee, Jinyoung Park, Seohyun Lee, Jongha Kim, Joonmyung Choi, Jinsung Yoon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent large language models achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, where reinforcement learning with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has emerged as a leading paradigm for optimizing models on self-generated trajectories. However, the on-policy nature of GRPO bounds the model to the reasoning skills it can already produce, restricting to learn more advanced capabilities. Prior works inject privileged reasoning traces from a stronger teacher policy to guide training, yet these traces are inherently out of distribution with respect to the student policy. We observe that this mismatch between on-policy and off-policy causes gradient clipping on semantically critical reasoning tokens, ultimately rewarding correct answers while leaving the reasoning that justifies them unlearned. Hence, we propose Echo-GRPO, a framework that lets the model reason in the words it speaks. Rather than imitating low-probability privileged traces from the teacher model, Echo-GRPO rewrites them into the student policy's own idiolect, that is, its own characteristic vocabulary and expression patterns, while preserving their semantics via Dual-Reference Decoding. We instantiate this framework as VideoEcho-R1 for video reasoning distillation, achieving consistent improvements across three multimodal LLM backbones and five benchmarks. Finally, we show that our idiolectal paraphrasing is a plug-in module that consistently improves both RL and supervised fine-tuning frameworks for reasoning distillation, demonstrating that policy-aligned supervision extends beyond GRPO.
  </details>

- **[ArmorOCR: Grounded Adversarial Visual Perception via Observation-Transferred Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20122)**  `arXiv:2608.20122`  `cs.CV`  
  _Linhan Cao, Siyuan Li, Jun Lan, Liangbo He, Guannan Li, Xiaolei Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models (LMMs) have demonstrated strong OCR recognition capabilities, yet remain vulnerable to adversarial visual text that is readable to humans but challenging for models to localize and recognize. Existing OCR benchmarks mainly focus on natural or document-style text, while adversarial OCR evaluations remain limited in scale, task coverage, or region-aware evaluation. In this paper, we formulate adversarial OCR as a \textbf{grounded OCR perception} task and introduce \textbf{AdvSpot}, the first benchmark for grounded adversarial OCR evaluation. AdvSpot comprises 390 images with region-level annotations, spanning 5 primary categories and 13 fine-grained adversarial OCR types. To address this challenge, we propose \textbf{ArmorOCR}, a two-stage training framework for robust adversarial OCR perception. ArmorOCR first acquires missing adversarial OCR perception from privileged transformed observations through On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), and then refines grounded OCR perception through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with task-conditioned rewards for localization, recognition, full spotting, and visual question answering (VQA). Experiments on our AdvSpot, other adversarial OCR benchmarks, and general OCR benchmarks demonstrate that ArmorOCR consistently improves adversarial OCR perception while preserving competitive general OCR capability.
  </details>

- **[HistoGPA: A Context-Conditioned Gene-Prior Attention Framework for Histology-Based Spatial Gene Expression Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24364)**  `arXiv:2607.24364`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziang Liu, Qinglin Wang, Yigui Feng, Shuai Li, Jie Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial transcriptomics is costly, motivating the prediction of spatial gene expression from routine hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) slides. Existing methods use tissue context to refine visual features but keep pretrained gene priors fixed across specimens. Since the relevance of such a prior may vary with tissue context, we propose HistoGPA, which conditions the gene prior itself on the slide being analyzed. A slide-level histological representation modulates a memory of pretrained gene embeddings before each spatial location queries it through cross-attention, and also gates context into the local features. Across ten cancer-specific tasks from HEST-1k, HistoGPA achieves a macro-averaged gene-wise Pearson correlation of 0.300 on the HEST-Bench genes, compared with 0.260 for the strongest of four recent methods. Capacity-matched ablations demonstrate the importance of slide-specific information and show that conditioning the gene prior provides benefits beyond visual context modulation, while pretrained gene embeddings outperform random tokens.
  </details>

- **[Do You Need Text Rectification? Soft Attention Mask Embedding for Rectification-Free Scene Text Spotting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18173)**  `arXiv:2605.18173`  `cs.CV`  
  _Antonio Colombo, Giovanni Bianchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end scene text spotting, which unifies text detection and recognition within a single framework, has witnessed remarkable progress driven by deep learning advances. However, most existing approaches still suffer from incomplete mask proposals caused by multi-scale variation, arbitrary text shapes, and complex background interference, thereby degrading recognition accuracy. In this paper, we propose a novel Soft Attention Mask Embedding module (SAME) that leverages the global receptive field of Transformer encoders to encode high-level features and compute soft attention weights, which are then hierarchically embedded with predicted masks to generate refined text-boundary-aware masks that effectively suppress background noise. Building upon this module, we present SAME-Net, a robust end-to-end text spotting framework that requires neither character-level annotations nor auxiliary text rectification modules. Since the soft attention mechanism is fully differentiable, recognition loss gradients can be back-propagated through the SAME module to the detection branch, enabling joint optimization of detection and recognition objectives. Extensive experiments on challenging benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach: SAME-Net achieves 84.02\% end-to-end H-mean on the arbitrarily-shaped Total-Text dataset, surpassing the previous state-of-the-art GLASS by 1.02\% in full-lexicon accuracy without additional training data, and obtains competitive 83.4\% strong-lexicon results on the multi-oriented ICDAR 2015 dataset.
  </details>

- **[MorphoHELM: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating Representations for Microscopy-Based Morphology Assays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15383)**  `arXiv:2605.15383`  `cs.CV`  
  _Emre Hayir, Lorin Crawford, Alex X. Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Microscopy images contain rich information about how cells respond to perturbations, making them essential to applications like drug screening. To quantify images, researchers often use representation extraction methods, and recent years have seen a proliferation of deep learning methods. While measuring the quality of these representations is essential, evaluation remains fragmented, with each proposed model evaluated on different tasks and datasets, using custom pipelines and metrics, making it difficult to fairly compare models. Here, we introduce MorphoHELM, a comprehensive open benchmark for evaluating feature extraction methods for Cell Painting, the most widely-used morphological profiling assay. MorphoHELM consolidates evaluation standards in the field, extends and corrects them to be more robust, and evaluates on the widest range of methods to date. A defining feature of the benchmark is that each task is evaluated at different degrees of batch effects (or technical noise), directly quantifying how the ability of methods to detect biological signal degrades as noise increases. Together, these properties enable MorphoHELM to detect trade-offs between methods, and we demonstrate that models that excel at certain kinds of biological signal are weaker at others. We show that no existing model outperforms classic computer vision analytic strategies across all settings, which remain the strongest general use-case representations. All datasets, code, and evaluation tools are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[See, Remember, Explore: A Benchmark and Baselines for Streaming Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23864)**  `arXiv:2603.23864`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuxi Wei, Wei Huang, Qirui Chen, Lu Hou, Xiaojuan Qi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial understanding is fundamental for embodied agents, yet most spatial VLMs and benchmarks remain offline-evaluating post-hoc QA over pre-recorded inputs and overlooking two crucial deployment-critical requirements: long-horizon streaming inference and active perception when the current view is insufficient. To address this gap, we introduce S3-Bench, a benchmark suite for streaming spatial question answering with active exploration, where queries are temporally grounded to specific timestamps and must be answered using only observations available up to that moment. S3-Bench adopts a dual-domain design, combining a scalable simulator with controllable trajectories and exploration actions, and real-world streaming videos that capture practical sensing artifacts for rigorous generalization evaluation. Overall, it spans 10K+ scenes and 26K+ trajectories, with dedicated training (S3-Train) and evaluation (S3-Eval) splits. We further propose AMF-VLM, which supports streaming spatial reasoning under bounded computing via (i) memory folding, which compresses long-horizon observations into compact structured memory, and (ii) active exploration, which outputs explicit actions (e.g. move/rotate/scan) to acquire missing evidence before answering. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, compared to models using identical training data, our approach yields improvements of 8.8% and 13.3% on the simulated and real splits of S3-Eval, respectively, while maintaining competitive transferability to standard spatial benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[A Deformable Attention-Based Detection Transformer with Cross-Scale Feature Fusion for Industrial Coil Spring Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13429)**  `arXiv:2603.13429`  `cs.CV`  
  _Matteo Rossi, Pony Matt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated visual inspection of locomotive coil springs presents significant challenges due to the morphological diversity of surface defects, substantial scale variations, and complex industrial backgrounds. This paper proposes MSD-DETR (Multi-Scale Deformable Detection Transformer), a novel detection framework that addresses these challenges through three key innovations: (1) a structural re-parameterization strategy that decouples training-time multi-branch topology from inference-time efficiency, enhancing feature extraction while maintaining real-time performance; (2) a deformable attention mechanism that enables content-adaptive spatial sampling, allowing dynamic focus on defect-relevant regions regardless of morphological irregularity; and (3) a cross-scale feature fusion architecture incorporating GSConv modules and VoVGSCSP blocks for effective multi-resolution information aggregation. Comprehensive experiments on a real-world locomotive coil spring dataset demonstrate that MSD-DETR achieves 92.4\% mAP@0.5 at 98 FPS, outperforming state-of-the-art detectors including YOLOv8 (+3.1\% mAP) and the baseline RT-DETR (+2.8\% mAP) while maintaining comparable inference speed, establishing a new benchmark for industrial coil spring quality inspection.
  </details>

- **[Train Short, Inference Long: Training-Free Horizon Extension for Autoregressive Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14027)**  `arXiv:2602.14027`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jia Li, Xiaomeng Fu, Xurui Peng, Weifeng Chen, Youwei Zheng, Tianyu Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models such as CausVid and Self Forcing enable efficient long-video generation through chunk-wise rollout with local sliding-window attention, yet suffer from visual degradation, appearance drift, and motion collapse beyond their training horizons. From a long-context sequence modeling perspective, we identify two underexplored inference-time bottlenecks in AR video length extrapolation. First, scarce high-quality long-video data and high training costs restrict video models to short training horizons. Consequently, low-frequency dimensions of 3D temporal RoPE remain under-exposed during training, inducing stronger spectral bias than in LLMs and hindering direct transfer of LLM context-extension methods. Second, both standard independent noise initialization and consistency-oriented methods with positive inter-frame correlation lack high-frequency dynamic priors to counter motion decay over long rollouts. To address these issues, we propose FLEX (Frequency-aware Length EXtension), a training-free, plug-and-play inference framework comprising three complementary components. Frequency-aware 3D RoPE modulation interpolates under-exposed low frequencies while extrapolating well-exposed high frequencies. Antiphase Noise Sampling (ANS) introduces negative intra-chunk correlation to inject high-frequency motion priors at initialization. An inference-only attention sink works with RoPE modulation to anchor global consistency. Experiments on Self Forcing and CausVid show substantial gains in long-horizon synthesis, with Self Forcing + FLEX matching or surpassing both training-free and training-based baselines on VBench-Long at $6\times$ to $12\times$ length extrapolation. Additional evaluations on Krea-14B and minute-level generation with LongLive demonstrate strong generalization across model scales, inference pipelines, and native long-video generators.
  </details>

- **[S2WMamba: Modality-Specific Wavelet Decomposition with Learnable Detail Bands for Pansharpening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06330)**  `arXiv:2512.06330`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Zhang, Junhan Luo, Yugang Cao, Liang-Jian Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pansharpening reconstructs a high-resolution multispectral image from a high-resolution panchromatic (PAN) image and a low-resolution multispectral (MS) image. The MS bands carry the colour that must be preserved, but they enter most networks as an undifferentiated feature dimension, mixed with the spatial correction before any spectral decomposition is formed. We propose S2WMamba (spatial, spectral, wavelet, Mamba), which treats the spatial and the spectral axes with different transforms: a two-dimensional wavelet decomposition applied to a learned embedding of the PAN image isolates spatial edges and textures, while a one-dimensional wavelet decomposition applied along the spectral axis of the fused cue separates the spectral base shared by all bands from the band-specific variation. In both transforms the approximation (low-pass) filter is fixed and carries no learned parameters, and only the detail filters are learned. The two branches are coupled in one direction: the spatial branch first reconstructs a correction to the upsampled MS image, which then conditions the input of the spectral branch. A Mamba-based aggregator merges the two branch outputs. On the PanCollection benchmark, S2WMamba attains the best reduced-resolution PSNR on each of WorldView-3 (39.502 dB), GF2 (50.083 dB) and QuickBird (38.802 dB), and in full resolution its HQNR is within 0.011 of the best on every sensor. Ablations show that the benefit of the cascade lies entirely in its direction: reversing it yields the same accuracy as removing the coupling altogether. Code:this http URLbenchmark:this http URL.
  </details>

- **[Tasklets: A Unit-Test Benchmark for Language Model Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27607)**  `arXiv:2611.27607`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zayd Muhammad Kawakibi Zuhri, Munachiso Samuel Nwadike, Kentaro Inui, Alham Fikri Aji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Tasklets, a benchmark of small synthetic tasks for evaluating the computational capabilities of language model sequence-mixing layers. These tasks probe foundational capabilities such as recall, sequence manipulation, and state tracking. Such tasks are widely used in architecture research, but differences in task selection, formulation, and evaluation make comparisons across studies difficult. Tasklets brings these tasks together, organizing them by the capabilities they test and providing a shared setup for evaluating how different architectural layer types learn fundamental computations. We compare a broad range of layer types through controlled experiments with small models. Our task implementations and evaluation code support reproducible, low-cost testing of new architectural proposals:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[How Language Models Compress and Compare: Understanding Selection with Token Covariance Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26874)**  `arXiv:2611.26874`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Moshe Eliasof_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many language-model tasks reduce to selecting among structured alternatives, yet we still lack a simple picture of how a decoder-only transformer organizes an entire selection prompt across depth. We introduce token covariance maps, a prompt-level geometric view that tracks how token positions co-vary in hidden-state space at each layer. This turns a structured prompt into a depth-indexed sequence of maps whose spectral and block structure can be aligned with instructions, context, and candidate answers. Across 14 decoder-only models from 4 families and 12 structured-selection benchmarks, token covariance maps reveal a robust compress-then-compare organization. Early layers represent prompts diffusely, middle layers compress them into a shared low-rank bottleneck, and late layers re-expand into context-option and option-option geometry. This organization recurs across larger fixed-template samples, deterministic prompt rewrites, pairwise reranking reformulations, and controlled answer-set manipulations, while also emerging during training from an initially flat depth profile. Its timing aligns with behavior: useful answer discrimination appears well after the bottleneck, and low-rank intervention around the bottleneck preferentially disrupts late comparison geometry. We additionally show that the same geometric profile appears in instruction-tuned models, across several languages, and during open-ended generation. We further show that changing the internal representation of one candidate around the compressed region can shift which candidate the model later prefers. These results show that token covariance maps are a diagnostic tool for comparing how models organize information across prompts, model families, and stages of training.
  </details>

- **[Right On Q: Recovering Reasoning under Quantization via Self-Generated Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29378)**  `arXiv:2610.29378`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Boyuan Zheng, Jing Xiong, Chenwei Wu, Hui Shen, Zesen Zhao, Hei Ting Una Chan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization-aware training (QAT) aims to recover reasoning capabilities in low-bit large language models (LLMs). Existing recipes typically supervise the quantized student with the original full-precision model on fixed response sequences. However, the resulting low-bit quantized models still struggle on long-horizon reasoning tasks and often fail to terminate within a token budget that suffices for the full-precision model. We identify two challenges in closing this gap. First, fixed-response supervision leaves a mismatch between training prefixes and those encountered during student generation, and this trajectory divergence grows as responses lengthen. Second, on its own prefixes, the student underpredicts some next-token candidates and overpredicts others, and this distributional misalignment becomes more frequent at high-entropy prefixes. We present OnQ, a low-bit recovery pipeline that combines on-policy distillation and candidate-wise Dynamic KL. Starting from block-wise calibration, the quantized student generates responses and receives full-precision teacher supervision on its own prefixes. Dynamic KL weights forward and reverse KL by each candidate's teacher-student probability ratio to balance correction of under- and overprediction. Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B at 2- and 3-bit precision, OnQ achieves the highest nine-benchmark average, with the largest gains on long-horizon reasoning benchmarks. At 2 bits, it outperforms every baseline on every benchmark at every model size, improving the Qwen3-8B average by 10.26 points over the strongest baseline. It also produces shorter responses with fewer truncations and repetitive errors.
  </details>

- **[Your Judge Inherits Your Prompt: In-Context Label Bias in an LLM-as-Judge Is Directional, Not Symmetric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26881)**  `arXiv:2611.26881`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Abdullah Ahmad, Ahmed J. O. Ahmed, Luca Citi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-judge has become a standard way to score open-ended model outputs, yet the judge is itself a frozen language model steered by a few in-context examples, and such models are known to absorb the label balance of their demonstrations rather than treat them as neutral illustrations. We ask whether this label bias reaches an LLM judge used inside a multimodal (video question answering) evaluation pipeline, and what form it takes. Using a fully frozen generator-judge pipeline, we hold the generated answers fixed and vary only the Yes/No balance of the judge's few-shot examples across four demonstration sets. Each verdict is validated against a two-annotator human consensus on 200 examples per dataset. The judge's acceptance rate stays close to human consensus when its examples are balanced or skewed toward rejection, but jumps sharply (by about 30 points on NExT-QA and 20 on MSVD-QA, with a strongly one-sided error profile) when the examples are skewed toward acceptance; the opposite skew produces no matching drop. The bias is therefore one-directional within the tested range of label ratios. A separate five-draw analysis shows that results from a single example draw are unreliable, varying by up to 18 points, more than any difference between prompting conditions. We conclude that demonstration label bias in an LLM judge is a directional, inflation-only failure mode that can fabricate apparent benchmark gains but not losses, and that it is cheaply avoided by balancing the judge's examples and reporting results averaged over several draws.
  </details>

- **[RoSci: Exploring Robustness in Science VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27750)**  `arXiv:2611.27750`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Junying Wang, Yijin Guo, Yalun Wu, Ziheng Jiao, Farong Wen, Qihang Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advancements in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable significant progress in scientific tasks. However, these models are generally assumed to be presented with complete and accurate information, overlooking the challenges of erroneous/missing information in wild scenarios. To address this gap, we introduce the concept of Scientific Model Robustness, which defines a model's ability to detect anomalies, assess solvability, and perform self-calibration when faced with Information Error (inconsistencies) or Information Loss (insufficiency). Specifically, we present RoSci-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,220 samples using a seed-variant strategy across six degradation types. To address VLM fragility, we further propose RoSci-Agent, a framework that employs a Parse-Verify-Decide pipeline to vet premises before reasoning. The evaluated VLMs sometimes answer questions whose premises are invalid or incomplete. RoSci-Agent achieves a higher Overall Robustness Score than the evaluated standalone VLMs. RoSci-Bench gives the multimodal reasoning community a common test of when models answer, request clarification, or reject a scientific question.
  </details>

- **[No Audio, No Transcripts, No Problem: LLM-based ASR Adaptation Using Only Domain Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27434)**  `arXiv:2611.27434`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Thibault Bañeras-Roux, Manjunath K E, Kadri Hacioglu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a speech recognizer be adapted to a new domain with no target audio and no transcripts, using only written documentation? This is the realistic onboarding setting: a new customer hands over raw documentation, not the transcripts that text-only adaptation normally assumes. We turn a domain's documents into adaptation text in two ways, directly as written sentences or rendered by a large language model (LLM) into synthetic spoken dialogs, and feed them to a recently proposed denoising-based text-only adaptation method. We show that, across in-domain, out-of-domain, and cross-domain settings on two corpora, document-only adaptation can match or closely approach real transcriptions; whether written or conversational rendering is best depends on speaking style and the amount of text generated. To our knowledge, this is the first study of document-only adaptation of LLM-based ASR. As no existing corpus pairs audio, transcripts, and documents, we also built and released a document-grounded benchmark.
  </details>

- **[WaXup: Weight Averaging of Experts as Data Augmentation for Robust Introspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27353)**  `arXiv:2611.27353`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Marc Boëlle, Lucas Dixon, Alexandre Ramé_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As the capabilities of large language models keep improving, traditional mechanistic interpretability methods struggle to keep pace. In contrast, trainable introspection methods emerge as a promising alternative; they repurpose the model itself as a self-diagnostic tool, trained to verbalize what it has learned during finetuning in natural language. While training on datasets of finetuned experts achieves encouraging results, it currently lacks robustness and generalization. Building on the data augmentation framework of MiXup, we introduce WaXup, a method that augments the introspection training set through linear interpolation in the weight space. This is motivated by the linear mode connectivity property: weight averaged experts interpolate the behaviors of the initial experts. We evaluate WaXup on three benchmarks: hidden-behavior models, a meta-dataset of real-world NLP tasks and AuditBench, a challenging alignment auditing suite of models adversarially trained to conceal their behaviors. WaXup improves the accuracy of generated descriptions as well as robustness to various distribution shifts. We hope to improve the reliability of trainable introspection methods towards better and safer models.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning in Fewer Tokens: Concept-Normalized Multilingual Scratchpads and Reversible Model-Adaptive Reasoning Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27326)**  `arXiv:2611.27326`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rabimba Karanjai, Suryabhan Singh Hada, Hemanth Hegadehalli Madhavarao, Qun Gu, Libin N. George, Wenhuan Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought prompting significantly improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) but incurs high inference costs due to lengthy autoregressive decoding. While previous efficiency approaches reduce costs by discarding tokens or utilizing continuous latent representations, these lossy methods fundamentally sacrifice interpretability and auditability. In this work, we introduce a novel compression framework for intermediate reasoning. We apply Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE) to reasoning corpora to learn a compressed vocabulary of Private Use Area (PUA) tokens, each representing frequent semantic spans. After expanding the LLM's vocabulary, we fine-tune the model via strictly 1:1, our method is lossless. We evaluate our approach on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-14B, and Qwen2.5-32B across four diverse benchmarks. Our method reduces total generated tokens by 14.7% on average with reasoning trace lengths dropping by up to 27.1%, while incurring a negligible average accuracy cost of 1.6 percentage points. Crucially, this approach is orthogonal to existing lossy methods, allowing practitioners to stack lossless and irreversible compression techniques. We demonstrate that a compressed intermediate language provides a highly efficient computational resource that accelerates inference while fully preserving the transparency of LLM reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Do Language Models Know Which Words Steer Their Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27317)**  `arXiv:2611.27317`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanbei Jiang, Chao Lei, Jey Han Lau, Krista A. Ehinger, Rena Gao Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits intermediate reasoning steps from language models, it is unclear which words in the context actually steer the CoT. We identify Phantom Context Influence (PCI): language models are disproportionately steered by semantically irrelevant context words, even as the same models deny those words' relevance when asked. To localize PCI, we introduce Leave-One-Out Word JSD analysis, which removes one context word at a time from the otherwise intact context to isolate its marginal effect on the next-token distribution; words whose removal flips the prediction (pivot words) are passed to the same LLM to self-judge whether it recognizes this predictive dependence. Across four models and three benchmarks, language models largely do not know which words steer their reasoning, a mismatch we call the Generation-Judgment Contradiction. Inspired by this phenomenon, we propose Online KV Suppression: it scores every prompt word's influence over the whole chain of thought in a single pass, then zeroes the KV-cache entries of the highest-influence word the model itself judges inert and redecodes. This single-word intervention improves CoT accuracy across four models and five reasoning benchmarks, by up to 8.9 percentage points on math reasoning benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Do Senior Agents Do Their Job? Measuring Role Integrity in How LLM Agents Use Subordinate Material](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27139)**  `arXiv:2611.27139`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinxiang Xie, Xinyi Zhang, Changjiang Jiang, Kexu Liu, Qi Yang, Yuxin Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly divide long tasks among subagents whose reports a senior agent turns into the final answer. Such a division of labor pays off only if each agent, and above all the senior agent, carries out the duties of its role. Current evaluations, however, mostly score the final answer, and a fluent, well-cited answer cannot show whether the senior agent weighed its subagents' reports or merely aggregated them. To assess whether a senior agent meets its assigned responsibilities, we define role integrity as the preservation of an agent's functional responsibilities when it acts on its subordinates' material. To measure it, we introduce RI-Bench, a behavioral benchmark grounded in the structures and public records of three institutional hierarchies: peer review, appellate adjudication, and parliamentary inquiry. We choose these hierarchies because their rules explicitly assign the senior role a duty toward subordinate material, and together they cover three basic duties: to weigh, to verify, and to relay. Rather than scoring output correctness, we measure how senior agents' outputs respond to controlled changes in subordinate material, using rule-based checks and a rubric-guided LLM judge calibrated against human annotation. Across eight LLMs and over 12,000 agentic episodes, senior agents rarely adopt a planted procedural error (2.9% of rulings) but often follow a majority of generic reviews (up to 71% of decisions) or drop witnesses' stated limitations (up to 65% of cited claims). Obligation prompts reduce these failures without removing them, and they also shift behavior in the control conditions. These findings motivate direct, obligation-specific evaluation of role integrity rather than inferring it from general capability or explicit role instructions. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ProbeAgent: Principled Diagnosis of Agent Failures in Verifiable Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26897)**  `arXiv:2611.26897`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Huang, Yuchen Ma, Shuhang Lin, Yuyang Wu, Zixuan Weng, Zixiang Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving agents requires discovering where they fail and understanding why. An agent acts over multiple steps in a changing environment, so diagnosing its failures requires more than inspecting the final response of a large language model (LLM).
