The same unsuccessful outcome can arise from task difficulty or from an error the agent repeats across tasks. We introduce ProbeAgent, an automated framework that discovers agent failures, tests their proposed causes, and identifies shared failure modes. Starting from benchmark tasks, ProbeAgent builds executable tests and validates them using reference solutions, automated checks, and review of the task requirements. For each failed trajectory, it explains the error and constructs new tasks that preserve the suspected cause while changing the surrounding context. Running the agent on these tasks tests whether the same error recurs. ProbeAgent then groups failures with similar causes and generates descriptions that distinguish their errors. This process connects explanations of individual failures to executable tests and descriptions of shared failure modes. We evaluate ProbeAgent across 22 agent configurations using tasks from 10 benchmarks, collecting 1,244 failures and producing 536 failure-mode descriptions. These descriptions distinguish specific errors within broad outcomes such as incomplete work, including planning without acting and stopping before delivering a file. In human evaluation, annotators accept 90.0% of assigned descriptions and correctly reject 83.3% of similar alternatives, supporting the accuracy and specificity of the discovered failure modes.
  </details>

- **[MedicalRSI: Medical Recursive Self-Improving Agents via Clinical Model-Harness Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26806)**  `arXiv:2611.26806`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqing Wang, Hamid Palangi, Lili Zhao, Kaize Ding_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical agents built on large language models (LLMs) have advanced rapidly, yet most stay fixed once deployed and do not keep using their experience to improve later attempts. As a result, the same kind of error can recur across patients. To address this, existing self-improvement methods update an agent's weights or its medical harness from its own experience, but their improving strategy stays fixed and never learns which changes actually help, which caps how much the agent can improve. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) instead improves both the agent and its improving strategy. However, achieving RSI for medical agents is challenging, because clinical workflows rarely come with a reference harness, and a change that fixes early errors may not fix the problems the agent meets later. We propose MedicalRSI, an RSI framework built on Clinical Model-Harness Co-Evolution, in which one model plays two roles. The Solver is the medical agent itself, and the Improver is its learnable improving strategy, which writes a medical harness from the agent's failures. Each harness is rewarded by its gain over acting without it, and both roles are trained together, so the Improver keeps adapting to the problems the Solver has not yet solved. On multiple benchmarks and LLM backbones, MedicalRSI outperforms existing context-level and weight-level self-improvement methods and transfers well to unseen workflows, new LLMs and new databases, a step toward medical agents that keep improving.
  </details>

- **[Entity Matching using Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34626)**  `arXiv:2610.34626`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Aaron Steiner, Ralph Peeters, Christian Bizer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Entity matching is the task of deciding whether two records describe the same real-world entity. Current methods often rely on pre-trained language models (PLMs), which require task-specific fine-tuning, or on generative large language models (LLMs), which need no fine-tuning but are more expensive and slower than PLMs at matching large numbers of records. Decision models are a new class of pre-trained models that return probabilities for predefined answer options rather than generate free-form text. This paper presents an extensive experimental evaluation of decision models for entity matching. We evaluate six decision models in a zero-shot setting on eight benchmarks and compare them to frontier generative LLMs and the fine-tuned PLM-based matcher Ditto. Without task-specific training data, the best decision model exceeds Ditto in mean F1 score and stays a few F1 points below the frontier LLMs at less than 1% of their cost. We show that three of the decision models produce well-calibrated match probabilities. We then explore two approaches to improving F1 scores at the expense of higher costs: First, we investigate model cascades that defer the least certain record pairs to a frontier LLM, increasing the mean F1-scores of the four strongest decision models by 2.7-4.8 percentage points. These cascades achieve F1-scores that do not differ significantly from those obtained by applying the frontier LLM to all record pairs, while reducing costs by factors of 2.5 to 5.1. Second, we employ an LLM to label a development set, which we use to select in-context demonstrations or to derive matching rules. These approaches raise the mean F1 score less than the model cascades. The cascade combining the best decision model with an ensemble of three frontier LLMs achieves an F1 score that does not differ significantly from that of the frontier LLM ensemble alone, at less than one-fifth of its cost.
  </details>

- **[CulturalRiddles: A Multicultural Benchmark of Riddles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29968)**  `arXiv:2610.29968`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _My Chiffon Nguyen, Tianyi Hu, Vinod Anbalagan, Henry Gagnier, Pouya Sadeghi, Farah Abdou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs expand their language coverage, evaluating how well they understand and represent diverse cultures becomes increasingly important. However, many benchmarks remain English-centric, with limited cultural coverage and a focus on factual knowledge over its application in real-world cultural contexts. We propose CulturalRiddles, a benchmark of original, culturally grounded riddles created entirely by humans through a participatory community effort, spanning 62 cultures (45 countries, 51 languages). These riddles are deeply rooted in their respective cultures and require abductive and compositional reasoning, alongside detailed factual and social knowledge, language skills, and an understanding of culturally specific associations. We benchmark 28 LLM configurations using both automatic and large-scale human evaluation. Even frontier models achieve at most 22% accuracy in some communities. We publicly release all artifacts alongside an open contribution process to expand the benchmark's linguistic and cultural coverage.
  </details>

- **[BioELX: Context-Aware Cross-lingual Biomedical Entity Linking without Task-Specific Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27380)**  `arXiv:2605.27380`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi Wang, Corina Dima, Liangyu Zhong, Steffen Staab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-lingual biomedical entity linking (BEL) maps mentions in any language to unique identifiers in a biomedical knowledge base, supporting clinical and biomedical NLP applications. We identify two issues affecting current systems. First, the UMLS (Bodenreider,2004) aliases used to train cross-lingual BEL retrievers are heavily skewed toward English, so retrievers generalize poorly to non-English mentions. Second, although context is often necessary for disambiguation, naively injecting context into retrievers trained only to align aliases severely degrades retrieval. We propose BioELX, a retrieve-rerank framework that addresses both issues. For retrieval, we continue training SapBERT_multi (Liu et al., 2021b) using Wikidata-derived cross-lingual alias supervision, forming shared concept neighborhoods across languages. For reranking, we adapt pretrained LLM rerankers to entity linking through mention-anchored prompting, which marks the target mention so that rerankers score candidates with respect to the intended mention rather than other salient tokens in the context. Experiments show that BioELX achieves new state-of-the-art results on four cross-lingual BEL benchmarks, improving Recall@1 by 4.9 to 18.2 percentage points over prior best results, without any task-specific BEL annotations. Our code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoOntologyBench: A Task-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Knowledge Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27734)**  `arXiv:2611.27734`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minye Shao, Chenghao Xiao, Xingyue Zhao, Yang Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation-model agents are increasingly extending beyond software engineering (SWE) into autonomous scientific research, where acquiring and integrating domain knowledge remains a critical challenge. Recent industrial advances have increasingly adopted knowledge graphs and ontology-based semantic layers to organize heterogeneous knowledge into reusable representations. We ask: Can autonomous agents construct ontologies that help them solve real tasks, and do they offer measurable benefits over alternative knowledge representations? Existing benchmarks evaluate ontology correctness or agent task success in isolation, leaving the value of autonomously acquired knowledge unresolved. We therefore introduce AutoOntologyBench, a task-grounded benchmark comprising 19,947 audited tasks grounded in 18 real-world knowledge sources across 7 domains, with source-native verification. Spanning Application, Repair, Organization, and Construction, AutoOntologyBench compares nine frontier-model agents under matched knowledge representations. Layered on retrieval, agent-built ontologies add 6.1 points of task success, yet trail an expert-derived reference by 13.0, identifying autonomous knowledge acquisition as the bottleneck to scalable scientific research. Code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Post-Training Unlearning in Diffusion Language Models using Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27652)**  `arXiv:2611.27652`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yunha Seo, Changhoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning is an approach to privacy protection: it aims to remove the influence of designated data while preserving the model's behavior on information that should be retained. While a growing body of work investigates this objective in autoregressive large language models, machine unlearning remains underexplored in diffusion language models (DLMs). DLMs generate text through iterative denoising, exposing partially masked states whose internal representations change throughout the generation process. This raises a DLM-specific question: can selective forgetting improve by identifying forget-data-associated features and adapting their suppression across denoising stages? We conduct a controlled investigation of this question in LLaDA-8B-Instruct. To evaluate the unlearning of knowledge plausibly encoded during pre-training, we use WMDP as our forget-domain benchmark. Using top-k sparse autoencoders and denoising-state replay, we compare static and stage-adaptive feature suppression. We find substantial shifts in forget-data-associated features across denoising stages, but tracking these dynamics alone does not reliably improve the forget-retain trade-off at the evaluated operating points. Together, our study provides an initial empirical foundation for developing and evaluating machine unlearning as a post-training privacy mechanism in DLMs.
  </details>

- **[Open CDLM: A Benchmark and Library for Continuous Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27536)**  `arXiv:2611.27536`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Jiang, Ruixi Chen, Jiayi Han, Yulei Sun, Yujian Chen, Gen Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable comparisons of continuous diffusion language models (CDLMs) require evaluating both model fit and the distribution of generated text. Two common practices leave this goal only partially addressed. Likelihood-based evaluation often reports a variational upper bound on discrete negative log-likelihood, whose slack can confound comparisons. Generated-sample-based evaluation commonly combines generative perplexity with within-sequence unigram entropy; both scores can remain favorable even when a sampler produces the same text on every draw. We introduce \textbf{Open CDLM}, a benchmark for evaluating CDLMs and their samplers, and describe the design of an accompanying open-source library to support reproducible comparisons. For likelihood-based evaluation, we clarify which comparisons of model fit are supported by reported perplexity bounds and how those conclusions depend on the evaluation procedure. For sample-based evaluation, we use \textit{Kernel Text Discrepancy} (\textbf{KTD}) to compare collections of generated and real texts, alongside diagnostics for diversity, validity, and coverage. In controlled OpenWebText experiments, replacing a collection of texts with copies of a single selected passage improves both conventional generation scores. MAUVE detects the resulting collapse, and its response to other perturbations depends on the text representation and the type of perturbation. This work marks the beginning of Open CDLM as an open-source project for CDLM research. We will maintain and extend the library over time and welcome contributions from the community.
  </details>

- **[Typhoon OCR 2: From Thai Document Transcription to Multilingual, Multi-Task OCR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27494)**  `arXiv:2611.27494`  `cs.CL`  
  _Surapon Nonesung, Natapong Nitarach, Sittipong Sripaisarnmongkol, Kunat Pipatanakul, Warit Sirichotedumrong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typhoon OCR 1.5 focused on reading Thai documents. However, in real-world workflows, users often need to extract specific information or preserve the structure of an entire page for further processing. Typhoon OCR 2 handles both tasks in a single model, with prompt-driven extraction and structured full-page parsing. Full-page parsing covers Thai, English, and Chinese. We release compact 2B and 0.8B models adapted from open document parsers through supervised fine-tuning on curated Thai and public multilingual data, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with task rewards and a rule penalizing unfinished responses. Among evaluated open models, Typhoon OCR 2 2B scores highest on Thai MDPBench (80.7 versus 68.9 for Typhoon OCR 1.5) and on ThaiOCRBench (59.91 versus 40.45), which covers recognition, extraction, and document understanding. The 2B model remains close to its base on English olmOCR-bench (84.7 versus 84.4) and on OmniDocBench, which evaluates English and Chinese together (90.97 versus 91.03). On MDPBench, GRPO reduces the mean share of pages with repetition loops for the 2B model from 3.2% to 0.7%, with a smaller and statistically uncertain score gain for the 0.8B model. We release the model weights, Python package, and evaluation suite.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Long Horizon: Short-Horizon Process Supervision for Long-Form Generation in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27348)**  `arXiv:2611.27348`  `cs.CL`  
  _Heyang Xu, HanWen Hao, Qianren Mao, Zhuo Liu, Qili Zhang, Sheng Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form generation is increasingly important for agentic LLM systems, yet current reinforcement learning methods typically optimize an entire document with a single outcome-level reward. This delayed and coarse-grained supervision makes it difficult to localize errors and assign credit across semantically distinct parts of the output. To address this, we propose a short-horizon framework that reformulates long-form generation through semantic decomposition into segment-level decisions. Each segment is generated with prefix-conditioned context, allowing cross-segment coherence to be preserved while keeping supervision local. The generated segments are then scored with rubric-based rewards for adherence, coherence, and relevance, enabling explicit local supervision at the segment level. These local rewards are used to compute step-wise advantages, yielding localized policy updates. To isolate the effect of supervision granularity, we instantiate both outcome-level supervision and our short-horizon process reward method (SH-PRM) under the same GRPO framework. Across multiple LLMs and two long-form generation benchmarks, SH-PRM consistently outperforms outcome-level supervision and strong inference-time baselines under the same optimizer and comparable budgets. Further analyses suggest that these gains primarily arise from improved credit assignment. Overall, our results suggest that aligning supervision granularity with the semantic structure of long-form generation is an effective design principle for long-form LLM training.
  </details>

- **[Observed, Curated, Reused: Observation Bias in Clinical AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27301)**  `arXiv:2611.27301`  `cs.CL`  
  _Md Shamim Ahmed, Lukas Galke Poech, Richard Röttger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical AI evaluations are vulnerable to observation bias embedded in routine medical documentation, which captures only what clinicians notice, test, and record rather than a complete clinical picture. As this evidence travels into publications and reference knowledge graphs, crucial distinctions disappear: unexamined symptoms blur with confirmed negatives, and chronological disease progression is lost. Surviving evidence can also enter both the benchmark query and the reference knowledge base, creating a self-referential loop where a patient is evaluated against clues derived from their own published case report.To quantify this bias, we tracked 6,503 clinical cases across six historical Human Phenotype Ontology (HPO) releases (October 2021-June 2026) to identify when HPO first cited the publications describing them. This yielded a confirmatory cohort with an observable "source ingestion" event, where HPO cited a case's source publication as target-disease evidence. Diagnostic rankers depended heavily on these source publications: for our primary semantic ranker (Resnik), publication-weighted mean reciprocal rank rose from 0.26 before paper ingestion to 0.84 upon inclusion in HPO, but collapsed to 0.05 when source-linked assertions were counterfactually removed. Two additional rankers (IC-cosine and LIRICAL) reproduced this trajectory, and control experiments confirmed the diagnostic jump was driven by the patient's publication rather than background HPO growth.Clinical AI systems may therefore score well because their knowledge base contains evidence linked to the test case's source publication, rather than through generalization. Modern knowledge is not the problem; evaluation evidence must match the claim. Trustworthy benchmarks must separate patients, source-linked evidence, and time: historical tests should use only knowledge available at the intended decision time.
  </details>

- **[Do Companies Deliver on Their IPO Claims?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27273)**  `arXiv:2611.27273`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vidhyakshaya Kannan, Shiv Banafar, Michael Galarnyk, Sagnik Nandi, Soumeya Kannan, Shauray Arora, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Initial public offering (IPO) filings describe what a company does and what it intends to become. Yet whether individual forward-looking claims are subsequently realized is difficult to assess. We introduce Vision Follow-Through (VFT), a benchmark and agentic pipeline that separates claim extraction, category assignment, verifiability, and evidence-grounded follow-through assessment. \vftclaims{} contains 5,066 human-annotated claim spans from 50 IPO filings, while \vftverify{} adds verifiability tiers, follow-through outcomes, and cited later evidence for 1,000 claims. Across language models, classifying human-annotated claims is substantially easier than extracting claims from full filings. Extraction coverage also varies sharply by category, with Organizational/Culture claims recovered far less often than Market Opportunity claims. Follow-through assessment poses a separate challenge: model judgments diverge from human assessments in different ways rather than exhibiting a single consistent bias. These results show that reliable assessment of corporate follow-through requires both finding the relevant claims and grounding subsequent judgments in later evidence.
  </details>

- **[CoPyro: Feeding Copy Proposals to Parallel Drafters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27217)**  `arXiv:2611.27217`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hong-Kai Zheng, Piji Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates language generation by letting a target model verify several draft tokens in one forward pass. Earlier neural drafters such as EAGLE extend each candidate sequence one token at a time. Recent work has turned to parallel drafters such as DFlash, which predict a whole block at once. Another approach uses n-gram retrieval to find an earlier occurrence of the current context suffix and copy the tokens that followed it directly into the draft. We introduce CoPyro, which feeds these copied tokens into a parallel drafter in place of masks. The drafter learns from context which tokens to keep and which to revise as it predicts the block in its usual forward pass. Across eight benchmarks on Qwen3-4B, CoPyro improves overall acceptance length for all four parallel drafters tested, with gains of 2.19-5.08% when trained on 100K samples. With DFlash trained on 710K samples, overall gains range from 1.08% to 2.00% across the tested model sizes and temperatures. Code generation benefits most in both settings. On Qwen3-8B, processing the proposals adds an average of 0.24 ms per decoding round in Transformers.
  </details>

- **[When Do LLM-Elicited Priors Help? A Controlled Audit of Language Models as Priors for Small-Sample Bayesian Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27075)**  `arXiv:2611.27075`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ran Zou, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly asked where to center the coefficients of a small-sample Bayesian regression, but the evidence is unclear: benchmarks are often built to match the prior and models may remember them. We audit this practice with one frontier and one flash-tier model, using hidden feature names, post-cutoff housing data and a pre-specified test that gives features the wrong descriptions. First, the models share their errors (correlation $0.92$--$0.99$ across draws, vendors and paraphrases): averaging $20$ draws is no more accurate than one, and treating draws as independent creates overconfidence. Much of this error comes from reporting each feature's effect alone, not with the others held fixed. Second, against the best classical prior, the LLM center helps clearly on one of four benchmarks (RMSE $-5.8\%$), marginally on two, and hurts mean RMSE on the fourth. Third, the LLM's stated uncertainty is unreliable; letting the data set the prior scale cuts the harm of a wrong center four-fold against a unit-variance prior. A pre-specified external test returned the verdict \textsc{partial}: on $18$ rule-selected problems the center helps on six and hurts on four, and a hard trust gate trusts a harmful prior while an evidence-weighted mixture is safer.
  </details>

- **[NightmareDiscourseBench: A Benchmark Dataset for Fictional Direct Discourse Identification Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27059)**  `arXiv:2611.27059`  `cs.CL`  
  _Claudia Carroll, Gabi Kirilloff, Dashiell Leigh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper we introduce a new benchmark dataset, NightmareDiscourseBench, for testing NLP pipelines that identify forms of direct discourse in English-language fiction. We first review existing publicly available benchmarks (providing summary statistics and reviewing available metadata) for this task, identifying theoretical inconsistency and exclusive focus on speech as primary issues in this domain. We then overview our narrative theory-based framework for classifying and evaluating different forms of direct discourse, and finally describe NightmareDiscourseBench, a new benchmark test set of 12 text samples accompanied by metadata pertinent to the task of direct discourse identification.
  </details>

- **[How Long Will My Language Model Run? A Benchmark for Pre-Execution Runtime Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26875)**  `arXiv:2611.26875`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ashish Molakalapalli, Kangda Wei, Haonan Duan, Alex Carter, Ruihong Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Researchers submitting deep learning jobs to shared GPU clusters need runtime estimates to request time limits, choose hardware, and budget compute. Large language models (LLMs) offer a tempting shortcut: describe the job and ask. We introduce PreRun, a benchmark for predicting deep learning training and inference runtime from information available before execution. PreRun contains 801 training and 1,884 inference records of language and vision models collected on a GPU cluster, paired with workload configurations and explicitly defined timing targets. It also includes 64 text-generation configurations measured under standardized conditions, each in three separate program launches. We compare three LLMs (Qwen3.5-9B, GPT-OSS-120B, and Claude Opus 5.5) with regression baselines and evaluate analytical estimates on a supported forward-pass subset. On 16 held-out generation configurations, the open-weight LLMs underestimate latency by 90-99% on average without examples. Claude's zero-shot mean absolute error is 4.5 times that of a Random Forest fit on 32 configurations. Five labeled examples substantially reduce Claude's generation error, but their benefit varies across timing targets. We will open-source our data and evaluation pipeline upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Know Your Context Bank: Repairing Inconsistency and Measuring Generalization in Context-Aware Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26775)**  `arXiv:2611.26775`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hailong Li, Mohammadreza Pourreza, Tal Neiman, Reza Sherkat, Per Jacobsson, Yannis Papakonstantinou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems increasingly rely on context banks: curated collections of example queries, metric definitions, and domain knowledge that tell a model how an organization writes its queries. In the enterprise, a bank grows over years as many analysts add entries, checked one at a time for correctness (does the SQL answer its own question?) and rarely for consistency with one another. Yet correctness is often loosely defined: a question seldom fixes how an entity is counted or which table defines it, so two entries can each pass review yet resolve the same decision in opposite ways. We call this condition context bank rot. A second question follows: does a system that uses a bank generalize from it, or only reproduce it? Existing benchmarks cannot tell, as they do not control how far a test question sits from the context. We answer both questions by comparing queries decision by decision, with a fast scorer distilled from human-calibrated LLM judges. Turned inward, the comparison diagnoses and repairs a bank. Fine-tuning on a cleaned BIRD-train bank beats a same-size random subset at every epoch (+2.6 points on average), and 92.4% of our removals had their SQL corrected by an independent expert audit, against 45.6% of audited entries. In-context examples that follow one convention beat those following the opposite one by 13.3 points, while a mix is barely better than none. Turned outward, the comparison generates test questions at a controlled distance from a bank, a pipeline we call CAT (Context-Aware Testing). On CAT-BIRD, for retrieval and fine-tuning alike, the benefit of a bank falls step by step with distance and is largely gone once about three quarters of a question's logic is new, and on CAT-Spider the benefit of retrieval likewise shrinks with distance.
  </details>

- **[Family Matters: Language Transfer and Merging for Adapting Small LLMs to Faroese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00810)**  `arXiv:2510.00810`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jenny Kunz, Iben Nyholm Debess, Annika Simonsen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we investigate strategies for adapting small language models to Faroese, a low-resource North Germanic language. Starting from English-pretrained models, we first continue pre-training on other Scandinavian languages, either individually or combined via model merging or multilingual pre-training, before fine-tuning on Faroese. To address the lack of existing Faroese evaluation resources, we construct two new minimal-pair probing benchmarks, one for linguistic acceptability and one for text comprehension, and complement them with human evaluations conducted by native Faroese linguists. Our results show that transfer from related languages improves language modeling and linguistic acceptability, while gains on comprehension probes are inconsistent across model sizes and tuning methods. Icelandic is the strongest single source language for linguistic acceptability, but no single language is reliably best for comprehension. Under full fine-tuning, merging improves on the best single source language in perplexity, outperforms joint multilingual pre-training, and gives the best comprehension scores. With LoRA however, joint multilingual pre-training is the stronger initialization.
  </details>

- **[Portable Active Recovery under Timed and Position-Dependent Visual Outages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27121)**  `arXiv:2611.27121`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Jinyuan Zhang, Shengshuo Jiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a camera blackout depends on physical position rather than elapsed time, stable hovering prevents visual recovery and mission completion. On stereo visual-inertial quadrotors without external localization, an exogenous timed blackout and an endogenous position-dependent dark zone appear identical at loss onset yet require opposite responses: hover-and-wait completes 93/96 timed blackouts but 0/96 spatial outages (13 hard failures), whereas blind continuation traverses many spatial zones yet fails heavily during timed blackouts. We formulate visual-outage recovery as a worst-condition active-observability problem and introduce a portable recovery module at the reference interface of an unchanged host controller with zero retraining. Using deployable telemetry alone, the module coordinates visual-loss detection, jerk-limited braking, bounded backward probing, settled rejoining, height-integral trim, and an optional gated prior-map branch. On a preregistered 48-scene benchmark (1,920 flights), our full MPPI configuration reaches 88.5% [81.2, 94.8] worst-condition completion, outperforming development-selected DATT-L1 by +29.2 pp [17.7, 40.6] and exceeding all six external baselines after Bonferroni correction. Attached without retraining to SimpleFlight, the module lifts worst-condition completion by +17.7 pp [7.3, 28.1] to 86.5% (and 90% across 1.0x/1.25x speeds, 0/80 hard failures). Trace diagnostics reveal that survival hinges on visual-inertial re-acquisition asymmetry at vision return -- not blind IMU drift suppression - and uncover a bias-drift map crossover confirmed on 72 held-out EuRoC windows.
  </details>

- **[Spatiotemporal Graph Neural Networks for Fault Detection and Structural Learning in Chemical Processes: Use Case on the Tennessee Eastman Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27698)**  `arXiv:2611.27698`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Rayane Ammar Khodja, Alexandre Voisin, Victor Costa, Fanny Casteran, Benoit Celse, Benoît Iung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical Fault Detection and Diagnosis (FDD) methods, including many data-driven approaches, assume a static normal operating space and interpret deviations from a fixed reference as fault indicators. When Operating Conditions (OCs) vary over time, this assumption breaks down: legitimate transitions trigger false alarms while moderate faults go undetected. We propose a spatiotemporal Graph Neural Network (GNN) framework that decomposes the normal operating space into OC-specific subspaces linked by transition functions, with a dual learning objective combining reconstruction loss and a Deep SVDD one-class term. It learns adjacency matrices through Graph Attention Networks (GATs) and integrates spatial modelling with temporal encoding to represent process dynamics under evolving OCs. This paper evaluates the framework's foundational components (fault detection, GATv2 spatial graph learning and temporal encoding) on the Tennessee Eastman Process (TEP) benchmark, training only on fault-free data. The architecture achieves competitive detection from reconstruction error alone, with similarity-based feature selection improving both accuracy and graph structure diversity. We then evaluate the physical interpretability of the learned attention matrices against 22 ground-truth sensor pairs derived from the TEP control structure and process topology. GATv2 attention fails to recover all known physical pairs across multiple hyperparameter configurations, suggesting a structural limitation of reconstruction-driven attention rather than a tuning issue. This challenges a common assumption in GNN-based FDD: that learned attention weights provide a basis for fault diagnosis and root-cause analysis. The architecture detects faults effectively, but the learned graph does not encode the physical topology needed for interpretable diagnosis, motivating physics-informed graph construction.
  </details>

- **[Unsatz: Evaluating Scientific Discovery Without a Given Ansatz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03713)**  `arXiv:2611.03713`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ethan Ji, Wei Wang, Yizhou Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Before a scientist fits any parameter, they must decide what the model contains: which quantities exist, which are hidden, how they interact, and how they are measured. Physicists call such a trial form an ansatz. Benchmarks for automated equation discovery mostly supply it, by fixing the variables, a library of candidate terms, or a law to be modified. We introduce Unsatz, a benchmark in which an LLM agent must construct the ansatz itself. Each task is a simulated system, generated from a private seed as a program in a small modeling language: ordinary, delay, or partial differential equations, discrete maps, static and causal models, or stochastic processes, possibly with latent states, distorted sensors, and uninformative measurement channels. The agent buys experiments within a budget, writes candidate programs in the same language, obtains their coefficients from the benchmark's fitter, and is scored only on how its final program predicts experiments it never saw: conditions outside the ordinary ranges, interventions, and longer runs. Across 80 systems in 20 domains, nine language models solve between 21% and 60%. On the 64 deterministic systems, the two strongest agents solve 39 and 31, and classical and neural baselines together 14. The difficulty lies in finding the structure, not in the data: in 55% of the agents' failed investigations, the experiments they bought suffice once the true program is given with unknown coefficients. We also start agents on a related system from the program of a first system, either the one they discovered or the true one; agents holding a correct program solve the related system in 15 of 42 investigations, and agents without one in 5 of 64. Stronger agents make fewer unscientific errors; beyond these, agents that find a system's variables still miss how they act, and prefer revisions that lower the training error to ones that predict better.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Self-Distillation: Measuring and Modeling Acquisition and Retention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39494)**  `arXiv:2609.39494`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Luis Zuin, Alexis Huet, Dario Rossi, Zied Ben Houidi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-distillation with privileged context adapts a language model from demonstrations by letting the model, once conditioned on a reference response, teach its context-free copy token by token. Our taxonomy reveals existing methods differ along three entangled axes: (i) the rollout source (student or teacher), (ii) the teacher coupling (frozen, or an exponential moving average of the student at some coupling rate) and (iii) the token-level divergence (here, reverse or forward KL), yet these axes are usually studied in fixed combinations and have led to conflicting conclusions. We formalize a unifying framework that covers both self-distillation and classic supervised fine-tuning: we train every combination of the three axes, on Qwen2.5-7B and Ministral-3-3B across ordinary and contradictory tasks, totaling 1,200 adaptation runs, to systematically investigate the impact of the above axes. We propose a controlled model of the same objective to interpret the resulting acquisition-retention trade-offs. We find that (i) the rollout source matters mostly where the task contradicts the pretrained behavior: there teacher rollouts raise acquisition (the accuracy gained on the new task) well above what student rollouts achieve, with almost no change in retention (the change in accuracy on general benchmarks); (ii) the teacher coupling changes acquisition most, on every task: acquisition rises with the coupling rate, then falls past a task-specific rate; (iii) switching the KL direction costs retention in one model but not the other, so which axis to tune first depends on the model. The controlled model reproduces the three trends.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Task Self-Evolving Harnesses with the Agent as Its Own Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38372)**  `arXiv:2609.38372`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Qiankai Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A harness is the code around a language model agent that organizes prompts, calls tools, manages context, and controls execution. As models grow stronger, recent work has begun to let agents improve their own harnesses, a line of work known as self-evolving harnesses. In most existing methods, a separate proposer running on a human-designed harness modifies the solver's harness, and a separate harness is evolved for each benchmark. Real-world tasks come from many domains, so both the evolution and the evaluation of a harness should cover a diverse range of tasks. We propose a framework close to recursive self-improvement: the same frozen model, on the same version of the harness, first solves tasks as the solver and then, as the proposer, reads the complete run records and directly edits the harness that runs it. Each evolution batch draws tasks from five benchmarks in different domains. To measure generalization, training and held-out tasks are strictly separated, and we additionally evaluate on five out-of-distribution benchmarks not used in the first stage. The design borrows from deep learning training and has two stages, multi-task pretraining and continual training. Starting from a 49-line seed harness, the harnesses evolved by GLM-5.3 and GPT-5.6 Sol gain about 5 points on the in-distribution benchmarks. They are on par with Claude Code and Codex on these benchmarks, and the GPT-5.6 Sol harness is also on par with Codex on five out-of-distribution benchmarks. The weaker model of each family benefits less: GLM-5.3-Flash gains less than 2 points, and the harness evolved by GPT-5.6 Luna scores below the seed. Continued evolution on Claw-Eval in the second stage further improves GPT-5.6 Sol and GLM-5.3-Flash on its multimodal tasks. We also analyze where the gains come from and the mechanisms that emerged during evolution. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Reward Hackability in Code RL Training Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16062)**  `arXiv:2606.16062`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shreshth Rajan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code RL rewards a policy for passing a task's tests, so a test suite that accepts a wrong patch trains the wrong behavior. We measure how often open code RL environments accept wrong patches and how reliably hackable tasks can be detected and repaired, checking each claim by execution against the task's gold patch. First, 22.4% of 49 SWE-bench Verified tasks accept a Docker-verified patch that behaves differently from the gold patch, and 57.8% against a stronger attacker. The gold-patch check is necessary: an attacker not shown the gold patch returns it for 31% of its passing "exploits". Second, across 134 public SWE-bench submissions, models pass hackable tasks 28.6 pp more often than tasks of the same difficulty, mostly because those tasks are passable at all; pooled over three attacker runs, hackable tasks still exceed matched passable ones (p = 0.014). Third, repair is harder to verify than detection: 61.9% of LLM-written repair tests fail on the gold patch, and even behind a gate an LLM judge is wrong on 38% of the verdicts execution can check. Finally, of 873 training trajectories behind an open coding agent run beside the gold fix, 19.4% answer differently on a case the issue describes; an LLM review that can only demote these executed differences judges 6.9% to be wrong fixes the tests rewarded (6.4-10.5% across three model reviews), and 5.4% in a second public training set (R2E-Gym). Audits and repairs of code RL environments should be decided by execution wherever execution can decide.
  </details>

- **[Belief-State Management Meets Rubric Rewards for Agentic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27286)**  `arXiv:2611.27286`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jingyi Chai, Yun Liao, Hao Lu, Kemeng Zhang, Bingyang Zheng, Yujie Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current large language model (LLM) agents still struggle to forecast real-world events. This stems from two primary limitations: i) standard ReAct harness accumulates reasoning and evidence within one growing context, and ii) outcome-based rewards in forecasting lead to a slow and noisy signal that cannot distinguish sound reasoning from lucky guesses. To address these, we introduce a forecasting training framework including harness design and post-training. For the harness side, we propose Proactive Belief-State Management (PBM), equipping agents with a fold tool to compress lengthy context into compact belief states and reset the context for subsequent exploration. For the training side, we develop SEIPH, a five-dimensional, outcome-blind rubric, providing process-quality rewards. Using the rubric-based reward, we post-train a 35B model with PPO and GRPO, yielding DeepForecaster-PPO and DeepForecaster-GRPO. Theoretically, we prove that idealized fold-based stopping converges and calibrated local rewards reduce segment-level gradient variance. Empirically, DeepForecaster-PPO (35B) achieves the best Brier score on both ForecastBench and Polymarket-Sim, outperforming frontier agents based on DeepSeek-V4-Pro (1.6T), Kimi-K2.6 (1T), and GPT-5.5. DeepForecaster-GRPO ranks second on both FutureX metrics. Ablations reveal that PBM also improves the untrained base model without any training. Calibration analysis shows that DeepForecaster-PPO makes more accurate high-confidence predictions than Qwen3.6 and GPT-5.5. Trajectory analysis further shows that forecasts generally improve from the first to the final belief state as agents gather evidence and revise their judgments.
  </details>

- **[AstronautBench: Measuring Agent Self-Improvement Across Episodes Through Knowledge, Evidence, and Progress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26910)**  `arXiv:2611.26910`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Congyuan Yu, Runping Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are expected to improve themselves from their own experience across episodes, with fixed weights. Self-improvement in an embodied world cannot be read from one measure. Existing benchmarks measure task progress, world-knowledge question answering (QA), and episodic memory, but a correct answer does not show that the agent ever observed the evidence for it, and progress does not show what the agent learned. We introduce AstronautBench, which measures one agent's self-improvement through three views on the same run: what it knows, whether its own evidence supports that knowledge, and how far it gets in the world. It is built on the game Outer Wilds, where each episode is a 22-minute loop after which the world resets, only the agent's knowledge carries over, and progress is gated by knowledge. After every episode, we measure the places reached and ship-log rumor facts revealed, the correctness of answers to 164 questions about the world, and whether each answer is supported by the frames and position the agent cites from its own recorded play. Two LLM judges from different model families check every answer. In eight-episode baselines with GPT-6.1 Sol and Gemini 4 Argon planners, which act through a learned controller or their own gamepad inputs, the three views do not move together, so any one of them alone gives an incomplete picture of whether the agent is improving. The GPT-6.1 Sol agent's correct answers grow from 14.6% to 26.8% of all questions, but at episode 8 only 59 to 68% of them are supported by its cited evidence. It reveals no new rumor facts after episode 3, while the places it reaches and its memory keep growing. More than half of the answers that become correct after episode 3 cite only evidence from earlier episodes.
  </details>

- **[SkillForgeBench: Evaluating Agent Reliability Grounded in Agent Skill Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14918)**  `arXiv:2610.14918`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengxi Li, Junyi Zhou, Qiyuan Zhang, Chuhan Wu, Fuyuan Lyu, Le Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating an agent's ability to strictly satisfy diverse and procedurally complex requirements is essential to determining its practical reliability. Conducting such an evaluation demands agentic tasks that reconcile broad domain coverage with deep procedural complexity. Existing paradigms face a fundamental trade-off: expert-curated benchmarks achieve procedural depth but suffer from domain diversity bottlenecks, whereas crowdsourced benchmarks span diverse scenarios but lack execution rigor. To resolve this tension, we present SkillForgeBench, which leverages agent skills as unifying execution blueprints across three co-designed stages: (1) Resource Preparation uses domain-balanced skills to guide artifact retrieval, establishing broad workflow coverage; (2) Skill-driven Task Synthesis employs skills as procedural anchors to partition artifact roles, synthesize naturalistic task instructions, and package sandboxed scenarios for deep procedural execution; and (3) Holistic Evaluation Design translates skill-embedded requirements into fine-grained completion and process rubrics to enable strict requirement-level scoring. Comprising 842 sandboxed tasks across five major professional domains, SkillForgeBench reveals a steep capability hierarchy among eight mainstream agents, led by Opus 4.8 ($40.9\%$ strict completion). Requiring all constrains satisfaction uncovers severe capability bottlenecks across all model tiers that remain masked under individual rubric pass rates ($70.0\%$ to $85.7\%$ vs. $12.6\%$ to $40.9\%$). Crucially, under the external guidance from skills, top-tier agents obtain larger gains than low-tier agents (i.e., $\Delta 19.2\%$ for Opus 4.8 vs. $\Delta 5.8\%$ for Haiku 4.5). Finally, agents satisfy process rubrics far less frequently than completion rubrics (under $3\%$ for Haiku 4.5), proving that completion-only checks overestimate real-world agent reliability.
  </details>

- **[A Benchmark Protocol and Fairness Analysis Towards Open-Set EEG Seizure Type Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27783)**  `arXiv:2611.27783`  `cs.AI`  
  _Targol Bakhtiarvand, Terrance E. Boult, Jugal Kalita, Adham Atyabi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated EEG seizure type classification systems are typically trained and evaluated under the closed-set assumption, where all seizure types encountered at deployment are seen during training. In clinical practice, this assumption fails: patients present with rare or atypical seizure types that fall outside the training label space, and a system that forces a wrong label is worse than one that abstains. We introduce the first open-set recognition protocol for EEG seizure type classification on the Temple University Hospital Seizure Corpus (TUSZ v2.0.3), defining known and unknown classes using the clinical taxonomy itself: seizure types with sufficient subject representation (at least 12 subjects) form the known set, and rare types form the unknown set. We evaluate seven post-hoc open-set scorers (MSP, MaxLogit, energy, Mahalanobis, kNN, GHOST, and COSTARR) applied to a partially fine-tuned EEG Foundation Model (EFM), using a subject-disjoint leave-one-class-out (LOCO) protocol over three seeds that tests whether methods can detect seizure types excluded from fine-tuning. On true unknown classes, the best scorer (Mahalanobis) reaches a mean AUROC of 0.66, and differences among the top scorers are within seed-to-seed variation. Held-out known seizure types from unseen subjects are detected only slightly above chance (AUROC 0.54-0.58 for all scorers). Per-subject rejection rates are highly uneven for all scorers, and GHOST does not significantly improve per-subject fairness over energy scoring (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank, p >= 0.25). We release the protocol, subject splits, and code as an initial baseline; our results indicate that open-set seizure type recognition remains a hard, open problem.
  </details>

- **[QuASAR: Toward an LLM-Assisted Framework for Screening, Prototyping, and Assessing Quantum-Advantage Opportunities in Scientific Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27778)**  `arXiv:2611.27778`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mark L. Carroll, Jordan A. Caraballo-Vega, Brian P. Powell, John L. Schnase_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Identifying scientific computations that could benefit from quantum algorithms requires both domain knowledge and fluency in quantum algorithm design, expertise that rarely resides in one person. We report results from QuASAR, an experimental, two-agent, LLM-driven pipeline intended to bridge this gap. One agent screens scientific literature for computational kernels with a plausible quantum-algorithmic analogue and proposes candidates, each backed by citations. A second agent independently verifies those citations. For candidates that survive verification, it reconstructs the source's computational structure, formulates a quantum mapping, builds and executes a prototype, and benchmarks it against classical baselines. We applied this pipeline to ten candidate computations drawn from the astrophysics literature. Nine candidates were carried through to a small-scale, benchmarked prototype. We report three in detail, chosen as the clearest examples with the least redundancy across the set. They span combinatorial optimization, stochastic simulation, and Bayesian inference. The pipeline attempted to supply quantum-design expertise a domain scientist might lack, reconstructing source algorithms where one existed and building original, disclosed mappings where none did. None of the three case studies achieved quantum advantage at the tested scales, and their astrophysical framing has not yet undergone expert validation. What the experiment does show is that the pipeline can turn a published idea into a runnable prototype, which a scientist can then inspect, test against classical methods, and decide whether to pursue. QuASAR is an exploratory framework, offered to the community for evaluation, critique, and further development, with an approach that could broaden the use of quantum computing in scientific research and extend to computational improvements more generally.
  </details>

- **[Can Vision-Language Models Synthesize Executable Game-Playing Programs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27741)**  `arXiv:2611.27741`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Tong, Jiaxin Qi, Dingyu Zhang, Jianqiang Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language models (VLMs) have endowed multimodal agents with increasingly strong visual perception and long-horizon reasoning capabilities, making VLM-based autonomous game playing feasible. Existing game benchmarks primarily evaluate whether an agent can select appropriate actions from the current visual state across different scenarios and games. However, most of them adopt a step-by-step interaction paradigm where the current visual observation is sent to the agent before each action. In practical deployment, this paradigm introduces two fundamental limitations: the game loop must wait for model inference, making real-time interaction impractical, and repeated VLM calls incur substantial cost. Therefore, we propose PlayScriptBench, a script-based game benchmark that evaluates whether VLMs can synthesize an executable game-playing program from game states. This setting jointly evaluates visual grounding, understanding of game dynamics, long-horizon strategic reasoning, and executable program synthesis, providing a more comprehensive assessment of game-playing agents. To support more tractable evaluation for current general-purpose VLMs, we further introduce a four-stage pipeline --- Explore, Perception, API Construction, and Strategy Composition --- that decomposes controller synthesis into game exploration, state perception, interface construction, and strategic control, enabling agents to progressively construct an executable program. Experiments on 16 games spanning 4 genres show that even strong VLMs struggle on our benchmark, revealing a substantial gap between passive visual game understanding and executable closed-loop game control. These results establish script-based controller synthesis as a complementary direction for benchmarking VLM-based game-playing agents.
  </details>

- **[Comparative Performance of Machine Learning Architectures for Fault Detection and Diagnosis in Chemical Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27696)**  `arXiv:2611.27696`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rayane Ammar Khodja, Alexandre Voisin, Victor Costa, Benoit Celse, Fanny Casteran, Benoît Iung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fault Detection and Diagnosis (FDD) is crucial for both maintenance and control in chemical industries, where early fault detection can prevent costly failures and optimize operations. This is particularly critical in pilot units like those at IFP Energies Nouvelles (IFPEN), which operate under short-term experimental conditions with frequently varying operational parameters. This study conducts an extensive benchmarking analysis using the Tennessee Eastman Process (TEP), a widely used simulated chemical process dataset, to evaluate multiple approaches for fault detection and diagnosis, featuring numerous continuous operations with different sensors and faults. Several methods were implemented and compared including Multi Scale PCA (MS-PCA), AutoEncoder, Ensemble Learning, and LSTM models for fault detection, alongside Random Forest, XGBoost, and BLSTM (Bidirectional LSTM) for fault diagnosis. Our results demonstrate that Ensemble Learning achieves detection rates ranging from 80% to 100% across various fault scenarios. For the fault diagnosis task, BLSTM achieved a diagnosis accuracy of 98.76%. The study reveals that ensemble-based approaches consistently outperform individual models in handling the complex, multivariate nature of chemical process data, due to its robustness in combining multiple perspectives, comprehensive data capture, and localized detection capabilities. Furthermore, the superior performance of the BLSTM is due to its ability to capture both past and future temporal dependencies in the sequential data, particularly important in chemical processes where fault patterns may manifest with complex temporal relationships. This research contributes to the field of Fault Detection and Diagnosis by providing empirical evidence for the effectiveness of ensemble methods and Bidirectional LSTMs in order to address industrial FDD applications on chemical processes.
  </details>

- **[Learning Inside Types: Epistemic Typestates for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27631)**  `arXiv:2611.27631`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lorenzo Bacchiani, Mario Bravetti, Giovanni Pau, Roberto Girau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Type disciplines make programs safe by construction: components checked against each other's types compose into programs that cannot go wrong in the ways the types exclude. Types suit reinforcement learning: a type constrains what an agent may choose, not how it chooses, so the guarantee holds for every policy a learner produces. We introduce epistemic typestates, a type discipline for safe multi-agent reinforcement learning. An agent's type is a protocol whose states record its beliefs, the worlds it cannot rule out from its own observations and actions, and permits only actions that are safe in every world of the belief, whatever the others do within their public types. Agents learn inside their types, which are built and checked one agent at a time and run without communication. We prove type soundness and team safety, give a synthesis that is sound and complete, build types from guards when worlds are infinite, and show that shields are a special case. All proofs are mechanised in Rocq. On nine benchmarks, no typed learner causes a safety violation, and task performance does not differ significantly from that of two recent multi-agent shielding methods. Where a contract-based method fails at five and ten agents, types for ten agents are built in under a second. In the two continuous benchmarks, Lagrangian learners cause thousands of violations in every training run.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarks' Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27622)**  `arXiv:2611.27622`  `cs.AI`  
  _James Zhu, Zekki Cat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public benchmarks guide model selection, post-training rewards and claims of progress in artificial intelligence, yet leaderboards report aggregate scores without verifying whether each task's prompt, environment and grader agree on what counts as success. How often that agreement fails in contemporary evaluations, and in which direction confirmed defects move scores, has remained unmeasured across grading designs. Here we synthesize task-level quality reviews of 749 tasks across eight frontier benchmarks and 767 public model evaluations spanning closed-form question answering, document rubrics and agentic execution to assess whether prompts, environments and graders align. Task validity stratifies sharply by grading architecture. Closed-form benchmarks revised after public error reporting reach 92.5% verified validity, compared with 29.3% across six multi-step suites that nevertheless already supply 161 verified complex tasks. Rubric suites fail through mismatches between prompts and reference checklists, whereas execution harnesses add incomplete state assertions, shared-channel leaks and grading-time collisions. Across confirmed defects, 71.7% withhold credit from compliant solutions while 28.3% permit shortcut passes, indicating that raw leaderboard ceilings understate frontier capability on many practical tasks. Grading committed patches inside a fresh container cuts confirmed runtime bypasses from 14.7% in shared containers to 2.7% (a 5.5x drop, and 0% in read-only or static suites), showing how targeted maintenance restores existing benchmarks at low cost. As the initial milestone of the long-term Benchmarks' Last Exam initiative, this synthesis launches sustained expansion across wider benchmark coverage and deeper evaluation-quality dimensions so that pursuing leaderboard gains directly advances genuine model capability.
  </details>

- **[Altair-Nidhogg: Which Model-Merging Theories Hold for a One-Pass Decision Encoder? Soups, Task Arithmetic, TIES and DARE Against Joint Training and Prediction Ensembling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27550)**  `arXiv:2611.27550`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Navin Kumar R, Akhil P_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Laya is a small model that answers a multiple-choice question in one pass: each option has a name and a written description. A new skill costs a training run, so we test whether published recipes for merging fine-tuned models (weight averaging, task arithmetic, TIES, DARE) work for it, and whether the theories behind them hold. All 156 models start from one released checkpoint: four plain fine-tunes, five specialists (each adds one kind of training row), joint fine-tunes on all rows, and prediction ensembles. Items are held-out, synthetic and LLM-labelled. In-family, each skill is scored on new items of its own kind, and "kept" is the share of a specialist's gain over the plain fine-tune. Averaging four specialists keeps 52% [38, 64] with +1.7 points of general accuracy; adding the full vectors keeps 42% [3, 63] with -9.0; TIES, which trims and resolves sign conflicts, keeps 64% [50, 76] with +1.5; joint fine-tunes, which see all the rows, keep 120% [104, 162] and 116% [104, 149]. DARE does not repair the full sum (-0.4 points), and ensembles of the models' predictions keep 26% to 54% depending on how they are combined. Swapping the seed of one specialist moves the average by up to 8 points (23 for the full sum). Of 10 predictions fixed in advance, 1 held, 1 held partly, 7 failed and 1 could not be tested. Out-of-family (benchmark questions, code-mixed tickets), the four-way average, joint models and ensemble stay within 2.6 points of the plain fine-tune, but full-vector sums lose up to 7.7. Labels are LLM-made, data synthetic, one base model.
  </details>

- **[Jagged Robustness: Uneven Safety Transfer and Emerging Vulnerabilities under LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27424)**  `arXiv:2611.27424`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ramneek Agnihotri, Yingte Xu, Hans Vath_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization enables efficient inference in language models through model parameter compression. This work audits the impact of quantization on capability and safety domains including model safeguards, instruction-following, ethics and bias. Through benchmarks, we find that model safeguards and bias can degrade under quantization, while other capabilities such as truthfulness remain more stable in Qwen3-4B. We define this uneven transfer of LLM capabilities under quantization as Jagged Robustness. We find multiple factors driving Jagged Robustness: task complexity, evaluation method and generation coherence. Moreover, we verify the degradation of model safeguards by observing increased compliance with unsafe requests under quantization, and demonstrate universal adversarial suffix attacks that are benign for the full model but jailbreak quantized models. The findings indicate that it is risky to assume that the performance and robustness can transfer evenly across different capability domains from full models to quantized models.
  </details>

- **[Evidence sufficiency assessment enables cost-effective review allocation in on-premise clinical AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27390)**  `arXiv:2611.27390`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwen Qiu, Che Sun, Shu Shen, Wentao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-premise clinical AI agents must auto-accept high-confidence diagnoses, deferring others to clinician review, yet token probabilities and self-reported confidence miss errors, while more reliable five-run consistency incurs fivefold compute. We propose training-free decision-point evidence sufficiency assessment and a three-tier sequential triage protocol. Masking reasoning and diagnosis, the same 4B model scores sufficiency from objective evidence via prior-debiased probabilities over five ordered levels. Calibration freezes a nominal top-50% threshold, selecting single-run, five-run consistency, or full-deferral tiers by whether the one-sided 95% Wilson lower confidence bound of pass rate among accepted cases meets the 95% clinical target line. Across 1,605 MIRA-v2, CDM, VivaBench, and in-hospital liver malignancy primary cohort cases, MIRA-v2 single-run top-50% pass rate reached 98.91%, matching five-run consistency, saving ~80% compute; CDM achieved 96.00% test attainment by escalating to the five-run consistency tier; cross-specialty, missing-imaging, and combined enriched cohorts entered the full-deferral tier, capping calibration false-certification rate at 0.00%-3.50%.
  </details>

- **[WePM: Hierarchical Conflict Reconciliation for Unified MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27365)**  `arXiv:2611.27365`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiqiang Yuan, Qiwei Yan, Liwei Yang, Jinzhe Liu, Yang Liu, Zhongyan Hou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bringing multimodal intelligence from the cloud to users' devices keeps sensitive data local and reduces inference cost, but deploying a specialized model per capability duplicates vision-language capacity under tight memory and compute budgets. We formalize heterogeneous capability density (HCD), measuring how broadly and effectively a model serves heterogeneous task families per unit of resident inference memory. High HCD across representation, generation, and decision raises two challenges: these capabilities need visual evidence at different spatial granularities under a fixed token budget, and separately collected task corpora yield conflicting updates to shared parameters, while cross-task paired supervision is costly and ambiguous for open-ended inputs. We present WePM, a family of pocket models unifying representation, generation, and decision within one vision-language backbone. Multi-Granularity Visual Budgeting (MGVB) allocates a fixed visual-token budget across global, regional, and fine-grained banks; Unified Listwise Knowledge Distillation (ULKD) enriches separately collected data with soft teacher guidance, avoiding cross-task paired annotation; Stable and Efficient Multitask Optimization (SEMO) regulates each task's contribution through explicit loss weights and exposure schedules, with sequential gradient accumulation under memory constraints. In the joint representation-generation setting, WePM outperforms all baselines across five evaluation groups, raising the Joint score from 38.15 to 52.74. In single-task evaluation, WePM-4B attains the best average score over 30 embedding, 39 generation, and seven typed-decision benchmarks (72.86, 62.41, 61.11). Against the separate-model stack with the highest HCD, WePM achieves slightly higher balanced performance with 61.9% less resident weight memory, a 2.64-fold higher HCD.
  </details>

- **[GRACE: Controlled Evidence Interventions for Diagnosing and Improving Knowledge Integration in GraphRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27350)**  `arXiv:2611.27350`  `cs.AI`  
  _Minh Pham, Quang Nguyen, Phong Kieu, Cong Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GraphRAG combines a language model's parametric knowledge with retrieved graph evidence, yet aggregate accuracy cannot show whether failures stem from missing paths, distractors, or conflicts with model knowledge. We introduce GRACE, a benchmark built by executing LC-QuAD 2.0 queries over Wikidata and applying four controlled interventions to each evidence subgraph: severing a support path, adding type-compatible distractors, attaching high-degree hubs, and replacing the supported answer with a counterfactual entity. Questions and option positions remain fixed across the five states. The validated benchmark contains 4,028 questions and 20,140 question-graph pairs. These comparisons reveal that question-only routing cannot distinguish evidence states, while relevance guidance can improve answerable states yet hurt when the support path is severed: relevant facts may not jointly suffice to answer. We propose QCFSE-SA, which combines typed local attention, global graph attention, and question-conditioned evidence guidance with a calibrated graph-support gate. With the factual context and generator held fixed, it improves controlled-state accuracy from 57.17% to 62.78% (+5.61 points; question-clustered 95% CI [+5.04, +6.17]) and broken-state accuracy from 55.41% to 67.85%. Over-abstention on semantic distractors remains an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[EcomWriteBench: A Real-World Benchmark for E-commerce Writing Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27349)**  `arXiv:2611.27349`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaicheng Liang, Jingyi Cui, Hongwei Wen, Feng Wang, Simiu Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used throughout e-commerce workflows to generate content that informs purchasing decisions and supports customer interactions. Evaluating these systems requires accounting for user needs, product evidence, structural constraints, and business requirements that are not fully captured by general-purpose writing benchmarks. We introduce EcomWriteBench, a real-world benchmark containing 3,861 queries from 69 e-commerce writing tasks across 13 categories and five languages. Through task-level extraction, cross-task consolidation, and refinement guided by human feedback, EcomWriteBench constructs a shared pool of 37 abilities and identifies the subset applicable to each task. Ability-wise scoring enables us to compare state-of-the-art LLMs on EcomWriteBench and characterize their relative strengths and weaknesses across fine-grained e-commerce writing abilities. We further propose a preference-calibrated weight-learning framework that converts human pairwise preferences into interpretable scoring rubrics through global, category-level, and task-level aggregation weights. This framework provides a reusable approach for translating human preferences into automated evaluation by combining ability discovery, ability-wise scoring, and preference-calibrated aggregation, and can potentially be applied to a broad range of benchmarks beyond EcomWriteBench. On held-out queries, our scoring framework achieves 88.96% agreement with human preferences, compared with 78.54% for conventional LLM-as-a-judge scoring.
  </details>

- **[GGDriver: Glance at What Is Shared, Gaze at What Differs for Perception-Free Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27330)**  `arXiv:2611.27330`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiawei Sun, Maosen Li, Jiamin Shi, Xiaoxu Zheng, Jiun Tian Hoe, Yechao Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A planner deciding whether to turn left or continue straight needs to inspect different parts of the scene for each maneuver. Yet many end-to-end driving planners evaluate every candidate trajectory against the same compressed scene representation. We let each candidate select the visual evidence used to refine and score it. A global glance first produces trajectory hypotheses from shared scene features. We then project a corridor around each trajectory into the camera views and use the resulting mask to attend to patch features at the backbone's native resolution. After refinement, the scorer projects each final proposal again and ranks it using evidence along its own path. This geometric selection requires only the predicted trajectory and camera calibration. On three planners with different scene representations (learned registers, a perception-supervised bird's-eye-view grid, and a coarse image-token grid), adding this gaze improves planning scores. In ablations, path-aligned patches outperform both a matched number of randomly selected patches and attention over all patches. Using the gaze for ranking yields larger gains than using it for refinement alone. With a ViT-S backbone and no perception labels, GGDriver achieves a rater feedback score of 8.110 on the WOD-E2E test set, 94.2 PDMS on NAVSIM-v1, 48.6 EPDMS on NAVSIM-v2, and a closed-loop Driving Score of 72.18 on Bench2Drive.
  </details>

- **[$γ$-Scape: An Agent-Centric World for Mobile Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27306)**  `arXiv:2611.27306`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaotao Zhang, Lin Qian, Hao Zhang, Xuanrui Cui, Yuehao Huang, Yunzi Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent embodied agents construct and adapt robot behavior through executable programs, tool use, and interaction feedback. Studying these systems in mobile manipulation calls for physical worlds that connect agent-directed execution with its physical consequences and reusable interaction experience. We introduce $\gamma$-Scape, an agent-centric simulation and benchmark platform for mobile embodied intelligence. Agents compose motion tools and learned skills to carry out complete tasks that combine navigation and manipulation in a persistent physical world. BenchForge grounds structured objectives in physical task instances with instructions, reference programs, and goal evaluators, defining task goals while preserving agents' choice of valid solutions. An agentic collection harness further enables agents to produce demonstrations by carrying out contact-rich interactions through sustained control and physical feedback. Assisted reference execution and human guidance provide complementary experience. The resulting tool traces and aligned observation-action records support program refinement and policy learning, with common task-goal criteria for subsequent evaluation. We benchmark representative agent systems on complete tasks and demonstrate contact collection and trace-informed program revision. Together, these workflows provide infrastructure for research on embodied recursive self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[Bel: Step-Level Policy Improvement of Visual Agents Without Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27222)**  `arXiv:2611.27222`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Ziwen Wang, Zhaoyu Zhu, Changyi Yang, Neil He, Chaoran Cheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A visual agent acts on what it sees: it taps and types on a screen, or points on a camera image to show a robot where to act. One wrong step can fail its task, yet the right action is often among its own samples, passed over by its greedy decode. Choosing it is a visual judgment, since an action is best judged where it acts, not by its text or coordinates alone: as text, a tap is two numbers; drawn on the screen, it is the Post button of an empty draft. We find that this is decisive: given the screenshot and a tap's coordinates, a decision model chooses among GUI-R1-3B's samples significantly worse on average than when the tap is drawn where it lands. Bel turns this finding into a method that trains nothing and needs only a frozen policy's samples and an image on which each action can be drawn. It marks each candidate on its own copy of the image it acts on, asks a decision model for the probability that executing it is the right decision, so that every kind of action shares one scale, and acts on another candidate only when greedy scores below a threshold and trails it by a margin. On recorded GUI steps, for each of three policies, Bel significantly beats greedy decoding on mobile, web and desktop screens alike and, on the same candidates, gives each a significantly higher mean step success than voting, CATTS, the trained GUI-Critic-R1 and two prompted Qwen3-VL judges shown the same marks; it also significantly improves the much larger Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B on every step-level test set. With GUI-R1-3B's thresholds unchanged, Bel significantly raises live AndroidWorld and MiniWoB++ task success for two other policies and robot-pointing success for three policies on each of two groups of benchmarks. Judging each action where it acts thus gives step-level policy improvement of visual agents without training, with significant gains for GUI agents and robot pointing.
  </details>

- **[MAPES: Multi-Value Alignment Pipeline via Expert Specialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27133)**  `arXiv:2611.27133`  `cs.AI`  
  _SungHo Kim, SangKeun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences requires simultaneously satisfying multiple, often competing values. Existing preference optimization methods collapse these into a single implicit signal, and even multi-objective extensions optimize within a shared parameter space, leaving cross-value conflicts unresolved. We propose MAPES, a multi-value alignment paradigm in which conflict resolution is a first-class concern at every pipeline stage: it first decomposes supervision into value-specific experts trained in isolated parameter subspaces, then recomposes them via preference-aware value merging and a lightweight harmonization stage that aligns the merged model with the expert ensemble. Across five preference alignment benchmarks, MAPES outperforms strong direct preference optimization (DPO) variants and multi-objective baselines on the benchmark-level average, with no large degradation on GSM8K and GPQA; matched controls isolate the contribution of each stage.
  </details>

- **[CoEvoBench: Can LLMs Drive Recursive Co-Evolution of Agentic Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27116)**  `arXiv:2611.27116`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chuan Xiao, Baojie Wang, Haizhao Fan, Hongzhe Du, Xander Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of an LLM agent depends on both its model and its harness, the code that mediates its interaction with tools and environments. Can LLM researchers drive this co-evolution as a recursive improvement process, deciding round after round which component to change when each change alters the value of the other? Existing benchmarks evaluate post-training or data research with the harness out of scope, or harness optimization with the model fixed, leaving this joint decision unmeasured. We introduce CoEvoBench, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs as researchers co-evolving the model and harness of a target agent across five agentic domains. Researchers start from a common base model and initial harness, train and revise within a 24-hour budget, and submit a model-harness pair that is scored on separate test tasks. Across five frontier researchers, the strongest closes more than four fifths of the gap to an instruction-tuned reference system, and co-evolution succeeds on interaction tasks but rarely on long-horizon coding. These results suggest that current LLMs can make a strong first improvement, but cannot yet turn feedback from an improved system into the next one. CoEvoBench provides a controlled testbed for measuring the research capabilities that recursive co-evolution requires.
  </details>

- **[Decision Models Are General-Purpose Process Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27068)**  `arXiv:2611.27068`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiajun Fan, Qinglong Zheng, Ziwen Wang, Bangji Yang, Neil He, Ruihan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) from verifiable outcomes gives a language agent one reward per episode: every step is credited alike, and nothing about the task is learned when all rollouts fail. The last mile, step-level credit, usually requires step labels, a learned critic, costly LLM judges or hand-written rules. We introduce DM-PRM, which turns a general-purpose decision model -- one answering yes/no questions with probabilities -- into a general-purpose process reward model (PRM), zero-shot: no step labels, no fine-tuning, no critic. DM-PRM asks one question per step and adds the standardised answers to the advantage.
