It plugs into any policy-gradient method; one model, with one question text per environment, serves household tasks, web shopping and multiple RL methods. With Qwen2.5-1.5B it lifts GRPO by over twenty points on ALFWorld and over ten on WebShop, overtakes GiGPO on ALFWorld and learns in far fewer iterations; it also improves GiGPO and GraphGPO on both benchmarks and reaches the highest success of our comparison, ahead of critic-based PPO and of state-of-the-art GiGPO, HGPO and GraphGPO. Its scores locate useful steps in failed rollouts, and trained agents drop idle actions and explore instead of circling. DM-PRM shows that step credit need not be learned: it can be asked for.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Token Merging for Efficient Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27064)**  `arXiv:2611.27064`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chris Gu, Marco Andono Sie, Nathan Zhou, Christopher Potts, Julie Kallini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) represent each image with hundreds of visual tokens, which can make inference costly even when a prompt depends on only part of the image. Dynamic Token Merging (DTM), introduced in MrT5 for byte-level text encoders, learns which tokens to remove: a lightweight delete gate scores contextualized hidden states, acts as a differentiable attention bias during training, and physically removes tokens at inference. We argue that DTM is a general token deletion framework rather than a byte-level technique, and extend it to the vision modality and to decoder-only models. Applying DTM to T5Gemma 2 and Gemma 3 yields V-MrT5Gemma2, which deletes visual tokens inside a bidirectional multimodal encoder, and V-MrGemma3, which compacts the prompt and its key-value caches during decoder-only prefill. Across ten benchmarks, DTM's advantage over attention-based and random deletion grows with the deletion rate and is largest on document, chart, and scene-text reading. At 80% visual token deletion (approximately 60% fewer FLOPs), DTM raises accuracy over FastV from 51.8 to 63.0 on ChartQA and from 63.3 to 67.8 on TextVQA with V-MrT5Gemma2, as well as from 62.2 to 68.8 and from 71.2 to 75.1 with V-MrGemma3, retaining 90-96% of unpruned performance on both tasks with significant efficiency gains. These results support DTM as a general framework for efficient transformers.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Verbalized Evaluation Awareness and Refusal through Neuron-Level Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27032)**  `arXiv:2611.27032`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyuan Feng, Gouki Minegishi, Andrew Gambardella, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large reasoning models (LRMs) become more capable, reliable evaluation becomes essential. LRMs can sometimes recognize that they are being evaluated, a phenomenon known as \emph{evaluation awareness}. This phenomenon raises concerns about whether safety behavior, particularly refusal behavior, generalizes from evaluation to deployment. Prior work manipulates \emph{verbalized evaluation awareness (VEA)} through activation steering and observes higher refusal rates when VEA is present, but the mechanisms underlying this connection remain poorly understood. We identify \emph{VEA neurons} by contrasting their activations in VEA and non-VEA reasoning segments. Intervening on 5,000 VEA neurons reduces the VEA rate from 12.6--51.8\% to at most 1.4\% across the model benchmark combinations in our main experiments, and lowers a probe score for unverbalized evaluation awareness (unVEA). In QwQ-32B, VEA-neuron intervention also reduces refusal even in rollouts without VEA; VEA text itself explains only 4--10\% of the decrease. We further show that their down-projection vectors contain refusal components, and preserving these components during intervention partially restores refusal while maintaining VEA suppression, accounting for about half of the refusal decrease and providing a mechanistic explanation for the connection between evaluation awareness and refusal. Finally, across the OLMo 3 SFT, DPO, and RLVR checkpoints, the VEA rate on SorryBench and StrongREJECT is similar after SFT and DPO but substantially higher after RLVR, alongside an increase in the number of VEA neurons. Together, these findings show that VEA text itself has a limited effect on refusal, and that VEA neurons integrate evaluation awarenss and refusal.
  </details>

- **[Same Facts, Different Decision: Fact-Invariant Manipulation and Provenance-Based Defense in Typed Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27016)**  `arXiv:2611.27016`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yujie Wang, Haotian Chi, Shunrong Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed decision models such as TypeSafe's Jev answer only with a probability, an option, or a score, so software can act on them directly. Their inputs, however, often include text from outsiders. Prior attacks test this risk poorly. Injected instructions rarely work on these models, and added facts can make a changed decision correct. We show that outside comments can persuade these models to make a wrong decision without adding any fact. We propose LEVER, an attack that writes such comments with four levers from argumentation theory, uses a separate verifier to discard any comment that adds a fact, and searches for effective comments guided by the model's output probabilities. We further propose the Provenance Gate, a defense that accepts a changed decision only if the model reaches it again without the outside comments. On three benchmarks, LEVER flips about a third of Jev's correct decisions, 2.8 times as many as the strongest prior attack under the same limit on facts, and more on five other models. The gate blocks all of these attacks with at most one extra query and still accepts trusted updates.
  </details>

- **[Reward Integrity in Adversarial Reinforcement Learning for LLM Guardrail Red-Teaming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26998)**  `arXiv:2611.26998`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chengming Liu, Leon Viti, Hongxu Chen, Erdem Kemik, Mary-Anne Williams, Ruth Oliver_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study reward fidelity in an adversarial reinforcement-learning loop for red-teaming financial-services language-model guardrails. Apparent wins can arise when an attack omits prescribed personally identifiable information (PII), or when a reward judge credits a response that did not break the targeted policy. On attacker-generated and public benchmark examples, TypeSafe Jev checks whether the prescribed PII is present. For outcome scoring, we compare the decision models TypeSafe Jev, Laya (pretrained and post-trained), and pretrained CLM-8B with logged Opus 4.8 judgements from a replay corpus. Post-training markedly improves Laya's agreement with the judge. A separate author-reviewed off-target set establishes that Opus can award false wins. The findings motivate specialised decision-model judges for checking prescribed PII in attack inputs and scoring target responses against the intended policy.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolving Beyond Human-Crafted Experience for EDA Scripting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26912)**  `arXiv:2611.26912`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Liu, Shengbo Tong, Xiyuan Chen, Shiwei Hou, Sen Yuan, Hao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing complexity of electronic systems demands efficient design flows, yet EDA scripting remains a bottleneck: proprietary APIs are sparsely documented, and errors often appear as silent failures or cryptic exceptions. Hand-crafted knowledge helps but is difficult to maintain as toolchains evolve. We present ZhuLong-SE (Self-Evolution), an execution-grounded LLM agent that learns reusable knowledge from prior task traces. Building on ZhuLong, it enhances the task-level harness with API retrieval, documentation responses, isolated sandbox execution, and static checking, then adds governed cross-task self-evolution. Beyond solving individual tasks, ZhuLong-SE distills past execution traces into a three-tier memory store governed by ground-truth anchoring, disconfirmation filtering, held-out rollback, and anti-leakage retrieval. On the 158-task EDA-Eval-PyAether benchmark, the full task-level harness, with self-evolution disabled, achieves 92.4% pass@1 and outperforms ZhuLong on all five tested LLM backbones. On a harder instance-disjoint test set, self-evolution from the bare configuration surpasses a human-crafted-experience baseline by 2.5 percentage points (80.0% vs. 77.5% pass@1).
  </details>

- **[Beyond Demographic Discrimination: Benchmarking Normative-Descriptive Calibration in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26870)**  `arXiv:2611.26870`  `cs.AI`  
  _Radhika Bhargava, Shinibali Bhattacharyya, George Mihaila, Jay Prajapati, Anwesan Pal, Yue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in socially consequential settings, where they must distinguish between normative reasoning (what should happen) and descriptive reasoning (what is likely to happen). Most fairness benchmarks evaluate demographic discrimination under a single reasoning objective, and none we know of tests whether a model treats the same item differently when asked to describe an outcome versus decide one. We introduce normative--descriptive calibration, the extent to which a model's differentiation between matched candidates depends on whether it is asked to predict or to decide, and measure it with the Normative--Descriptive Calibration Benchmark (NDCB): 697 matched-pair scenarios across six domains, each posed under both framings, and three metrics, Normative Engagement Rate (NER), Normative Leakage Rate (NLR), and Normative--Descriptive Separation (NDS). Across six LLMs, NDS separates 13 of 15 model pairs, against 7 for BBQ accuracy and none for BOLD, although these counts also reflect each benchmark's sample size. Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Claude Opus 4.6 separate the framings most strongly (average NDS $= +0.55$ and $+0.52$, with overlapping confidence intervals), whereas GPT-5.5's average NDS is negative ($-0.14$) under the single-pick instruction and near zero ($+0.02$) without it. In a simplified prompt template, removing single trigger words never changes the sign of a model's average NDS but can shift it by up to 0.46 and reorder models. Reversing candidate order reveals a further effect: when forced to choose between identical candidates, all six models favor whichever is listed first, and once order is averaged out their demographic-aligned lean is toward the marginalized candidate. Describing disparities and prescribing them are separable behaviors that differ across models.
  </details>

- **[ChatAtom: A Multi-Agent LLM System for Molecular Dynamics Simulation and Visualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26782)**  `arXiv:2611.26782`  `cs.AI`  
  _Trupti Mohanty, Ming Du, Mitchell A. Wood, Brad L. Boyce, Remi Dingreville, Mathew J. Cherukara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular dynamics (MD) simulation resolves atomic-scale motion and predicts the structural, mechanical, and dynamic properties of materials, making it cen- tral to materials design and discovery. Yet an MD study requires an expert-driven workflow: designing the simulation, writing reliable input scripts, executing them, and analyzing the results with separate tools. Here we present ChatAtom, a multi-agent large language model system that translates a natural-language request into a complete MD workflow without the user writing input scripts or analysis code. Each agent has a defined responsibility within the workflow. An orchestrator plans the study; the LAMMPS and OVITO agents each use a generator-reviewer pair to prepare and review simulation and visualization scripts before execution; a high-performance computing agent dispatches jobs to remote resources; and an analysis agent interprets the completed run. The work- flow is accessible through a browser interface that streams thermodynamic data and rendered frames during local execution, allowing users to follow the simu- lation as it progresses. The architecture also supports new capabilities through loadable skills without modifying the agents, enabling autonomous hypothesis generation and testing. We evaluate ChatAtom through script-generation bench- marks, an end-to-end study of crack propagation, and a closed-loop campaign. By bringing script generation, execution, visualization, and analysis into one reviewed and observable pipeline, ChatAtom lowers the barrier to conducting MD studies and broadens access to advanced simulation workflows.
  </details>

- **[Coherence by Construction: Synthesizing CTI Knowledge Graphs and the Formal Limits of Coherence Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26717)**  `arXiv:2611.26717`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tanzimul Alam Fahim, Mohammad Arif Hossain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthesizing relational training data such as knowledge graphs with a large language model typically emits structured objects one at a time, which keeps each object valid but leaves the relationships between them ungrounded. We study an alternative paradigm, generation by simulation: rather than describing a relational artifact, we simulate a process that produces it and take the artifact to be its causal record. Language-model agents propose intent and a deterministic referee resolves outcomes against an explicit world model, so a relation is emitted only when the event it represents occurs, making coherence a property of construction. We instantiate the paradigm for cyber threat intelligence knowledge graphs in the STIX 2.1 standard with three engines, among them an evolutionary engine applying language-model genetic operators to structured graph genomes, sharing a deterministic compiler that makes malformed and dangling output unrepresentable. We prove referential closure, causal grounding, and identifier stability, an ablation localizes each to the referee and world model rather than the policy, and two independent LLM judges reproduce the rubric's engine ranking. Our central result is cautionary, and we prove it: a validator reading any graph feature set a structure-matched random graph can reproduce is uninformative about coherence for a coherence-by-construction generator. Using Erdős-Rényi controls we realize the theorem four times, as structural separation, embedding transfer, inductive link prediction, and real-data augmentation each reward a triviality rather than coherence; we give the mechanism behind each and the conditions a valid benchmark must meet.
  </details>

- **[LLM GerryMandering: Separating Interactions from Additive Effects in LLM Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26538)**  `arXiv:2611.26538`  `cs.AI`  
  _George Rahul, Ronit Jalihal, Bhaskara Hanuma Vedula, Ponnurangam Kumaraguru, Pushkar Shukla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intersectional bias arises when a model treats people who hold several marginalised identities at once differently from what their separate identities would predict. Auditing such intersectional subgroups remains a challenge: their number grows quadratically, and there is no agreed baseline for what independent attributes would predict. Without such a baseline, many existing audits flag a subgroup when it exceeds each attribute alone, a condition that attributes acting independently already satisfy. We test this standard criterion on 150 synthetic datasets built with no interaction. With the shuffle null, it flags 66.1 false subgroups per dataset on average, and at least one in every dataset. When we plant interactions of known size into these datasets, a repaired null lets the criterion recover only 32% of them. We introduce ADDIS (Additive Intersectional Screen), which fits an additive model over all attributes, scores each subgroup by its Additive Residual Distance (ARD) from that model's prediction, and tests it against a Freedman-Lane null. On our synthetic benchmark, ADDIS flags 0.09 false subgroups per dataset and recovers 84% of the same planted interactions. We then build IndiPersona, 10,000 synthetic Indian personas with six identity attributes, and audit 13 open language models. The standard criterion flags 7.1% of the 738 tested subgroups on average; ADDIS flags 3.0%. Gender identity crossed with sexual orientation is the largest pairing in every model, and counterfactual personas confirm it and additionally reveal a caste x class interaction in all but one model that the observational test lacked the data to detect. Much of what audits report as intersectional bias can thus arise from independent single-attribute effects alone, yet a small core of interactions survives.
  </details>

- **[When Figures Disagree with Text: Evaluating Autonomous Visual Evidence Verification in Scientific Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24353)**  `arXiv:2611.24353`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guijia Zhang, Hongbo Qiu, Jiajun Zha, Guohao Cai, Hao Zheng, Huihao Jing, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figures carry evidence for the claims that cite them, so reviewing a paper means checking that a figure agrees with the text, and not only describing what the figure shows. Benchmarks of figure-text verification typically hand the model the figure, the claim, or the knowledge that an inconsistency exists, which leaves untested whether a model can find the figure worth checking in a complete paper, relate a visual element to the claim it conflicts with, and recognize the conflict. We study this autonomous setting on 31 full manuscripts, each carrying a reviewer-documented figure-text inconsistency from PRISMM-Bench that is hidden from the model. Six multimodal models review each PDF under a neutral prompt, and we then add one input at a time to the same manuscripts. Of 1,445 open-ended reviews, 60.6% mention the flawed figure, yet a grading rubric accepts only 3.3% as diagnosing its contradiction; a blind audit finds the rubric lenient, so we read these as rubric-accepted rates and compare conditions rather than absolute values. In a separate controlled run, naming the figure, without stating that it is flawed, raises rubric-accepted diagnosis from 4.3% to 20.4%, while a consistency instruction and coarse visual cues do not. When the contradiction is visible only in the rendered figure, text-only review yields no accepted diagnosis, while review of the rendered pages with the figure named reaches 15.8%. A baseline that lets the model choose which figures to check, ranked by centrality, stays at 4.8%. Deciding what to verify is thus a large part of the difficulty, and the selection rule we tested does not resolve it. Multimodal document understanding should be evaluated on whether a model can propose what to verify, not only answer questions about a figure it is handed.
  </details>

- **[MLE Agents: Autonomous ML Engineering in Large-scale Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23910)**  `arXiv:2611.23910`  `cs.AI`  
  _Matthew Li, Albert Jiongqian Liang, Dilip Raj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The typical workflow for improving large-scale ranking and recommendation models is slow and tedious: an engineer forms a hypothesis, implements it as a code change, trains a model, and evaluates it both offline and online, often relying on their own intuition and experience along the way. Autonomous LLM agents promise to speed this up by running the loop on their own, but it is still unclear whether they can do this in industry-scale systems rather than on benchmarks or small proxy tasks. We build human-on-the-loop machine learning engineer (MLE) agents that propose, implement, train, and evaluate model changes on large-scale search rankers. Engineers retain control over objective design, search direction, and final-model review. We identify three obstacles that make this industry setting challenging: feedback takes hours per experiment, offline metrics do not always predict online impact, and improvements saturate after the early wins. We address these with parallel training runs, an objective calibrated to match online impact, and careful context engineering. In one campaign, the system ran 1,097 completed candidate experiments and accepted 325, a 29.6\% acceptance rate compared with 15.6\% in the earlier campaign under a different objective and search constraint. Its best model increases search volume by 0.23\% in an A/B test of large scale. Several improvements were architectural changes the team had never tried, drawn from the broader industry and literature, suggesting that MLE agents can explore regions of the design space that human intuition overlooks. We find that the design of the harness around the model matters: intuition suggests that a stronger underlying model is enough, but in our experience, the design of the loop around it is just as critical. More broadly, our work shows that autonomous agents can do real machine-learning engineering in industry-scale systems.
  </details>

- **[Regularizing Treatment Leakage for LLM-based Uplift Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20794)**  `arXiv:2611.20794`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bojing Feng, Jinqiu Deng, Zihan Hong, Yuan Xue, Wenqi Xiang, Shuhan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) provide semantic features for uplift estimation, but treatment-revealing text can induce prediction shortcuts that do not transfer to deployment. We propose LLM-based Uplift Leakage Regularization (LULR) to address this risk. With the LLM encoder frozen, LULR penalizes dependence between the causal head's trainable shared representation and treatment while retaining separate outcome heads. The penalty uses a variational approximation to the contrastive log-ratio upper bound (CLUB) of mutual information, targeting treatment dependence rather than generic parameter shrinkage. Controlled proxy experiments provide exploratory evidence of improved ranking scores under injected treatment information relative to no regularization and the tested weight-decay setting. Structured-only benchmarks show modest, dataset-dependent effects. On an industrial pricing randomized controlled trial, mean offline lift@0.3 is 0.258 for LULR, compared with 0.106 for the same-backbone baseline. A separate one-week online A/B test of the complete deployed pricing policy records 5.45% higher sales volume and 1.91% higher gross merchandise value than the control arm. These short-term policy-level gains do not isolate the regularizer's effect.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming for Prediction and Classification of Target Variables in Structured Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19292)**  `arXiv:2611.19292`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming fixes every parameter of a feed-forward network by hand, with no gradient descent anywhere in the construction. Every demonstration so far starts from a target function handed to the model as a rule system, making it a compilation problem. A supervised dataset withholds that specification, so the procedure must do the statistical inference itself and then express the result in the parameterisation it was given. We ask whether a large language model can do both. It is shown a small dataset in full, with the architecture of a feed-forward network, and writes out every weight and bias as a literal number, with no optimizer, no gradient, no code execution, and no chance to evaluate it first. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score. The graded suite runs from two toy problems, through nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, to the classical benchmarks and one real engineering regression. The frozen network matches the closed-form optimum wherever that optimum is linear, reaches the irreducible error of the generating process on three rungs where it is not, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. It fails on two rungs, both times because the structure to be found is oblique to the coordinates it analysed. Three further constructors separate two failure modes a single score conflates: one returns no parameter vector at all on the hardest rungs, another delivers everywhere while writing networks worse than predicting the mean. One of those failures proves avoidable: another constructor solves the oblique-hinge rung by a margin that survives multiple-comparison correction. What predicts whether the hidden layer earns its keep is the training error of a linear separator, which the composite complexity index misses.
  </details>

- **[Task-Specific Agent Memory For Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29144)**  `arXiv:2609.29144`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yezhou Cheng, Runjia Du, Zeming Liu, Qibai Chen, Hang Lyu, Yankai Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents can learn continually by storing useful instructions in memory and reusing them as new tasks arrive, without changing model weights. This requires both learning from new feedback and retaining earlier gains. However, an instruction learned from one task family can hurt other families when the agent uses the same memory for every task. We introduce task-specific agent memory: proposed skills are checked with execution tests, stored by task family, and retrieved for tasks in that family. We evaluate this design on a 12-round code-repair benchmark with recurring task families, tracking performance throughout learning. Holding proposals, acceptance decisions, and task completions fixed, task-specific retrieval raises average performance from 0.713 to 0.816. Across 27 paired randomized-order streams with Qwen3-Coder-Next, it improves average performance by 0.063 over global memory and by 0.055 over an agent that never updates its memory, accumulating 63 skills with no observed harmful acceptance. Capacity-matched retrieval and cumulative one-shot controls further support the value of task-specific retrieval and sequential skill accumulation. Our contribution is to identify cross-family interference and show how task-specific agent memory supports continual learning through skill accumulation and retention.
  </details>

- **[FUSE: A Modular Framework for Evaluating Dangerous Capabilities of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02168)**  `arXiv:2609.02168`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengyi Jin, Ru Zhang, Xinbo Liu, Xiao Chen, Jiaxuan Lin, Jia Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dangerous capabilities of large language models (LLMs) cannot yet be compared across models: knowledge, refusal, and harm benchmarks each cover one step of a misuse path, on different models, protocols, and scales, so regulators cannot tell which model poses greater risk. We present FUSE, a modular framework that defines dangerous capability as a model's ability to advance a black-box adversary along a misuse path, and measures three stages of that path under one protocol: hazardous knowledge, estimated by item response theory; defense under multi-turn adversarial induction by a red-team agent; and the actionability of content produced once the model complies (harm). The stages form a fixed profile, not a scalar score, whose harm entry pairs how often a model complies with how severe its output is. New danger domains plug in as four-asset modules without changing the pipeline. On twelve commercial LLMs in the chemical-biological domain, the stages diverge: models with comparable knowledge differ markedly in defense, strong defenders do not produce less actionable content when they comply, and Claude, DeepSeek, and GPT occupy distinct regions of the profile. Across 41 months of releases, dangerous capability has not monotonically declined: knowledge rises within every family, defense diverges by family, and conditional severity does not fall. A tool-enhanced cybersecurity pilot on three models shows the same divergence between knowledge and defense. Three domain experts agree closely among themselves (ICC 0.91 for defense, 0.85 for harm); the judge preserves their ordering (Spearman 0.78 and 0.63) but scores 23.8 and 18.4 points higher on a 100-point scale, so we use it to rank models, not to measure absolute risk.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical Task Network Planning with LLM-Generated Heuristics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07707)**  `arXiv:2605.07707`  `cs.AI`  
  _Felipe Meneguzzi, Alexandre Buchweitz, Augusto B. Corrêa, Victor Scherer Putrich, André Grahl Pereira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  HTN planning is a variation of classical planning where, instead of searching for a linear sequence of actions, an algorithm decomposes higher-level tasks using a method library until only executable actions remain. On the one hand, this allows one to introduce domain knowledge that can speed up the search for a solution through the method library. On the other hand, it creates challenges that go beyond those of classical state-space search. While recent research produced a number of heuristics and novel algorithms that speed up HTN planning, these heuristics are not yet as informative as those available in classical planning algorithms. We investigate whether large language models (LLMs) can generate effective search heuristics for HTN planning, extending the methodology of Corrêa et al. (arXiv:2503.18809) from classical to hierarchical planning. Using the Pytrich planner on seven standard total-order HTN benchmark domains, we evaluate heuristics generated by nine LLMs under domain-specific prompting and compare them against two established domain-independent heuristics in the same planner and against the PANDA planner. A configuration fixed in advance solves more instances than either domain-independent heuristic, the best generated heuristics expand fewer nodes than both on most instances, and the virtual best over all models is on par with PANDA in coverage.
  </details>

- **[ScrapMem: A Bio-Inspired Framework for On-Device Personalized Agent Memory via Optical Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03804)**  `arXiv:2605.03804`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiale Chang, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term personalized memory for LLM agents is challenging on resource-limited edge devices because multimodal histories grow continually and are costly to store. To address this challenge, we propose ScrapMem, a bio-inspired framework that consolidates each day's heterogeneous records into a unified "Scrapbook Page." ScrapMem introduces Optical Forgetting, an optical compression mechanism that progressively reduces the resolution and JPEG quality of older pages, lowering storage costs while keeping recent evidence at higher fidelity. To preserve semantic consistency and support structured retrieval, we construct an Episodic Memory Graph (EM-Graph) that organizes semantic nodes into causal-temporal EM-Paths. This design separates a stable retrieval structure from aging visual evidence, allowing relevant episodes to remain accessible as page fidelity decreases. Under the common ATM-Bench protocol, ScrapMem without forgetting establishes a new non-oracle state of the art with 51.0% Joint@10 and reaches 70.3% Recall@10. Optical Forgetting reduces the persistent memory footprint by up to 93.0% relative to the raw corpus while retaining over 90% of No-Forget performance. ScrapMem offers a storage-efficient and interpretable approach to long-term multimodal memory for on-device LLM agents. The code is available on GitHub.
  </details>

- **[VeRA: Verified Reasoning Data Augmentation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13217)**  `arXiv:2602.13217`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zerui Cheng, Jiashuo Liu, Chunjie Wu, Jiayang Sun, Jianzhu Yao, Pramod Viswanath, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Static benchmarks wear out: their problems circulate widely and can enter training data, and their scores saturate as models improve. Renewing a benchmark requires new problems with reliable answers, which are expensive to write by hand at competition level. We introduce VeRA (Verified Reasoning Data Augmentation), in which a language model automatically compiles each benchmark problem into an executable specification: a question template, a generator of valid inputs, and a program that computes the answer. Once its programs pass automatic checks, a specification yields many fresh, code-labeled problems at almost no cost, and people are needed only to audit. VeRA-E (equivalent) draws new instances of the same computation. VeRA-H (hardened) modifies the computation to create harder problems where scores saturate; because code computes every answer, the language model that writes them never has to solve them. VeRA-H Pro keeps the hardest of up to five such candidates, as ranked by a judge. Across 16 frontier models, fresh instances of AIME 2024 problems lower accuracy from 84.46% to 70.25%, and every model drops. GSM8K and the less-circulated Beyond-AIME, compiled by the same pipeline, stay essentially unchanged, so the drop reflects familiarity with specific problems, not an artifact of rewriting. On the near-saturated AIME 2024 II problems, VeRA-H Pro lowers accuracy from 84.91% to 58.57%. Expert audits back the labels, with all 220 audited fresh instances accepted and 95.1% of hardened candidates usable after repair. Compiling all 1,319 GSM8K problems cost about USD 100. VeRA thus makes benchmarks renewable by construction: wherever code can compute the answer, a benchmark can be refreshed whenever its problems leak or models catch up.
  </details>

- **[PsyAgent: Constructing Human-like Agents Based on Psychological Modeling and Contextual Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06158)**  `arXiv:2601.06158`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zibin Meng, Kani Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human-like agents must express stable dispositions while adapting to roles, relationships, and norms. We present PsyAgent, a schema-first framework that operationalizes the trait-context interface by coupling a Big Five trait prior with explicit social-structural conditioning. PsyAgent comprises (i) Individual Structure (IS), a machine-usable trait-grounded profile, and (ii) Multi-Scenario Contexting (MSC), a curated library of role-relationship-norm frames spanning eight everyday arenas. At inference, fixed structured prompts couple the active MSC frame with the IS profile, encouraging behavior that is stable yet context-sensitive. To demonstrate learnability beyond prompt engineering, we use IS x MSC to synthesize supervision and fine-tune compact backbones with PEFT (SFT and optional DPO). Under a controlled psychometric-style evaluation protocol in percentile space, PsyAgent improves trait-faithfulness and multi-turn persona consistency, and is competitive with several larger general-purpose instruction-tuned baselines under matched decoding and scoring controls. We further triangulate the automatic protocol with external benchmarks and a small blinded human study. Overall, PsyAgent provides a precise and data-efficient approach to personality-grounded, norm-aware agents.
  </details>
